3. Time-budget batching as the author understands it: chunk size is not fixed, the per-step time is; an offline-profiled cost table lets the scheduler pick the batch that fits the budget. The author built a similar estimator for a PP deployment and argues the idea is the same for EP or PP.

Decode balancing is, as the author expected, a combination of KV-cache usage and running-request count, tuned case by case; the author's own note writes it as a weighted score a*kv_cache_usage + b*num_running and decomposes per-step decode cost into compute intensity, KV cache and MLA terms that all scale with num_running.

Autoscaling: prefill by aggregate compute tokens, decode by num_running (restates intake-2136#record claim 7).

11. Author's 7 Oct 2026 notes (image 2), prefill cache-aware policy order: (1) overload filter first (num waiting requests > 5); (2) dplb long/short pools (long: TP/PP; short: EP, ~2k, load-balanced); (3) cache-aware = scan the workers' cache-hit state and pick the one with the most hit; cold sessions (turn 1) are placed by power-of-two-choices / least-sessions and are roughly uniform; the short pool ignores cache hit and relies on pooling; least-compute-tokens is the control-overhead criterion; local is always faster; incast / hot-spot: Mooncake Store's allocation can put data on NUMA0 while the NIC is on NUMA1 (RoCE congestion signal, QP blow-up), so destination memory should be allocated in the NUMA group of the GPU/NIC.
   - *unaffected*: Transcribed from image 2; the NUMA incast remarks concern RoCE/NIC placement in a GPU cluster.

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 combined Stage-1+Stage-2 (operator-pasted input; read raw via FxTwitter JSON + 3 images, read_depth FULL). No Stage-1 digest existed to overturn. Second reader (independent agent, 2026-10-21): re-find 32/32, hash 32/32, support yes 9 / partial 4 / no 0; the four partials were anchor-coverage gaps (claims 3, 10, 11) closed by adding the missing anchors; no transcription error between PNGs and transcripts. Recorded: (a) the relayed summary's misattributions (PP-vs-EP is the author's, not the article's; 'decode owns metadata' garbles 'decode writes block KV'; 'xgrammar per a reply' unverifiable here, supported by intake-2116#record code); (b) claim 7 TTL inversion vs zhihu.md L134; (c) 'Together explored bucketing' provisional after 8 searches. Dive-surfaced sources: arXiv 2604.08075 (Token-Budget-Aware Pool Routing, canonical of withdrawn 2604.09613) and the vLLM-Ascend ShortRequestFirst scheduler doc selected for a Stage-2b wave; decline PROPOSED (operator confirms at Stage 3; reasons also in the plan): kvcache-ai/Mooncake repo (companion of intake-472#record; NUMA awareness lives in its Transfer Engine, no single-host consumer), novitalabs/pegaflow (GPU-cluster RDMA KV store; design reference only), deepseek-ai/3FS (declined 2026-10-20 in intake-2136#record; URL recorded for citation: https://github.com/deepseek-ai/3FS, design notes docs/design_notes.md), SGLang model gateway docs (gateway already indexed as the Shepherd Model Gateway entry), Dynamo routing-concepts page (intake-1785#record covers it).

### Verdict

Cite this entry ONLY for the author's own routing heuristics as design hypotheses (claims 5, 10-12) and for the PP-vs-EP prefill position (claim 4). Every DeepSeek figure or mechanism must be cited from intake-2136#record (article) or intake-2116#record (code), never from here. Never cite the TTL sentence (claim 7: direction inverted vs the article), the 300k average context (author's unnamed deployment), or 'Together explored bucketing' (unverified recollection). 'No cache-aware routing' is valid only with a shared KV store; on a per-instance-KV fleet it is a hypothesis WP-15 must price, not a design rule.
adopt_patterns: the routing heuristics transfer as offline replay arms; nothing here is a measured result, so no adopt_component or new_opportunity.

## intake-2215#record — ShortRequestFirst Prefill Scheduling (vLLM-Ascend feature guide; three-lane prompt-length-tiered waiting queue with aging)

- **URL**: https://docs.vllm.ai/projects/ascend/en/latest/user_guide/feature_guide/short_request_first.html
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty low, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, local_inference, kv_cache)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2214#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Waiting requests are split into immediate / short / long lanes; short means num_prompt_tokens <= threshold (default 256, unit = prompt tokens; threshold must be a non-negative int).
   - *unaffected*: doc and code agree; threshold validated >= 0 (ascend_config.py:1574-1575); unit = prompt tokens (num_prompt_tokens).
   - anchor `Fields (docs page)` @ docs.vllm.ai ascend/en/latest (developer preview; stable banner v0.23.0), fetched 2026-10-21, sha256 00dc362c: "Requests with num_prompt_tokens <= threshold are treated as short requests."
   - anchor `vllm_ascend/core/short_request_first_scheduler.py:L151-L153 @ 2164a24` @ vllm-ascend @ 2164a24d1e509fe211b60c4635fa266e292bd8b4: "if request.num_prompt_tokens <= self.threshold: return self._short_queue return self._long_queue"
1. Threshold guidance: sit at the valley between the short-request cluster and the long tail; with no traffic model start at P70-P85 of prompt length and refine from the real histogram; never a midpoint and never near the longest prompts.
   - *unaffected*: doc guidance only; no histogram or measurement backs the P70-P85 range in the source.
   - anchor `Threshold tuning (docs page)` @ docs.vllm.ai ascend/en/latest (developer preview; stable banner v0.23.0), fetched 2026-10-21, sha256 00dc362c: "If you do not yet have a refined traffic model, start with P70-P85 of prompt length as an engineering baseline and then refine it using the real request-length histogram."
2. Anti-starvation: long_max_wait_ms (float, default 0 = strict short-first) is a fairness gate; only the HEAD of the long lane is aged, from its enqueue time, and it is promoted only if a short request is actually waiting. The doc says to size it inside [W_normal, W_slo] from the long-request waiting tail measured under strict short-first, not from mean short TTFT.
   - *narrowed*: code ages ONLY the head of the long lane from its enqueue time (L155-162, L194) and promotes only if the short lane is non-empty (L176); a long request not at the head does not age independently; long_max_wait_ms <= 0 disables aging (L156).
   - anchor `Scheduling behavior (docs page)` @ docs.vllm.ai ascend/en/latest (developer preview; stable banner v0.23.0), fetched 2026-10-21, sha256 00dc362c: "if the oldest long request waits past long_max_wait_ms, it can jump ahead of waiting short requests"
   - anchor `vllm_ascend/core/short_request_first_scheduler.py:L176-L177 @ 2164a24` @ vllm-ascend @ 2164a24d1e509fe211b60c4635fa266e292bd8b4: "if wait_ms is not None and wait_ms >= self.long_max_wait_ms and self._short_queue: return self._long_queue"
3. Dispatch priority is immediate > aged-long > short > long; immediate = recovery requests (PREEMPTED, num_computed_tokens > 0 or num_output_tokens > 0).
   - *unaffected*: L171-182 implements exactly immediate > aged-long > short > long; immediate predicate L329-335.
   - anchor `Scheduling behavior (docs page)` @ docs.vllm.ai ascend/en/latest (developer preview; stable banner v0.23.0), fetched 2026-10-21, sha256 00dc362c: "immediate > aged-long > short > long"
   - anchor `vllm_ascend/core/short_request_first_scheduler.py:L332-L334 @ 2164a24` @ vllm-ascend @ 2164a24d1e509fe211b60c4635fa266e292bd8b4: "request.status == RequestStatus.PREEMPTED or getattr(request, "num_computed_tokens", 0) > 0 or getattr(request, "num_output_tokens", 0) > 0"
4. Scope is the waiting-queue policy only (a vLLM RequestQueue replacement); schedule() is not overridden, so chunked-prefill token budgeting and preemption stay upstream vLLM; the policy reorders admission and cannot preempt or shrink an in-flight long prefill.

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b wave 1 (dive-vllm-ascend-srf): combined ingest+dive; source read FULL; implementation pinned @ 2164a24; doc 'not supported with profiling-chunk' contradicted by code (claim 6 narrowed); claim 2 narrowed to head-of-lane aging; no measured numbers exist in the source; the policy reorders WAITING only and cannot preempt an in-flight long prefill. Second reader (independent agent, 2026-10-21): re-find 14/14, hash 14/14, line-range locators 7/7; support yes 6 / partial 2 / no 0 (claim 4 chunked-prefill consequence is an inference already flagged NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE; claim 7 absence statement reaches sources the reader was not given). Dive-surfaced: arXiv 2602.16603 (FlowPrefill) and arXiv 2606.09061 selected for wave 2; arXiv 2401.11181 (2024, disaggregated SJF/LJF prefill) and PR #12240 (batch-job scheduler, offline-batch concern) decline PROPOSED (operator confirms at Stage 3).

### Verdict

A shipped, default-off, waiting-queue-only shortest-first-with-aging policy whose value is its tuning doctrine (threshold at the prompt-length valley, P70-P85 start; aging bound from the long-wait tail measured under strict short-first) and its cheap observability (promotion-streak warning, periodic lane stats). It has no benchmark behind it and cannot touch an in-flight long prefill, so it addresses only the queue-wait share of short-request TTFT. On our stack that share exists only when all np slots are busy (llama-server deferred deque) or when the orchestrator lease holds longs back (where a bounded short-pass rule already exists); the prefill-share interference DI-126 is really about is KVU-16g's budget, not this. Do NOT cite any TTFT gain for it and do NOT copy the 256-token default.
adopt_patterns: the tuning doctrine and the trigger split transfer; the code does not (NPU install mechanics, 256 default wrong for our traffic, our head-of-line locus is slot occupancy plus the shared prefill batch).

- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SRF-A1: split DI-126's trigger into queue-wait vs prefill-share components; lane reordering only addresses the former.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SRF-A2: record queue-wait ms and the admission verdict per serving record (KVU-15d refinement) so the queue-wait component is measurable.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SRF-A3: knowledge-only tuning doctrine (threshold at the prompt-length valley, aging from the long-wait tail); re-check the 16384 lease threshold against the :8083 new-token histogram.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SRF-A4: lane-aware pop_deferred_task() on an experimental branch only if the queue-wait component of DI-126 fires (monitor).

## intake-2216#record — Dual-Pool Token-Budget Routing for Cost-Efficient and Reliable LLM Serving

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08075
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, cost_aware_routing, routing_intelligence, kv_cache, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2214#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Routing rule: each request goes to a short pool or a long pool by an estimated TOTAL token budget L_total = ceil(|r| / c_k) + max_output_tokens, where |r| is the request byte length and c_k a per-category (code / prose / CJK / mixed) bytes-per-token ratio; no tokenizer in the router, O(1) per dispatch, cold start c_0 = 4.0.
   - *unaffected*: Routing rule confirmed verbatim (Eq. 3, Algorithm 1); signal is byte length / calibrated ratio plus max_output_tokens, no prompt-token count at dispatch time.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "Each request is routed based on its estimated total token budget, computed using a per-category bytes-to-token ratio that is learned online via exponential moving average from usage.prompt_tokens feedback, eliminating the need for a tokenizer."
1. Route on L_total, not prompt length: routing on L_in alone caused preemption storms for short-prompt / long-generation requests (L_in = 200, L_out = 8192 example); adding max_output_tokens removed the issue (prose anecdote, no table).
   - *unaffected*: Stated as an early-prototype anecdote; no table quantifies the L_in-only preemption storm.
   - anchor `Section 2.1, paragraph Why total budget, not prompt length.` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "Early prototypes routed on $L_{\text{in}}$ alone. This caused preemption storms when “short-prompt, long-generation” requests (e.g., creative writing with $L_{\text{in}}{=}200$ , $L_{\text{out}}{=}8192$ ) were sent to the short pool. Routing on $L_{\text{total}}=L_{\text{in}}+L_{\text{out}}$ elim..."
2. Online calibration: c_k is an EMA (beta 0.95) of |r| / usage.prompt_tokens from each response; the routing estimate is biased conservative (c_k minus gamma sigma_k, gamma 1.0) because errors are asymmetric (long-to-short causes preemption, short-to-long only wastes throughput). Simulated Azure trace, Table 5: converges within 3.5% after 50 observations per category; mis-route rate drops from 4.1% (global static c = 4) to under 1%.
   - *unaffected*: Convergence numbers are from the simulated Azure trace with synthetic category tags; true c_k values are simulator inputs.
   - anchor `Section 2.1, paragraph Closed-loop calibration.` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "with decay $\beta{=}0.95$ . Because routing errors are asymmetric — sending a long request to the short pool causes preemption, while sending a short request to the long pool merely wastes some throughput — the router uses a conservative estimate:"
   - anchor `Section 4.5, Table 5 text` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "After 50 observations per category, the EMA ratio converges to within 3.5% of the true value. The conservative estimate (Equation 5) reduces mis-routing — sending a request to a pool that cannot serve it — from 4.1% (global static) to under 1% for all categories."
3. Threshold: B_short = 8192 is the default; any value in 4K-16K gives more than 80% of peak savings (Figure 6; curve values reconstructed from the SVG: Azure 1K 18.2 / 2K 29.5 / 4K 37.1 / 8K 41.9 / 16K 39.8 / 32K 28.3 percent; LMSYS 21.4 / 28.8 / 30.9 / 31.3 / 29.1 / 22.7). The threshold is set empirically, not derived; a feasibility check (L_total above the short pool C_max goes long) and a load-aware spillover wrap the threshold rule.
   - *narrowed*: Figure 6 carries no numeric labels in the text; the per-threshold values are reconstructed from SVG coordinates (8K points match Table 2); the threshold is chosen empirically, no derivation.
   - anchor `Section 2.2, paragraph Choosing B_short.` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "Empirically, a wide range of values between 4K and 16K tokens achieves near-optimal performance, making the system robust to imperfect tuning. In practice, initializing $B_{\text{short}}{=}8192$ provides a reliable default across diverse workloads."
   - anchor `Figure 6 caption` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "Figure 6: Savings vs. threshold. Any $B_{\text{short}}$ in 4K–16K delivers $>$ 80% of peak savings. The threshold is forgiving."
4. Pool definitions: SAME hardware (a homogeneous fleet partitioned); pools differ only in vLLM max_model_len (C_max 8K vs 65K), hence max concurrent sequences (128 vs 16 per GPU), max batch (16K vs 8K) and throughput mu (11.2 vs 2.8 req/s per instance, rho = 4.0). Mechanism: vLLM reserves C_max KV tokens per sequence.
   - *unaffected*: Pools differ only in software config (max_model_len / N_seq / batch); hardware identical by construction.
   - anchor `Section 4.1, Table 1 Pool configurations (rows)` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "Short $\mathcal{P}_{s}$ | 8K | 128 | 16K | 11.2 |"
   - anchor `Section 4.1, Table 1 Pool configurations (rows)` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "Long $\mathcal{P}_{l}$ | 65K | 16 | 8K | 2.8 |"
   - anchor `Section 4.1, Table 1 legend` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "Here, $C_{\max}$ denotes the maximum supported context length, $N_{\text{seq}}$ the maximum number of concurrent sequences per GPU, $B_{\text{batch}}$ the maximum batch size, and $\mu$ the measured throughput per instance."
5. Evaluation is SIMULATION ONLY: Llama-3-70B-Instruct BF16 on A100-80GB TP = 2 in a discrete-event simulator calibrated against Vidur; traces = 100K requests with Poisson arrivals derived from the Azure LLM Inference Dataset and LMSYS-Chat-1M; baseline = single pool with round-robin dispatch; SLOs P99 TTFT 2 s and P99 TPOT 80 ms near 90% utilization.
   - *unaffected*: Simulation only; calibrated against Vidur is asserted without calibration error numbers; Table 1 mu described as measured without method.
   - anchor `Section 4.1, paragraph Model and hardware.` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "We simulate serving Llama-3-70B-Instruct (BF16, 80 layers, 8 KV heads, $d_{h}{=}128$ ) on NVIDIA A100-80GB GPUs with tensor parallelism degree 2. Performance metrics, including throughput and latency, are obtained using a discrete-event simulator calibrated against Vidur [28], which models prefil..."
   - anchor `Section 4.1, paragraph Baselines.` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "We compare against two configurations: (1) Homogeneous, a single-pool deployment using round-robin dispatch; and (2) Token-budget routing, our proposed method as described in Section 2, with threshold $B_{\text{short}}{=}8192$ and load-aware spillover enabled."
6. Headline numbers at 1,000 req/s, B_short 8192 (simulator outputs): GPU instances 358 to 208 on Azure (41.9%) and 358 to 246 on LMSYS (31.3%); 2.86M USD/yr at 2.21 USD/GPU-hr; preemption 47.3 to 8.7 per mille (5.4x), OOM 2.1 to 0.4 per hour, success 99.69% to 99.95% (Azure, 90% util); TTFT P50 0.42 to 0.28 s, P99 1.82 to 1.71 s; LMSYS P99 TTFT slightly worse (1.45 to 1.48 s). The MI300X / Qwen3-235B-A22B case study is an arithmetic projection, not a simulation.
   - *narrowed*: All headline numbers are simulator outputs at 1,000 req/s; LMSYS P99 TTFT slightly worsens; MI300X numbers are arithmetic projection; the withdrawn duplicate reported 17-39%.
   - anchor `Section 4.2, Table 2 text` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "Token-budget routing reduces GPU instances by 41.9% on the Azure trace and 31.3% on LMSYS (Table 2). At $2.21/GPU-hr (AWS p4d.24xlarge), the Azure savings amount to $238K/month or $2.86M/year."
   - anchor `Section 4.3, paragraph Aggregate effect.` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "preemption drops 5.4 $\times$ (47.3 $\to$ 8.7‰), OOM drops 5.3 $\times$ , and the success rate rises from 99.69% to 99.95%."
   - anchor `Section 4.4, Table 4 text` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "TTFT at P50 improves by 33% (0.42 s $\to$ 0.28 s) and at P99 by 6% (1.82 s $\to$ 1.71 s). TPOT improves at both percentiles (11% at P50, 7% at P99)."
7. Closed-form estimator: savings = alpha (1 - 1/rho) with alpha = short-traffic fraction F(B_short) and rho = mu(C_short)/mu(C_long). The paper calls it a lower bound in Section 3 and an upper bound in Section 4.2 (predicted 60.0% vs simulated 41.9%, gap 18.1 pp); self-contradictory, and its own data support upper bound.
   - *unaffected*: The claim records the contradiction itself; the paper asserts both lower bound (Section 3) and upper bound (Section 4.2) and its own data (60.0 vs 41.9) make it an upper bound.
   - anchor `Section 3, Eq. 7` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "where $\alpha=F(B_{\text{short}})$ is the short-traffic fraction and $\rho=\mu(C_{S})/\mu(C_{H})$ is the throughput gain ratio."
   - anchor `Section 3, paragraph Why the formula is a conservative lower bound.` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "Consequently, Equation 7 is a lower bound: realized savings are at least as large, and often larger, than the formula’s prediction."
   - anchor `Section 4.2, paragraph Model validation.` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "The simulation yields 41.9% — a gap of 18.1 pp."
   - anchor `Section 4.2, paragraph Model validation.` @ arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML (paper.html sha256 63b9416ced8a; text dump paper.txt): "The formula consistently provides an upper bound on realizable savings, which is its intended use: teams can compute the ceiling cheaply, then simulate for precision."
8. The withdrawn duplicate arXiv 2604.09613 (v2 withdrawn as a duplicate of this paper) reported different headline numbers (17-39% GPU reduction) for the same traces, with no explanation of the revision.
   - *unaffected*: Withdrawal banner and the duplicate comment read from the 2604.09613 abs page; the 17-39% figure is from that abstract.
   - anchor `arXiv abs page of 2604.09613, Comments cell (raw HTML; renders as: duplicate of arXiv:2604.08075)` @ arXiv abs page 2604.09613 (abs-09613.html sha256 cc67abb9bf27), fetched 2026-10-21: "duplicate of <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08075" data-arxiv-id="2604.08075" class="link-https">arXiv:2604.08075</a>"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b wave 1 (research-intake/stage2b): combined ingest+dive; FULL read of arXiv:2604.08075v1 HTML; title corrected from the believed Token-Budget-Aware Pool Routing (that is the withdrawn duplicate 2604.09613) to Dual-Pool Token-Budget Routing for Cost-Efficient and Reliable LLM Serving; the lower/upper-bound contradiction and the withdrawn duplicate divergent headline numbers recorded; Figure 6 values reconstructed from SVG; no repository URL exists in the paper. Second reader (independent agent, 2026-10-21): re-find 19/19 (whitespace-normalised), hash 19/19; support yes 8 / partial 1 / no 0 (claim 3 Figure-6 curve not printed in the text, already narrowed); three reported_results spot-checks match; a paper-internal arithmetic looseness in the Section 4.6 left-slope sentence noted. Dive-surfaced: Medha arXiv 2409.17264, GreenLLM arXiv 2508.16449 and EWSJF arXiv 2601.21758 (independent; bear on whether tiering or in-instance scheduling answers the HOL symptom) selected for wave 2; decline PROPOSED (operator confirms at Stage 3): FleetOpt 2603.16514, the 1/W Law 2603.17280 and the vision paper 2603.21354 (same author core, restatements), ServeGen 2505.09999 and BurstGPT 2401.17644 (production length distributions; our own HSF-4 log distribution is the relevant alpha), vllm-project/semantic-router (no evidence in the paper that the mechanism ships there).

### Verdict

A simulation-only, same-group-corroborated argument that length-tiering a vLLM fleet by max_model_len pays when a large short fraction alpha exists and the engine reserves max-context KV per sequence so that concurrency scales about 1/C_max. Cite only the transferable patterns: route on prompt plus max_output budget rather than prompt length; calibrate a chars-to-tokens ratio from the server real usage.prompt_tokens; compute alpha (1 - 1/rho) from your own length distribution as a CEILING before building anything; watch preemption and TTFT-under-long-prefill, not utilization. Do NOT quote the 31-42% / 2.86M headline as expected gain (Vidur-calibrated simulator, internal inconsistencies, withdrawn duplicate with other numbers). Nothing in the paper covers KV locality, sticky sessions or prefix-cache-aware routing, which dominate a single-host per-instance-KV deployment.
adopt_patterns: route-on-total-budget, offline chars-per-token calibration and the alpha (1 - 1/rho) ceiling transfer as zero-compute replay inputs; the headline savings do not transfer and are simulator-only.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `within-role-placement-state-machine.md`
- updated `heterogeneous-slot-fabric-residency.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): TB-A1 refine WP-15: score the replay rule on L_total = estimated prompt tokens + n_tokens_budget (both already in dispatch.v1), not prompt length alone
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): TB-A2 refine WP-15: fit chars-per-token per client.class offline from the request_id join to serving_call.v1 real token counts; report dispersion and the mis-route rate a conservative estimate would give
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): TB-A4 refine HSF-4: emit alpha(B) for B in {2K, 4K, 8K, 16K} from the append-length distribution and compute alpha (1 - 1/rho) with rho = measured full-vs-half shape speed ratio, as the ceiling of any length-tiering gain at single-host scale
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): TB-A5 refine WP-15: add a candidate rule short to half instances / long to full instance and count sessions that cross the threshold mid-session (stickiness migration cost, which the paper never models)

## intake-2217#record — EWSJF: An Adaptive Scheduler with Hybrid Partitioning for Mixed-Workload LLM Inference

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21758
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty low, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2216#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Job size is the input prompt length b only: the scheduler uses input-side statistics (no output-length prediction), and the per-request compute cost C_prefill(b) is an estimated prefill cost for input length b whose functional form is NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE (§2.3, §4.1).
   - *narrowed*: Length-only is stated; the estimator C_prefill(b) is named but never defined (no formula, no fit), so "size estimator" reduces to prompt length.
   - anchor `§2.3 Predictive Cost Modeling` @ arXiv:2601.21758v1 HTML: "EWSJF avoids these issues by relying solely on input-side statistics and adapting online via Bayesian meta-optimization, eliminating the need for workload-specific retraining while remaining robust to distributional drift."
1. Starvation control is a single score on each queue's head-of-line request, Score = qf * (w_base + w_urg * W_t/C_prefill(b) + w_fair * log(b+1)) with qf = q_i/(b+1): urgency is wait time normalised by estimated prefill cost, the log(b+1) term boosts long jobs, and the weights are learned; no numeric weights, no aging threshold and no weight schedule are given anywhere (§4.1, §4.4.1, Thm A.1).
   - *narrowed*: Formula and terms verified; no numeric weights or schedule exist; theorem premise (w_fair) and proof (w_urg term) disagree.
   - anchor `§6.3 Baselines, 'Why EWSJF Wins'` @ arXiv:2601.21758v1 HTML: "By incorporating a Density-Weighted Scoring function (Section 4.4), EWSJF prevents long requests from “dying” in the queue and maintains fairness comparable to FCFS."
2. Scheduling locus is the waiting queue / batch construction above the engine (vLLM admission), not in-batch or iteration-level: the tactical loop picks the arg-max-score queue, greedily fills a batch from it, then backfills from adjacent length queues; O(k) per step (§3.2, Alg. 1, §5).
   - *unaffected*: Locus verified as waiting-queue admission / batch construction; the paper explicitly leaves iteration-level scheduling to the engine.
   - anchor `§3.2 Tactical Loop, Batch Builder bullet` @ arXiv:2601.21758v1 HTML: "Batch Builder: Selects the highest-scoring queue and constructs a batch by greedily pulling requests. If capacity remains, it backfills the batch with requests from adjacent queues to maximize GPU utilization."
3. Queues are contiguous prompt-length intervals found by Refine-and-Prune (k-means k=3, recursive split at gaps > alpha * mean gap, utility-based merge down to max_queues), re-run roughly every 10 minutes; 32 queues was the throughput optimum on their trace; gap-falling requests get a temporary bubble queue with a 10% tolerance (L <= max_len*1.10 or L >= min_len*0.90) (§4.2, §4.3, §6.4, Alg. 2).
   - *unaffected*: Mechanism and the 32 / 10-minute / 1.10-0.90 constants are present; alpha is NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE and max_queues appears only as an example value (32) in the appendix complexity text.
   - anchor `§6.4 Queue Clustering Analysis` @ arXiv:2601.21758v1 HTML: "Table 3 shows that while standard K-Means suggests 5–10 queues, the Refine-and-Prune algorithm identifies that 32 queues maximize throughput."
4. Evaluation: 4x NVIDIA A100-80GB, LLaMA-2-13B-Chat with tensor parallelism, vLLM v0.11.0, a bimodal synthetic mix (32-4096 input tokens, 80% short / 20% long, Poisson arrivals); the only baseline with numbers is vLLM FCFS; Greedy SJF is named as a baseline but has no numeric rows; Sarathi and Orca are discussed, not compared (§6.1-6.3).
   - *narrowed*: Setup verified; the SJF baseline has no numbers and Sarathi/Orca are never run, so "vs FCFS/SJF/Sarathi" collapses to "vs FCFS".
   - anchor `§6.2 Experimental Setup, Software` @ arXiv:2601.21758v1 HTML: "Software. EWSJF was implemented as a pluggable scheduler within vLLM v0.11.0. All benchmarks used the LLaMA-2-13B-Chat model with tensor parallelism enabled."
5. Headline throughput: token throughput over FCFS ranges from +5.2% (10 req/s, 200k requests) to +54.1% (100 req/s, 10k requests), +36.1% at 500 req/s on 200k requests; requests/s is sometimes below FCFS at 10 req/s (7.88 vs 8.13; 5.15 vs 5.38); the best short-prompt run is 163.92 vs 90.40 tok/s (Tables 4-10).
   - *narrowed*: Numbers transcribed from Tables 4-10; tok/s gains are partly more tokens generated (Table 8/9 token columns), and req/s is below FCFS at the lowest rate in two of four scales.
   - anchor `§6.5 Performance Under Load, Summary` @ arXiv:2601.21758v1 HTML: "Gains range from modest improvements at low load (e.g., +5.2% at 10 req/s, 200k requests) to substantial boosts under high contention (e.g., +54.1% at 100 req/s, 10k requests)."
6. The "up to 4x" reduction in average TTFT for short requests appears only in the abstract and conclusion; no TTFT, TPOT or p99 table exists, and tail latency is reported qualitatively as "High" vs "Lower" (Table 10). Per-length-class latency numbers: NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE.
   - *narrowed*: The 4x TTFT figure has no table, no conditions and no percentile; treat as an abstract-only assertion.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2601.21758v1 HTML: "Implemented in vLLM, EWSJF improves end-to-end throughput by over 30% and reduces average Time-To-First-Token for short requests by up to 4"
7. Adaptation horizon: the Bayesian meta-optimiser runs trials of 10-15 minutes and converges in 5-8 trials (about 50-120 min); the authors list as limitations length-only features, multi-minute adaptation, no formal fairness guarantee, and single-node scope (§4.4, App. B, Limitations).
   - *unaffected*: Trial length and convergence count are in §4.4 and App. B; limitations verbatim.
   - anchor `Limitations (after §7)` @ arXiv:2601.21758v1 HTML: "Second, the Bayesian meta-optimizer adapts over multi-minute intervals, which may be insufficient for extremely bursty or adversarial traffic patterns."
8. Weights are context-aware per queue, e.g. w_urg(mean_b_q) = a_u * mean_b_q + b_u so short queues weight urgency and long queues weight fairness; the fitted coefficients are NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE (§4.1, §4.4.1).
   - *narrowed*: Linear meta-policy form verified; coefficients and the fairness-weight analogue are NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE.
   - anchor `§4.1 Learnable and Context-Aware Scoring` @ arXiv:2601.21758v1 HTML: "allowing the scheduler to emphasize urgency in short queues and fairness in long queues."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b wave 2: Stage-1 framing of this paper as a job-size-prioritised (weighted SJF) in-instance scheduler with per-class latency results was narrowed: job size is prompt length only, the cost estimator is undefined, the weights are learned but never reported, the SJF and Sarathi baselines carry no numbers, and the headline 4x TTFT claim has no supporting table; the verified contribution is the score shape and the learned length partition, both prose-level for our stack. Second reader (independent agent, 2026-10-21): re-find 9/9 (NBSP preserved), hash 9/9; support yes 8 / partial 1 / no 0 (claim 1 anchor names only the scoring function; the formula is verbatim in Sections 4.1 and 4.4.1); six reported_results groups match; the max_queues example value noted in claim 3.

### Verdict

Cite this entry only for the shape of a waiting-queue ordering score (wait time / estimated prefill cost, plus a log-length boost) and for the observation that a learned lane count with adjacent-lane backfill beat FCFS on a vLLM A100 synthetic mix. Do not cite it for any latency number (none is tabulated), for weight values (none are given), or as a comparison against SJF or Sarathi (never run). For our stack it refines DI-126 / SRF-A1 prose only; the learned optimiser is non-applicable at our request rates and adaptation horizon.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): EW-A1: refine DI-126 (kv-unified-stack-rollout.md:1363) prose: if the queue-wait component fires, the candidate in-instance ordering rule is a static cost-normalised aging score (wait_ms / estimated_prefill_ms, plus a log-length boost) over the llama-server deferred deque, not a learned optimiser; cite this entry for the formula shape only, with weights to be fitted from our own serving_calls/dispatch.v1 records.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): EW-A2: refine SRF-A1 (intake-2215#record) prose: EWSJF is the same waiting-queue lever with the lane count learned (32 on their trace) instead of fixed at three and with adjacent-lane backfill; it still supplies no numeric weight schedule, so SRF-A1 stays unnumbered.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): EW-A3: no task: Refine-and-Prune and the Bayesian meta-optimiser are non-applicable at our request rates (frontdoor serves a handful of agents, not 10-500 req/s) and their 50-120 min adaptation horizon is longer than our bursts.

- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): EW-A5: knowledge only: record the credibility defects (no latency table behind the 4x claim, no SJF numbers, token-count mismatch between schedulers, reference-list anomalies) so the entry is not cited for numbers.

## intake-2218#record — No Request Left Behind: Tackling Heterogeneity in Long-Context LLM Inference with Medha

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.17264
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, kv_cache)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2216#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Problem statement (convoy / HOL): with quadratic attention a 100K-token request costs about 10,000x a 1K one, so in a non-preemptive system (LoongServe, FCFS) every short request arriving behind a long prefill waits for that whole prefill, which can take minutes; the paper measures this on Llama-3 8B / 16 A100 at 0.75 QPS as 8x higher short-request TTFT with 1% long requests and 30x P50 / 174x P90 with 5% long requests (128K-1M tokens).
   - *narrowed*: Measured against LoongServe (CP, no chunking) and Sarathi with STATIC chunks on 16 A100; the 30x/174x are the no-preemption baseline at 128K-1M-token longs. A server already running chunked prefill (llama-server -ub 2048) is not the baseline this number describes.
   - anchor `§1 Introduction, Motivation paragraph` @ arXiv:2409.17264v5 HTML: "These spikes occur because arriving short requests must wait for the entire prefill computation of the long request to complete — which can take several minutes."
1. Chunked-prefill read amplification is a non-issue for GQA models on modern kernels: the arithmetic intensity of a chunked attention step is c * h_q / h_kv (depends on chunk size, not on context length), so on H100 with Llama-3 70B chunks of about 40 tokens saturate compute and 32-token chunks cost only 11% more than 2048-token chunks at 1M context (Figure 3b). The paper attributes the opposite earlier finding (Sarathi 2023) to older kernels that did not parallelise over both query and KV dimensions.
   - *narrowed*: H100 + FlashInfer specific; our PB-QCOST-1 fit has a 324 ms per-iteration intercept on MI210, so the small-chunk regime is not free here.
   - anchor `§3.1 Debunking KV-Cache Read Amplification, Arithmetic Intensity Analysis` @ arXiv:2409.17264v5 HTML: "The critical insight is that arithmetic intensity for chunked attention depends solely on chunk size, not total context length."
2. Adaptive chunking: a long prefill is MLP-bound early (needs large chunks for efficiency) and attention-bound late (small chunks suffice), so Medha starts with large chunks and shrinks them as the KV cache grows, keeping iteration time roughly constant; this is implemented implicitly by a batch packer that fills a fixed per-iteration time budget t_target (derived from the decode TPOT SLO, e.g. 20 ms) by binary-searching the largest chunk that fits, using a runtime model (Vidur, under 5% error).
   - *unaffected*: Mechanism is a time budget with a cost model; this is what KVU-16g + PB-QCOST-1 already implement (quadratic q*c term), minus the LARS ordering.
   - anchor `§3.2 Managing Interference with Adaptive Chunking, Resolving the Tradeoff; §4.3 Iterative Batch Packing` @ arXiv:2409.17264v5 HTML: "The policy begins a prefill with large chunks and dynamically shrinks them as the bottleneck shifts, thereby maintaining a predictably low iteration time."
3. LARS (Length-Aware Relative Slack) ordering: priority = slack / total work (rho); FCFS causes convoys, EDF starves long requests until they pass their deadline and then convoys everyone (measured: EDF degrades to FCFS-like at 1.75 QPS), while LARS lets short requests preempt only when their relative slack falls below the long request's. Knobs: t_target (20 ms example), rho_max maximum yield fraction (0.4 example), at most one long prefill per batch, per-request deadlines d_i as inputs; the scheduler is claimed to run with sub-millisecond overhead.
   - *narrowed*: Requires per-request deadlines and total-work estimates; the EDF collapse is shown only at 1.75 QPS in one figure.
   - anchor `§4.1 An SLO-Aware Prioritization Policy, LARS paragraph` @ arXiv:2409.17264v5 HTML: "Short requests still receive priority when urgent (low relative slack), but long requests aren’t perpetually starved as in EDF. The result is a natural balance that avoids both convoy effects and starvation."
4. Multi-prefill batching: a late-stage small chunk of a long prefill saturates attention but leaves the MLP memory-bound, so an early-stage chunk of a short prefill can be co-packed almost free; long prefills yield (1 - rho) of the budget. Isolated effect: up to 1.8x lower median TTFT vs plain LARS at 1.75 QPS (Figure 13, Llama-3 8B, TP8-SPP2).
   - *narrowed*: The 1.8x is multi-prefill batching over plain LARS at high load; isolated gain, authors' own ablation.
   - anchor `§4.2 Multi-Prefill Batching Policy` @ arXiv:2409.17264v5 HTML: "To leverage this slack, the scheduler co-locates two prefill chunks in the same batch with complementary profiles – packing a short, early-stage (MLP-dominant) chunk and one long, late-stage (Attention-dominant) chunk."
5. Static length pools are evaluated as a baseline (6.5): LoongServe with 2 of 8 context-parallel instances reserved for requests under 8192 tokens loses up to 20% of long-request completions vs Medha (10% vs plain LoongServe) through fragmentation, and the paper concludes pools do not remove the convoy effect; the text reports completion rate and decode latency for this arm, not the short-request TTFT distribution.
   - *narrowed*: One configuration (2 of 8 CP instances reserved, threshold 8192, 5% long); the text gives long-completion and decode numbers, no short-TTFT figure for the pool arm, so "pools do not solve the convoy" is asserted for short requests, measured only as a throughput cost on long ones.
   - anchor `§6.5 Alternate Scheduling Approaches: Multiple Request Pools` @ arXiv:2409.17264v5 HTML: "This reservation increases contention for long prefills, leading to up to 20% lower completions for long requests compared to Medha, and 10% lower than default LoongServe."
6. Multi-node mechanisms: Stream Pipeline Parallelism pipelines successive chunks of ONE request across pipeline stages (1.64x lower 1M-token prefill latency than context parallelism on Llama-3 8B/H100, 80%+ scaling to 128 GPUs) and KV-Cache Parallelism shards KV reads of one sequence across GPUs with online-softmax merge (about 40% lower TPOT for 10M-token decode at p_kvp=2 on Llama-3 8B, 2x lower TPOT at 1.75 QPS for 70B 3D vs 2D). Both exist because a single device cannot hold or finish a million-token prefill; neither applies within one GPU.
   - *narrowed*: SPP/KVP are multi-device; nothing to pipeline or shard within one MI210.
   - anchor `§3.3 Scalable Parallelism for Preemptive Inference, opening sentence` @ arXiv:2409.17264v5 HTML: "To achieve interactive latency for million-token requests, preemptive chunking must be combined with a scalable, multi-node parallelism strategy."
7. Headline: 5.7x throughput and 30x P50 / 174x P99 lower latency than LoongServe/Sarathi on a 5%-long mix (Llama-3 8B, 16 A100, TP8-SPP2 vs LoongServe TP2-CP4 / Sarathi TP8-PP2); Sarathi with static 512/2048 chunks reaches TPOT of 1 s on late chunks of long sequences, which is the interference adaptive chunking removes.
   - *narrowed*: Headline numbers are authors' own on Azure clusters, no venue, no independent replication found.
   - anchor `§4.3 Dynamic Batch Packing, Iterative Batch Packing paragraph (budget = the mechanism behind the headline)` @ arXiv:2409.17264v5 HTML: "Critically, this fixed-budget approach naturally implements adaptive chunking (Section 3.2): early-phase prefills with empty KV caches fit large chunks within the budget, while late-phase prefills with populated KV caches are constrained to smaller chunks."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b wave 2: dived v5 (2025-11-26) in full from the arXiv HTML; the v4 title differs (search-result data) and pre-v5 citations emphasise million-token scaling rather than heterogeneity. Three WebSearches found no venue, no replication and no independent critique; FlowPrefill 2602.16603 and LAPS 2601.11589 surfaced as later same-problem work. No intake id assigned; entry not written to research/intake_index.yaml. Second reader (independent agent, 2026-10-21): hash 8/8; 7/8 byte-exact, claim-7 anchor re-found only modulo an NBSP the source carries (corrected to the source string and re-hashed); support yes 8 / partial 0 / no 0; eight numbers match; two paper-internal inconsistencies (P90 vs P99; KVP degree) recorded in contradicting_evidence.

### Verdict

Medha is the strongest written argument that the HOL symptom for mixed short/long traffic is solved INSIDE an instance by time-budgeted chunked prefill, and its one pool experiment says static length pools pay for short-TTFT relief with long-request throughput. On our stack the in-instance lever already exists as KVU-16g (decode-aware budget) plus the PB-QCOST-1 quadratic fit; what Medha adds is the observation that the budget must bind whenever ANOTHER prefill is waiting, not only while something decodes, and that pool rules must be scored on long-request throughput too. Do not transfer the 32-token-chunk efficiency, the 5.7x/30x/174x headlines (non-chunked baselines, 16-128 GPUs, million-token requests), LARS (needs deadlines), or SPP/KVP (multi-device). The deciding single-server measurement is a two-request collision on :8083: short TTFT under an 80k prefill with nothing decoding, budget off vs on.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `gpu-serving-tie-in-program.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- updated `within-role-placement-state-machine.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): MD-A1 refine KVU-16g-2 / gpu_slot.sh: add a short-prefill-under-long-prefill arm (80k neighbour prefilling, nothing decoding, 1k request arrives 2 s in) and verify whether --prefill-budget-target-ms bounds the iteration when NO slot decodes; the KVU-16g text caps prompt tokens only while any slot generates, which is exactly the Medha case it would miss.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): MD-A2 refine WP-15 / TB-A5 scoring: report the long-request completion or throughput delta beside the short-TTFT tail for any pool rule, because the only pool evidence in the literature (Medha 6.5) shows the cost lands on long requests.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): MD-A3 refine DI-126 trigger text: a colliding short request's TTFT rising by more than one prefill iteration (1.3-1.8 s per the PB-QCOST-1 fit) is the budget failing, not queue wait; join iteration ms at collision time.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): MD-A4 knowledge-only for PB-QCOST-1: Medha's 32-token-chunk-is-free result does not transfer; our fitted intercept is 324 ms per iteration, so shrinking chunks here has a floor that H100/FlashInfer does not; keep the quadratic budget, do not copy small chunks.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): MD-A5 decline LARS, SPP, KVP: per-request deadlines do not exist on our API, and SPP/KVP need a second device (monitor with DI-016's second-MI210 trigger).

## intake-2219#record — Fairness-Aware and Latency-Controllable Scheduling for Chunked-Prefill LLM Serving

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09061
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty low, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2215#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. PROBLEM (qualitative only): in chunked-prefill engines decode is admitted first and prefill candidates share the residual per-iteration token budget, so the prefill ORDER decides service share. FCFS lets a long prompt at the head consume the residual budget for many rounds (chunk-level head-of-line blocking of short prompts); shortest-prefill-first can delay long requests indefinitely under sustained short arrivals. No fairness or starvation METRIC is defined anywhere (no Jain index, max-wait, per-class TTFT or starvation count): NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE.
   - *narrowed*: The starvation/fairness problem is stated qualitatively; the paper measures no fairness metric, so fairness here means aggregate mean/P95 latency, not a starvation bound.
   - anchor `§3.1.1 Motivation and Design Goals, para 2` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "On the other hand, a shortest-prefill-first policy improves the responsiveness of short requests, but it can indefinitely delay long requests when short requests keep arriving. These two policies represent opposite extremes: FCFS preserves arrival order but can amplify chunk-level head-of-line bl..."
1. POLICY: Aging priority P_i(n) = alpha*(t - a_i) + beta*r_i(n) with alpha > 0 (waiting-time weight), beta < 0 (remaining prefill tokens r_i(n), decremented by each scheduled chunk c_i). The only knob is the ratio alpha/|beta|: small = close to shortest-prefill-first, large = close to FCFS. No aging bound and no SLO term appear in the aging policy; the paper explicitly disclaims a fixed request class and a hard starvation threshold; priority is continuous. Maintained as a max-heap on the key K_i(n) = -alpha*a_i + beta*r_i(n) (the shared alpha*t drops out), O(k log n) per round for k selected requests.
   - *unaffected*: Formulation read from MathML alttext; the text has no aging bound or SLO term, as stated.
   - anchor `§3.1.2 Priority Formulation, Eq. (1) MathML alttext` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "P_{i}(n)=\alpha\cdot(t-a_{i})+\beta\cdot r_{i}(n),"
   - anchor `§3.1.2 Priority Formulation, para 2` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "Therefore, the scheduler does not rely on a fixed request class or a hard starvation threshold; instead, priority evolves continuously with both waiting time and prefill progress."
2. BUDGET INTERACTION: aging runs only after decode slots are reserved; it ranks prefill candidates and hands out the residual budget in that order, chunk = min(remaining tokens, residual budget); unfinished requests are re-queued with the updated key. It changes which prefill gets the next budget, never the chunking, KV management or backend. Benefit therefore needs fine granularity: it is reported for 256/512-token chunks and vanishes at 1024.
   - *unaffected*: Budget interaction is as the text states; the granularity dependence is the paper's own finding (Table 4, Fig. 5).
   - anchor `§3.1.3 Scheduling semantics` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "Aging is applied only after decode requests have been admitted. In each scheduling round, the scheduler first reserves capacity for ongoing decode requests to preserve generation continuity. It then ranks prefill candidates according to the aging priority and allocates the remaining prefill budge..."
   - anchor `§4.3.1, conclusion 2 after Table 4` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "The benefit becomes smaller when the chunk size increases to 1,024 tokens. In this case, Aging and FCFS show nearly identical P95 latency, while Aging slightly increases mean E2E latency."
3. HEADLINE (Sarathi-Serve, 1x RTX 4090, Qwen3-8B, max context 512, max gen 512, ShareGPT 200 requests at a fixed 0.1 s inter-arrival, median prompt 19.0 tokens / P90 179.4, baseline = Sarathi default FCFS only): chunk 256 mean E2E 118.72 -> 106.56 s (-10.24%), mean TTFT 107.82 -> 95.67 s (-11.27%), P95 E2E 195.73 -> 183.58 s, P95 TTFT 184.86 -> 172.73 s; chunk 512 mean E2E -6.75%, mean TTFT -7.11%; chunk 1024 mean E2E 109.38 -> 110.59 s (Aging slightly WORSE), P95 equal. Decomposition: model execution time unchanged (10.85 vs 10.86 s), all gain is queue wait (107.77 -> 95.63 s). Mean TTFT near 100 s means the trace is a saturated backlog drain, not an SLO regime. No SJF/SPF arm, no throughput figure, no per-class (short vs long) TTFT, no P99 for the aging experiment: NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE.
   - *narrowed*: Headline over 10% holds only at chunk 256 on the 4090/512-context backlog regime; chunk 1024 is a small loss; no short-vs-long split, no P99, no throughput, no SJF arm exist for the aging experiment.
   - anchor `§4.3.1 Fairness under Mixed Workloads, text after Table 4` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "With a 256-token chunk, our aging policy reduces mean request end-to-end latency from 118.72s to 106.56s, achieving 10.24% improvement over FCFS. Mean TTFT decreases from 107.82s to 95.67s, improving by 11.27%. Tail latency also improves: E2E P95 decreases from 195.73s to 183.58s, and TTFT P95 de..."
   - anchor `§4.3.1, conclusion 3 after Table 4` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "Aging does not reduce model execution time: the average model execution time is 10.85s under Aging and 10.86s under FCFS. Instead, the improvement comes almost entirely from queuing reduction. The average scheduling waiting time decreases from 107.77s to 95.63s."
4. SENSITIVITY (the only aging-vs-anything trade-off measurement): at chunk 512, waiting weight base 100 gives mean E2E 106.99 s / TTFT P95 173.20 s; base 500 gives 107.73 s / 174.78 s, i.e. stronger aging is slightly worse because it drifts toward pure time ordering. The semantics of base and the alpha, beta values used in the headline runs are NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE. Two-GPU (2x RTX 5090): chunk 256 / 10 seqs Aging LOSES (mean E2E 103.84 vs 99.05 s); chunk 512 / 32 seqs modest gain 61.77 -> 60.55 s. Ascend 910B port (vLLM-Ascend, OpenPangu model): standalone numbers only (200 req in 14.99 s, 13.35 req/s, P99 12.70 s), no FCFS comparison.
   - *narrowed*: Weight base semantics and the alpha/beta values are undefined in the text; the sensitivity is two points at one chunk size and the two-GPU 256/10 loss is the paper's own.
   - anchor `§4.3.2 Parameter Sensitivity, para 2` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "When the weight base is 100, mean E2E latency is 106.99s and TTFT P95 is 173.20s. Increasing the weight base to 500 raises mean E2E latency to 107.73s and TTFT P95 to 174.78s."
   - anchor `§4.3.3 Multi-GPU Extension, para 2` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "With the chunk size of 256 and the sequence capacity of 10, Aging performs worse than FCFS because the limited concurrency prevents its waiting-time compensation from translating into useful scheduling progress."
5. LPRS (latency-prediction-based chunk sizing): replaces the static token budget by a target per-iteration latency T* (105 ms in the evaluation) with a hard token cap kept; a 3-layer MLP (128,64,32) over 11 raw + 5 derived features (prefill/decode tokens, context sums, GPU/CUDA-pool memory MB) trained with a bucket-weighted ASYMMETRIC Huber loss that penalises under-estimation; discrete candidate search at granularity Delta, asymmetric score with lambda_o > lambda_u (values NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE). Predictor: MAE 1.13 ms, MAPE 1.26%, 99% within 5 ms on 36,868 samples (RTX PRO 5000 72 GB, Qwen3-8B). End-to-end vs token budget 1024: at 0.1 s arrivals P99 prefill 924.22 -> 889.45 ms (-3.8%), P99 request 986.93 -> 952.56 ms (-3.5%); at 1.0 s arrivals LPRS is slightly WORSE at every quantile (P50 request 138.52 vs 129.29 ms), which the paper calls similar.
   - *narrowed*: The text calls the 1.0 s regime similar; Table 9 shows LPRS worse at every quantile there, so the LPRS gain is conditional on 0.1 s arrivals.
   - anchor `§4.4.2 End-to-End Performance Evaluation, item 2 (quote carries U+00A0 before s and ms as in the HTML)` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "Specifically, under high concurrency (0.1 s interval), LPRS reduces the P99 prefill latency from 924.22 ms to 889.45 ms, and the P99 request latency from 986.93 ms to 952.56 ms. Under regular load (1.0 s interval), however, both methods deliver similar latency profiles."
6. APC (active prefill control): caps the number of concurrently active unfinished prefills, C_t = min(C_max, S_max - |decode set|, floor((B_max - U_t)/L_min)), and enforces a minimum effective chunk m_i = min(r_i, L_min), with a warm-start chunk when no prefill is active. Ablation (49:1 short 30-50 tok : long 200-220 tok): avg scheduled prefill seqs 5.32 -> 0.46, avg prefill chunk 0.78 -> 6.29 tokens, mean request E2E 143.65 -> 111.68 ms (-22.26%), P90 -24.62%. The APC-off baseline averages under one token per prefill per round, i.e. a self-inflicted degenerate configuration; the gain is against that, not against a tuned static budget.
   - *narrowed*: APC gains are against an APC-off baseline averaging 0.78 tokens per prefill per round, a degenerate configuration; not a gain over a tuned static budget.
   - anchor `§4.5 Evaluation for the Designed APC, para 1` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "When APC is enabled, the average number of scheduled prefill sequences decreases dramatically from 5.32 to 0.46, whereas the average prefill chunk size surges from 0.78 to 6.29."
7. STATED LIMITS: benefit needs scheduling opportunities (fine chunks, enough sequence slots); evaluation is Qwen3-8B, 512-token context for the aging runs, single node, single tenant; future work names larger models, longer-context workloads, multi-node and multi-tenant settings. Code released at github.com/Charmstok/Chunked_Prefill_Serving (a Sarathi-Serve tree, exists, pushed 2026-06-05, 1 star) and github.com/lhx-666-cool/vllm-ascend branch aging (exists, commit 834a853c); neither was read in this dive.
   - *unaffected*: Limitations are the paper's own words; repo existence verified via GitHub API, contents not read.
   - anchor `§4.1 Experimental Setup, para 2` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "The maximum context length is set to 512, and each request has a maximum generation length of 512 tokens. The workload is replayed from ShareGPT"
   - anchor `§5 Conclusion and Future Work, para 2` @ arXiv:2606.09061v1 HTML: "Second, the proposed mechanisms should be further evaluated in larger-scale and more complex production environments, including larger models, longer-context workloads, multi-node deployments, or multi-tenant settings, in order to further examine their scalability and practical applicability."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b wave 2: v1 HTML read in full (sha256 12e3d255), equations recovered from MathML alttext; the Stage-1 belief (fairness-aware chunked-prefill scheduling, SPF starves longs, aging as middle ground) is confirmed; corrections are that the paper measures no fairness metric and no SPF arm, the over-10% figure is chunk-256 only with a loss at chunk 1024 and at 2-GPU 256/10, the LPRS gain is 0.1 s-arrival only with a regression at 1.0 s, and the APC gain is against a degenerate 0.78-token-chunk baseline; the released repos exist (GitHub API) but were not read. Second reader (independent agent, 2026-10-21): re-find 13/13 (Eq. (1) in MathML alttext; U+00A0 byte-identical), hash 13/13; support yes 7 / partial 1 / no 0 (claim 1 over-read "the paper says so explicitly" about the SLO term, reworded); chunk-1024, weight-base, predictor and Ascend numbers all match.

### Verdict

A textbook aging (wait-time minus remaining-work) ordering of prefill candidates inside a Sarathi-style chunked-prefill engine, measured ONLY against FCFS on an 8B model with a 512-token context and a saturated 200-request backlog: about -10% mean E2E / -11% mean TTFT at 256-token chunks, shrinking to nil or a small loss at 1024-token chunks or 10 sequence slots, with every gain coming from queue wait and none from execution time. It does NOT supply the aging-vs-SLO trade-off that vLLM-Ascend SRF (intake-2215#record) lacks: there is no aging bound, no SLO term, no per-class TTFT, no starvation metric, only one two-point sensitivity whose lesson is that stronger aging regresses toward FCFS. Transfer: (1) score any ordering policy on queue wait alone (the SRF-A1 split), (2) reordering needs a fine per-iteration prefill budget (KVU-16g N in the 256-512 class) to have anything to reorder, (3) the aging bound, if ever needed, must come from our own long-request queue-wait tail under strict short-first, capped at the long TTFT SLO. LPRS and APC are design references for --prefill-budget-target-ms and for a fragmentation guard, not results to cite.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- updated `gpu-serving-tie-in-program.md`
