## intake-2220#record — GreenLLM: SLO-Aware Dynamic Frequency Scaling for Energy-Efficient LLM Serving

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16449
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, routing_intelligence, hardware_optimization, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2216#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Routing rule: at ingress requests are partitioned by PROMPT length into n prefill queues/workers using n-1 cutoffs; n = 2 in the paper, with a short/medium class up to approximately 1024 tokens and a long class above it. The cutoff is given as an example (e.g.), not derived: NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE for any percentile, sweep, or sensitivity over the cutoff. The 1024 figure coincides with the upper end of the prefill microbenchmark prompt range (256-1024 tokens).
   - *narrowed*: The 1024 cutoff is introduced with e.g. and approximately; no percentile, sweep or sensitivity exists in the paper. It is not a measured split point.
   - anchor `Section 3.1 Adaptive Prompt Routing, paragraph 2` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "we configure $(n-1)$ threshold cut-off points on the prompt length to divide traffic among $n$ workers based on the size of the input (n is 2 in our design)."
   - anchor `Section 3.1, bullet Short-Context Model` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "Short-Context Model: Optimized for low-latency processing of SM inputs (up to approximately 1024 tokens)."
   - anchor `Section 2.2.1 Profiling with microbenchmark` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "Prompts are length-randomized within 256 $-$ 1024 tokens."
1. Pools: both prefill workers are the same hardware and the same model (Dynamo prefill pool = 2 workers x 2 A100 each; decode pool = 4 workers x 1 A100); the only thing that differs between the two prefill classes at runtime is the SM clock the prefill optimizer chooses per class. NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE for any per-pool batch size, max context, KV reservation or energy-mode difference between the short and long workers.
   - *unaffected*: Pool composition confirmed in two places; per-pool batch/context/KV settings are absent, so the Short-Context Model / Long-Context Model names denote classes, not different configurations.
   - anchor `Section 3 overview (Figure 4 walk-through)` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "A Prefill pool (two workers, each using 2 GPUs) computes the KV-cache."
   - anchor `Section 4.2.1 Models` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "The system uses two execution pools: a Prefill pool (2 workers, 2 GPUs each) and a Decode pool (4 workers, 1 GPU each)."
2. HOL-blocking evidence for routing alone (PrefillSplit ablation, Qwen3-14B, Alibaba chat trace): at 8 QPS the TTFT pass rate rises from 89.9% to 96.4%; at the other intensities routing alone is neutral or slightly negative (Table 3: 1 QPS 99.3 to 98.5, 3 QPS 99.5 to 98.8, 5 QPS 96.0 to 99.0, 10 QPS 84.9 to 89.1; Azure code5 98.5 to 96.6) and changes energy by only about 1-3%. The routing benefit is load-regime dependent: it appears when the single queue approaches prefill saturation.
   - *narrowed*: The 89.9 to 96.4 figure is one row (chat 8 QPS, Qwen3-14B); the same table shows routing-only lowering TTFT pass rate at 1 and 3 QPS and on Azure code5, which the text does not discuss.
   - anchor `Section 3.1, last paragraph` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "In total, the percentage of the requests meeting the SLO increase sharply from 89.9% to 96.4%."
   - anchor `Section 5.2, paragraph after Table 4` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "The routing-only ablation PrefillSplit reduces head-of-line interference and slightly tightens TTFT tails, but yields only $\lesssim$ 1–3% energy change across traces and models."
   - anchor `Section 5.2, Table 3 text` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "Especially for QPS 8, the TTFT% from defaultNV shows much worse result, where it is only 89.9% compared to 95.7% in GreenLLM."
3. Objective is GPU ENERGY under latency SLOs, not latency itself: SLOs are TTFT below 400 ms (short/medium), below 2 s (long), P95 TBT at or below 100 ms (Azure/DynamoLLM targets). Prefill: per-class SM clock from a queueing-aware optimisation (quadratic latency fit in prompt length at a reference clock, latency scaled by f_ref/f, cubic power fit P(f), minimise active plus idle energy subject to total busy time within the deadline D). Decode: a dual-loop controller (200 ms TPS window selects a 3-clock band from an offline TPS-to-frequency table with 3-interval hysteresis; a 20 ms loop steps 15 MHz up when P95 TBT / 100 ms exceeds 1.0 and down when it is below 0.65; band table re-biased every 6 s when more than 80% of steps hit a bound).
   - *unaffected*: SLO targets and both controller loops confirmed verbatim; the decrease threshold 0.65 was recovered from HTML alttext after the text dump dropped it.
   - anchor `Section 3.3.2 Fine-Grain TBT Tracker` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "Its role is to track the target TBT (100 ms P95) and conserve energy by reducing the frequency whenever possible."
   - anchor `Section 5.1.1 Prefill-Stage Evaluation` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "for short/medium prompts (400 ms SLO), GreenDVFS intentionally save energy by exploiting the slack from SLO"
   - anchor `Section 3.3.1 Coarse-Grain Frequency Band Selection` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "For each TPS bucket, we identify the lowest frequency that satisfies two criteria, namely, maintains P95 TBT below 100 ms and minimizes energy per token (i.e., power/TPS)."
4. Energy-vs-frequency is U-shaped with different knees per phase (A100-40GB, Qwen3-14B): prefill minimum in a narrow band around 0.95-1.05 GHz (70-80% of the 1.41 GHz max) across TPS levels; decode knee lower (energy rises again beyond about 1.2 GHz; decode is memory-bound so time per token saturates while power grows superlinearly); on the Alibaba chat trace at 5 QPS a fixed cap near 0.75 GHz cut total energy by about 47% versus the default governor.
   - *unaffected*: Knee values are read from the prose describing Figures 3(a)-(c); the 47% figure is a fixed-cap sweep at 5 QPS, not the controller.
   - anchor `Section 2.2.2 System Profiling, Figure 3(a) text` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "higher TPS shifts the curves upward but the energy-minimizing frequency remains in a narrow band around 0.95–1.05 GHz, about 70–80% of the 1.41 GHz max"
   - anchor `Section 2.2.2 System Profiling, Figure 3(c) text` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "capping the GPU clocks around 0.75 GHz reduced total inference energy by $\sim$ 47% compared to the default performance governor (which drives frequencies near peak)."
5. Evaluation: one DGX-A100 (8 x A100-SXM4 40 GB, EPYC 7302), NVIDIA Dynamo v0.3.1 with TensorRT-LLM, DVFS via NVML application clocks with memory clock pinned and autoboost off; models Qwen3-14B (dense, BF16) and Qwen3-30B-A3B (MoE, BF16); traces Alibaba ServeGen chat at 1/3/5/8/10 QPS for at least 30 min per run and Azure 2024 code/conversation downsampled to 1/8 and 1/5 (Section 4.2.2 also lists 1/4; no 1/4 rows appear in the tables); baselines DefaultNV, PrefillSplit (routing only), GreenLLM.
   - *narrowed*: Azure downsampling rates disagree between Section 4.2.2 (1/8, 1/5, 1/4) and Section 5.2 / tables (two rates); single node only.
   - anchor `Section 4.1 Hardware and Software Setup` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "all experiments run on a NVIDIA DGX-A100 node, using NVIDIA Dynamo as deployment framework."
   - anchor `Section 4.2.2 Traces and Evaluation Methods` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "We compare three configurations: DefaultNV (NVIDIA default), PrefillSplit (length-based routing only), and GreenLLM (length-based routing + prefill/decode optimizer)."
   - anchor `Section 5.2 Trace Evaluation with Intensity Scaling` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "We replay the Alibaba trace at {1, 3, 5, 8, 10} QPS for at least 30 minutes per run"
6. Headline numbers: total energy reduction up to 34% (Azure_conv5, Qwen3-14B: 34.09%); Alibaba chat falls from 27.5% at 1 QPS to 6.8% at 10 QPS; Qwen3-30B-MoE 19.7-31% on Azure and 10-21% on Alibaba; decode energy 0.62-0.73x default on Azure (14B). The abstract claims less than 3.5% additional SLO violations and no loss of throughput, but Table 4 shows TBT pass rate falling 99.8 to 93.8 (Azure_conv8, MoE: 6.0 pp vs defaultNV) and 99.7 to 95.9 (Azure_code5, MoE: 3.8 pp vs defaultNV), and the authors themselves describe 93.8-97.0% TBT pass rates; NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE for any throughput (tokens/s or requests completed) table supporting the no-loss claim.
   - *narrowed*: Abstract headline contradicted by Table 4 (6.0 pp TBT pass-rate drop vs defaultNV) and unsupported on throughput (no throughput data in the paper).
   - anchor `Section 5.2, Table 3 text` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "Energy savings of GreenLLM drop from 27.5% at 1 QPS to 6.8% at 10 QPS as higher decode clocks and power are needed to sustain TBT. On Azure, however, GreenLLM consistently saves 28–34%, mainly from reduced decode energy (0.62–0.73 $\times$ defaultNV)."
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "with no loss of throughput and only less than 3.5% SLO violations increase"
   - anchor `Section 5.2, Table 4 text` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "pass rates are similarly high at feasible intensities with occasional modest TBT% reductions (e.g., 93.8–97.0%) on Azure code slices."
7. Decode controller cost and limits: at light load (200-400 TPS) GreenLLM P90 TBT is about 60-66 ms versus 40-46 ms for the default governor (still under the 100 ms SLO); decode energy savings 20-25% at low TPS falling to 8-12% at 2400-3000 TPS; sinusoidal trace 8.9% lower decode energy at p99 TBT 83.2 vs 84.6 ms. NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE: a limitations section, a code or artifact URL, any CPU-side power accounting, any multi-node result, any mid-session class migration or KV-locality interaction with the routing.
   - *unaffected*: Controller cost at light load and the sinusoidal result confirmed; absence items are NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE and cannot be anchored.
   - anchor `Section 5.1.2 Decode-Stage Evaluation` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "at 200–400 TPS GreenLLM shows $\sim$ 60–66 ms versus $\sim$ 40–46 ms for the defaultNV, still comfortably below the SLO."
   - anchor `Section 5.1.3 Dynamic-Tracing Evaluation` @ arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML: "GreenLLM achieves 8.9% lower decode energy while achieving similar or slightly better tail latency (p99 TBT 83.2 ms vs. 84.6 ms)."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b wave 2 (research-intake/stage2b): combined ingest+dive; FULL read of arXiv:2508.16449v1 HTML; the brief framed the source as independent evidence for a length-routing cutoff near 1024 tokens, corrected to: a GPU-DVFS energy paper whose routing stage uses an example cutoff with no selection method and identical-hardware pools; the abstract headline (under 3.5% extra SLO violations, no throughput loss) is contradicted by Table 4 and unsupported on throughput; answer to the WP-15 question is that the paper supplies a load-regime dependence of routing-only gains, not a cutoff location, and energy is declined as a WP-15 decision axis (shared-package attribution, no lab objective). Dive-surfaced: LAPS arXiv 2601.11589 (dual-queue short/long prefill on one or several instances; closest to a single-instance temporal split and worth a wave-3 dive), Ascendra arXiv 2504.20828 and QUARTZ (ACL Findings 2026) (priority/cost-aware alternatives to a static cutoff; PROPOSED, operator confirms at Stage 3), throttLL eM arXiv 2408.05235 (predictive GPU DVFS, same energy axis; decline unless energy becomes an objective), the unrelated Shi et al. GreenLLM (Dec 2024, carbon-aware fleets; decline, name collision only). 2026-10-21 second-reader correction: Table 4 baseline column fixed (6.0 pp / 3.8 pp vs defaultNV), four anchor quotes restored to the source thin spaces.

### Verdict

GreenLLM is a GPU-DVFS energy paper whose length-routing stage is an undirected example (two identical-hardware prefill workers split at about 1024 prompt tokens, no selection method). Its one useful routing datum is the ablation: routing alone recovers TTFT pass rate only when the single prefill queue is near saturation (Alibaba 8 QPS, 89.9 to 96.4) and is neutral to slightly negative at low load, so any replay of a short/long split on the EPYC frontdoor must be scored per load regime, not pooled. It is not evidence for a cutoff value; our own HSF-4 append distribution is. Do not cite the abstract headline (34% energy, under 3.5% extra violations, no throughput loss): the tables show a 6.0 pp TBT pass-rate drop vs defaultNV and no throughput data. Energy is measurable on the EPYC host and the MI210 but is not attributable per CPU instance (shared package) and is not a lab objective; it does not enter WP-15 as a decision axis.
adopt_patterns: the load-regime-stratified replay design and the alpha(1024) curve point transfer as zero-compute refinements to WP-15 and HSF-4; the energy machinery is not applicable now and is recorded as knowledge-only with a named re-open trigger.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `within-role-placement-state-machine.md`
- updated `heterogeneous-slot-fabric-residency.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- updated `gpu-serving-tie-in-program.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): GL-A1 refine WP-15: stratify the replay score and the holdout by load regime (fleet in-flight count and arrival-rate bucket at ts_arrival from dispatch.v1), because routing alone moved TTFT pass rate only at the saturating intensity (8 QPS: 89.9 to 96.4) and was neutral or slightly negative elsewhere; a pooled score averages the effect away
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): GL-A2 refine HSF-4 (with TB-A4): include B = 1024 in the alpha(B) set {1K, 2K, 4K, 8K, 16K} and label the two cutoff scales as different mechanisms (prompt-length prefill HOL in GreenLLM vs total-budget KV reservation in intake-2216#record); from the recorded append p50/p90 (467/5362) alpha(1024) lies between 0.5 and 0.9 and must be read from the log, not from this paper
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): GL-A3 DI-126 note (knowledge-only): the GreenLLM 8 QPS row is the magnitude class a shared-queue HOL symptom produces (about 6.5 pp of TTFT pass rate at saturation); the trigger text is unchanged because the number is A100/Dynamo-specific
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): GL-A4 decline energy as a WP-15 DECISION axis now: host package energy cannot be attributed per instance (AK-X-5: shared_package_window, never lane-exclusive; AK-X-5a cross-lane loaded-frequency ratios 0.74-0.83), and the lab has no energy objective in the measurement constitution; re-open only if the operator adds energy/power as an objective, in which case the first step is a zero-inference energy-per-dispatch column joined to dispatch.v1 from the existing RAPL energy_uj counters, not a routing term
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): GL-A5 knowledge-only: the decode-is-memory-bound-so-a-lower-SM-clock-costs-little-TBT finding is the first probe to run on the MI210 (rsmi energy counter, 1 ms refresh, counter x 15.3) if a power or thermal cap ever constrains the GPU champion; not applicable while GPU posture is maximum performance under fixed clocks
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): GL-A6 citation hygiene: never cite the abstract headline (34%, less than 3.5% extra SLO violations, no throughput loss); cite Table 3/4 rows with their intensity, and note the 6.0 pp TBT pass-rate drop vs defaultNV in Table 4

## intake-2221#record — FlowPrefill: Decoupling Preemption from Prefill Scheduling Granularity to Mitigate Head-of-Line Blocking in LLM Serving

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16603
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, benchmark_methodology, cost_aware_routing)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2215#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Problem: the blocker is an IN-FLIGHT long prefill, not queue order. On a PD-disaggregated prefill instance a single long-context prefill monopolizes the GPU for hundreds of ms; SJF-style reordering of the waiting queue (multi-level queues, length prediction) cannot touch it, and chunk-level (Sarathi/Medha) or layer-level (Layered Prefill, Laser) preemption only reaches fixed boundaries, trading throughput (small chunks) against blocking (large chunks).
   - *unaffected*: Problem statement verified in sections 1 and 2.2; it is the same gap the SRF dive (intake-2215#record) found for waiting-queue-only reordering.
   - anchor `§1 Introduction` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "However, these approaches still cannot address HoL blocking caused by long prefill computations."
   - anchor `§2.2 HoL Problem in Disaggregated Serving` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "Since prefill is compute-bound, a single long-context request can monopolize a GPU for hundreds of milliseconds, causing severe HoL blocking for subsequent requests."
1. Mechanism: cooperative operator-level preemption. Preemption checks are inserted between core operators (qkv_proj, attn, o_proj, gate_up_proj, down_proj; MoE adds gate and experts). On a scheduler signal the runtime finishes the current operator, ACKs, and suspends the task; each request runs as its own thread with a dedicated CUDA stream and vLLM runner (ThreadPoolExecutor on vLLM-0.11.2), so suspended state is the live thread/stream; TP safety via a synchronized iteration counter. Memory held by a suspended mid-layer task is NOT quantified (NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE).
   - *narrowed*: Operator list and cooperative ACK protocol verified; the resident memory of a suspended mid-layer task and the resume cost are not quantified anywhere in the paper.
   - anchor `§5.1 Operator-Level Preemption` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "In this work, which targets dense LLMs, we place preemption checks at the boundaries between core operators, including qkv_proj, attn, o_proj, gate_up_proj, down_proj."
   - anchor `§5.1 Operator-Level Preemption` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "By restricting preemption to operator boundaries, FlowPrefill avoids interrupting in-flight kernels while bounding preemption latency by the execution time of a single operator."
   - anchor `§6.1 Experimental Setup / Implementation` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "The Execution Pool uses ThreadPoolExecutor to manage per-request execution tasks, each running in its own thread with a dedicated CUDA stream and vLLM runner, synchronized via concurrency primitives."
   - anchor `§5.1 Tensor parallelism supporting` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "To prevent this, FlowPrefill uses a synchronized iteration counter among tensor parallel processes and only suspends them when they reach the same counter, ensuring safe preemption without disrupting communication."
2. Policy and knobs: event-driven scheduling (a scheduling round only on request arrival or completion, about 2 rounds per request) with Slack-aware EDF priority = sgn(slack)/deadline where slack = deadline - now - predicted TTFT, and SLO-aware batching that adds candidates to the highest-priority request only while predicted batch latency fits its remaining time and a batch token budget G is not exceeded (G ablated at ~4K-8K, little difference between the two). Knobs: per-task TTFT SLOs, G, preemption-boundary choice, optional chunk size.
   - *unaffected*: Priority formula, event set and Algorithm 1/2 verified; G values 4K/8K from the Figure 11 prose, exact best G NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE.
   - anchor `§5.2 Event-Driven Scheduling` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "Instead of continuously polling for preemption opportunities, scheduling is triggered only by two types of events: request arrival and completion."
   - anchor `§5.2 Event-Driven Scheduling` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "In other words, the request with the highest priority is the one with the earliest deadline and non-negative slack. By incorporating slack into the priority, S-EDF can proactively deprioritize requests that cannot meet their deadlines, thereby improving the stability of SLO adherence."
   - anchor `§5.2 Event-Driven Scheduling / SLO-aware batching` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "The batch token budget ensures that the benefits of batching short requests are properly captured."
3. Evaluation setup: vLLM-0.11.2, 8x NVIDIA A800-SXM4-80GB (200 GB/s NVLink), Llama3-8B/Qwen2.5-14B/Llama3-70B at TP 1/2/4 plus Qwen3-30B-A3B at TP2; QwenTrace (four task types, prompt means 532-6833 tokens, P99 up to 22390) with per-task TTFT SLOs (Llama3-8B: 0.25/0.5/4.0/6.0 s) and random token contents; baselines DistServe 1P1D with FCFS, DistServe + chunked prefill + EDF at chunk 2048 (CP2K) and 8192 (CP8K), both built by the authors; co-located vLLM-CP2K.
   - *unaffected*: Hardware, software, models, trace, SLO table and baseline construction verified verbatim; note both chunked-prefill baselines were built by the authors on DistServe, not vendor configurations.
   - anchor `§6.1 Experimental Setup / Implementation` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "We implement FlowPrefill on vLLM-0.11.2 (Kwon et al., 2023)."
   - anchor `§6.1 Experimental Setup / Hardware` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "We conduct experiments on a server equipped with eight NVIDIA A800-SXM4-80GB GPUs interconnected via 200 GB/s NVLink, two 52-core Intel Xeon Platinum 8470 CPUs, and 1 TB of host memory."
   - anchor `§6.1 Experimental Setup / Baselines` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "Since DistServe defaults to a First-Come-First-Served (FCFS) policy, we augment its prefill stage with Chunked Prefill (CP) (Agrawal et al., 2024a) and an Earliest-Deadline-First (EDF) policy to enable SLO-aware scheduling. We evaluate two variants: DistServe-CP2K (chunk size 2048) and DistServe-..."
   - anchor `§6.1 Experimental Setup / Workloads` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "As the trace contains only request lengths and does not include the actual prompt contents, we generate random token sequences that conform to the specified lengths for each request."
4. Headline numbers: goodput (max request rate at 90% TTFT-SLO attainment) 4.7x-5.6x over plain-FCFS DistServe, but only up to 2.0x over the authors' own DistServe-CP2K and up to 4.5x over CP8K; 1.5x-2.3x tighter SLOs than CP2K, 2.1x-3.1x than CP8K; MoE Qwen3-30B-A3B up to 1.6x goodput / 2.4x tighter SLO; co-located TBT SLO attainment up to 1.6x.
   - *narrowed*: The 5.6x abstract figure is vs FCFS DistServe; vs chunked-prefill+EDF baselines the paper's own text gives up to 2.0x (CP2K).
   - anchor `§6.1 Experimental Setup / Metrics` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "We define goodput as the maximum sustainable request rate at 90% SLO attainment."
   - anchor `§6.2 End-to-End Speedup` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "Across all models, FlowPrefill sustains 4.7 $\times$ –5.6 $\times$ higher request rates than DistServe, and outperforms DistServe-CP2K and DistServe-CP8K by up to 2.0 $\times$ and 4.5 $\times$ , respectively."
   - anchor `§6.2 End-to-End Speedup` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "Compared to DistServe-CP2K, FlowPrefill supports 1.5 $\times$ –2.3 $\times$ tighter SLOs, and compared to DistServe-CP8K, 2.1 $\times$ –3.1 $\times$ tighter SLOs."
5. Overheads: preemption blocking time (signal to ACK) is 3.5x-4.2x lower than layer-level checks with all observed values below 4.5 ms (Llama3-8B); the checks show no measurable throughput loss on a single-SLO ShareGPT workload (inputs < 2K, so little preemption actually occurs); the TTFT predictor is a polynomial in token count fitted offline, accurate because PD disaggregation removes decode interference.
   - *narrowed*: The 'no measurable efficiency loss' result is on a workload with inputs below 2K where preemption rarely fires; blocking-time numbers are Llama3-8B only.
   - anchor `§6.4 Runtime Analysis / Preemption blocking time` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "As illustrated in Figure 12, operator-level preemption reduces the average blocking time by 3.5 $\times$ –4.2 $\times$ compared to layer-level preemption, with all observed latencies remaining below $4.5$ ms."
   - anchor `§6.5 System compatibility / Single-SLO scenario` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "These results demonstrate that the preemption checks used in §5.1 do not introduce measurable efficiency loss."
6. Limitations stated by the authors: for very long inputs a single operator can still take noticeable time, so FlowPrefill is combined with chunked prefill at an intermediate chunk size (2K too small, 16K too coarse; the best value is only shown in Figure 15); decode instances stay FCFS and are not studied; the proxy is round-robin; the system targets PD-disaggregated prefill instances.
   - *unaffected*: Limitations verified in section 6.5 and section 4 (decode FCFS, round-robin proxy).
   - anchor `§6.5 System compatibility / Chunked prefill` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "For very long inputs, however, one operator can still introduce noticeable latency. In such cases, combining operator-level preemption with chunked prefill reduces individual operator runtimes and further tightens the upper bound on blocking time."
   - anchor `§6.5 System compatibility / Chunked prefill` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "As shown in Figure 15, small chunk sizes (e.g., 2K tokens) provide finer preemption granularity but incur substantial overhead from frequent request splitting, degrading performance. Large chunk sizes (e.g., 16K tokens) reduce splitting overhead but lack sufficient preemption granularity, also hu..."
7. Corroboration of a design choice we already made: small fixed chunks collapse prefill throughput (kernel-launch overhead, re-reading prior K/V under causal attention, device under-utilization), which is the same reason KVU-16g caps the prompt tokens per iteration only while a slot is decoding and keeps -ub 2048 for solo prefill; the per-iteration cost model (polynomial in token count) parallels PB-QCOST-1, except ours carries a q*c cache-depth term because our slots share one KV cache.
   - *reattributed*: The chunk-size efficiency finding is the paper's motivation (Figure 3, Takeaway-1); the mapping to KVU-16g and PB-QCOST-1 is this dive's framing, not a claim of the paper.
   - anchor `§3.1 Preemption Granularity v.s. Efficiency` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "Small chunks lead to a significant throughput collapse due to: (1) increased kernel launch overheads; (2) redundant memory accesses to the KV cache (since causal attention (Radford et al., 2018) requires reloading prior keys/values); and (3) under-utilization of device computation."
   - anchor `§6.4 Runtime Analysis / TTFT prediction` @ arXiv:2602.16603v1 HTML: "Our prediction model fits a polynomial to offline prefill profiles, with $x$ as token count and $y$ as predicted TTFT."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b wave 2: confirmed title/authors/v1 date 2026-02-18; corrected the headline from 'up to 5.6x vs state-of-the-art' to 'vs FCFS DistServe, up to 2.0x vs the authors' chunked-prefill+EDF DistServe'; established from the frozen v10 source (not from the paper) that llama-server's residual HoL is slot-index-order dependent and that the KVU-16g budget is decode-gated, which re-scopes DI-126 and KVU-16g-2; no numbers invented, figure-only values marked NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE. Second reader (independent agent, 2026-10-21): re-find 22/22 in paper.txt, hash 22/22; support yes 7 / partial 1 / no 0 (claim 7 mapping to KVU-16g is the dive framing, already reattributed); three numbers verbatim; independently re-verified in the frozen tree: server-context.cpp:2793 iterate() plain for-loop, :3210-3212 return on batch.size() >= n_batch, :3614 fill while batch.size() < n_batch @ ffc1bac82eeca6f9099e1ccd9ba49703c460a115, prefill_budget_get() returns 0 when pb_iter.n_generating == 0 (98c0ce12a), and 98c0ce12a is NOT an ancestor of ffc1bac82 (experimental branch only; zero prefill_budget hits in the frozen file).

### Verdict

FlowPrefill answers a different question than our prefill budget. KVU-16g bounds how much of a neighbour's prefill rides in each iteration WHILE A SLOT DECODES (decode-vs-prefill); FlowPrefill gives a higher-priority prefill the GPU within one operator (prefill-vs-prefill). Its mechanism (per-request threads and streams, cooperative suspend inside the forward) is not portable to llama-server's one-graph-per-iteration execution, and its preemption bound (< 4.5 ms) is not needed: the paper itself shows an intermediate chunk is the efficient operating point. What IS ours to take is the ordering: the frozen v10 pending-prompt loop fills the batch in fixed slot-index order and skips every later slot once the batch (or the budget) is spent, so a short request landing in a higher-index slot waits for the whole remaining long prefill (PB-QCOST-1 predicts ~75 s vs ~4 s mid-way through a 64K prompt), and the budget never applies when no slot is generating. The smallest measurement is the 2x2 slot-index x budget probe (FP-A3); if it confirms, the fix is shortest-remaining-first ordering of that loop plus a prefill-vs-prefill budget on an experimental branch (FP-A4), not operator-level preemption.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `gpu-serving-tie-in-program.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): FP-A1 (task, text refinement of DI-126 in kv-unified-stack-rollout.md:1363): replace the dormant trigger with the structural fact found in this dive. In the frozen v10 tree the pending-prompt loop (tools/server/server-context.cpp:3205-3230 and :3614) walks slots in fixed index order via iterate() (:2793) and returns for all remaining slots once batch.size() >= n_batch; a short request whose slot index is above a prefilling long slot therefore gets zero prompt tokens until that prompt is complete, while one below it waits exactly one iteration. The monitor must record id_slot of both requests; the DI-126 signature is slot-index dependent, not a p95 drift.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): FP-A2 (task, text refinement of KVU-16g-2 in kv-unified-stack-rollout.md:1114): record that --prefill-budget-decoding is gated on n_generating > 0 (prefill_budget_get(), 98c0ce12a) and never caps prefill-vs-prefill; enabling it in v11 does NOT address DI-126 and must not be counted as covering it.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): FP-A3 (task, GPU window, owner gpu-serving-tie-in-program.md, proposed id PB-HOL-1): the smallest residual measurement. On the champion store build, np=3, one 64K prompt pinned with id_slot, a 128-token prompt pinned to the other slot sent ~midway through the prefill, 2x2 arms {short slot index below, above} x {budget off, budget on with pb512 armed by a third slot decoding}, 3 repeats each, record short TTFT, id_slot, and the prefill-budget: iter log lines. PB-QCOST-1 fit (27B, t = 324 + 0.987 q + 2.41e-5 q c ms) predicts below/off ~4 s (one iteration at c~32K), above/off ~75 s (the remaining 16 iterations), below/on ~1.2 s, above/on unchanged ~75 s. Rule: above/off >= 2x below/off confirms slot-order HoL and opens FP-A4; otherwise close DI-126 as not reproducible.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): FP-A4 (task, conditional on FP-A3, experimental branch only): shortest-remaining-prompt-first (or oldest-arrival-first) ordering of the pending-prompt loop, i.e. sort the processing slots before iterate() fills the batch, plus an optional prefill-budget-shared cap that applies when >= 2 slots are in SLOT_STATE_PROCESSING_PROMPT with no decoder. This is the portable part of FlowPrefill's S-EDF: preemption granularity stays one iteration (budget tokens), which the paper's own Figures 3/15 say is the efficient operating point. Gate: short TTFT in the above arm drops to ~one iteration with solo 64K prefill within the KVU-16g -4% envelope and off == byte-identical.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): FP-A5 (decline): operator-level cooperative preemption itself. llama-server executes a ubatch as one ggml_backend_sched graph; the only mid-graph hooks are the eval callback and llama_set_abort_callback, and an abort discards the ubatch's work. The per-request thread + dedicated stream + runner model does not map onto one-graph-per-iteration execution; the cheaper equivalent of a smaller preemption bound is a smaller prompt chunk (the budget), already built.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): FP-A6 (decline): S-EDF with per-request TTFT SLOs and SLO-aware batching. Our server has no SLO classes; the orchestrator's SharedKVPoolAdmission only knows long (>= 16384 tokens) vs short. Revisit only if per-role TTFT SLOs are ever declared (none in the worktree handoffs).
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): FP-A7 (knowledge-only): the polynomial TTFT predictor is the PD-disaggregated cousin of PB-QCOST-1; ours needs the q*c term because slots share one KV cache. No action.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): FP-A8 (knowledge-only): Figure 3 / Takeaway-1 corroborate keeping -ub 2048 for solo prefill and capping only under contention (KVU-16g design). No action.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): FP-A9 (monitor): a ggml backend-scheduler suspend/resume primitive (graph split at operator boundary with resumable state) would make FP-A5 revisitable; trigger = any upstream ggml change that lets ggml_backend_sched_graph_compute return partway and resume.

## intake-2222#record — LAPS: A Length-Aware-Prefill LLM Serving System (v1 title: PLA-Serve: A Prefill-Length-Aware LLM Serving System; MLSys 2026 oral)

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11589
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, kv_cache, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2218#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Problem: prefill-decode disaggregation does not remove interference INSIDE the prefill stage. Mixing long (>1K-token, compute-bound) prefills with short (<64-token in Figure 1, <256 elsewhere, memory-bound) prefills/re-prefills in one batch raises the P90 TTFT of BOTH classes versus serving each class alone, and the penalty grows with concurrency (Qwen2.5-32B, one H200). The authors model it as an M/G/1 head-of-line term proportional to (S_long - S_short)^2 and note short requests suffer more in normalized latency; on LMSYS-Chat-1M about 63% of first-turn prompts and 81% of later-turn prompts are under 256 tokens, so the short class dominates.
   - *unaffected*: Figure-only evidence for the interference magnitude; the 63%/81% short-prompt shares are LMSYS-Chat-1M trace facts, not our traffic.
   - anchor `Section 1 Introduction, paragraph 1` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "even with PD, interference still persists within the prefill stage when long, compute-bound prefills are mixed with short, memory-bound prefills/re-prefills."
   - anchor `Section 1, Figure 2 caption` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "approximately 63% of requests contain fewer than 256 tokens. In subsequent turns, the proportion of prompts shorter than 256 tokens increases to an average of 81%."
1. Mechanism (dual queue, mutual exclusion): every request is classified by prompt length against a boundary L_m into a short queue Q_s or a long queue Q_l, and a prefill batch is formed from ONE queue only, never mixed. Short batches are built by the AWD scheduler; a long batch advances a single request by fixed-size chunks C_l. The paper calls this a fourth serving mode, "prefill batch temporal/spatial disaggregation", beside mix, PD temporal and PD spatial disaggregation.
   - *unaffected*: Mechanism as described; the paper gives no code and no SGLang upstream equivalent was found.
   - anchor `Section 3.2, Mutual exclusion` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "All requests are first classified by prompt length L_{p} using the boundary point L_{m} . Each class maintains an independent queue: a short queue Q_{s} and a long queue Q_{l} ."
   - anchor `Section 1, fourth serving mode bullet` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "Prefill batch temporal/spatial disaggregation (ours): Enabling disaggregation within the prefill stage (rather than between prefill and decode), separating long- and short-prefill batches temporally on a single instance or spatially across instances."
2. Single-instance "temporal disaggregation" is UNDERSPECIFIED. The introduction says long and short batches are separated "temporally on a single instance", but Section 3.2 defines the mode as each instance being dedicated exclusively to one class and pulling only from its own queue; no alternation rule, time slice, budget split, priority between Q_s and Q_l, or preemption of an in-flight long chunk is given for the one-instance case (NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE). Operationally the only mechanism the text supports is batch homogeneity: a short batch and a long chunk never share a forward pass, with per-queue policy SLA-first (deadlines) or token-max (no deadlines).
   - *narrowed*: The single-instance temporal mode has no stated alternation/priority rule between Q_s and Q_l; only batch homogeneity is supported by the text. Do not cite LAPS for any time-slicing or preemption policy.
   - anchor `Section 3.2, Temporal disaggregation mode for single instance` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "LAPS adopts a temporal disaggregation mode, where each GPU instance is dedicated exclusively to either short- or long-prefill execution. Two global queues Q_{s} and Q_{l} are maintained for short and long requests, respectively, and each instance pulls tasks only from its own queue."
3. Length threshold: L_m is the compute/memory crossover of a fitted latency model T_comp ~ alpha*L*(L+2H) + beta*L and T_mem ~ gamma_w*L + gamma_r*H (L new tokens, H cached history), with alpha, beta, gamma_w, gamma_r fitted from runtime samples; with long histories re-prefill stays memory-bound up to about gamma_r/(2*alpha) new tokens. The paper cites prior profiling placing the transition at 150-512 tokens on A100/H100/H200 for 7B-32B models; the experiments use <256 tokens as short. The actual fitted L_m used in the experiments is not reported (NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE).
   - *narrowed*: The 150-512-token crossover is cited from prior work (Yuan et al. 2024, DistServe), not measured here; the fitted L_m used in the experiments is not reported.
   - anchor `Section 2.1, Fitting at runtime` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "We collect runtime samples (T_{\text{comp}},T_{\text{mem}},L,H) to fit these two curves and obtain \alpha,\beta,\gamma_{w},\gamma_{r} , and then calculate the boundaries"
   - anchor `Section 2.1, Roofline model` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "shows that this transition typically occurs between 150 and 512 tokens"
4. Short-prefill batching rule (AWD, Adaptive Wait-Depth): accumulate short requests until a waiting window W expires or a target depth D (the largest captured CUDA-Graph batch that fits the memory budget) is reached; W = clip(min(W_SLA, W_GR), [W_min, W_max]) where W_SLA is the last safe wait before any pending request would miss its deadline after one prefill step and W_GR is the expected time to reach D at the estimated short arrival rate; dispatch immediately if slack <= sigma or head-of-line wait exceeds T_max; requests are grouped by length and padded to power-of-two (L in {8..256}, B in {1..64}) buckets with one CUDA Graph captured per bucket at startup; W and D are updated after each dispatch. Numeric W_min/W_max/sigma/T_max are not given (NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE).
   - *unaffected*: Algorithm 1 pseudocode is partially garbled in the HTML rendering; the claim is built from the prose, which is consistent with it.
   - anchor `Section 3.1, Graph-aware memory-based batching (AWD prose)` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "During each scheduling round, AWD accumulates short-prefill requests until either the waiting window W expires or the target depth D is reached."
   - anchor `Section 3.1, Graph capture and bucketization` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "During inference, each short-prefill request is padded to the nearest bucket and grouped with others sharing the same (L,B) configuration, thereby maximizing graph reuse with negligible memory overhead."
5. Interaction with decode and chunked prefill: LAPS is built on the PREFILL instance of a PD-disaggregated SGLang deployment. Long prefills use fixed-size chunks C_l (value NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE); no per-iteration token budget shared with decode exists because decode runs elsewhere. In the non-disaggregated "Mix with Decode" setting (Figure 8) prefill RPS DECREASES, and the paper states the CUDA-Graph throughput benefit for short prefills is only realised inside the PD-disaggregated architecture. Enabling CUDA Graphs alone can degrade throughput (graph eligibility checks and launch overhead); the disaggregation is what makes the graph gain positive.
   - *unaffected*: The non-PD degradation is the paper own finding and is the decisive fact for our single-server topology.
   - anchor `Section 3.2, SLA-constrained mode` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "Long-prefill dispatch continues to advance a single request by fixed-size chunks C_{l} , and each instance remains exclusive to either short or long mode."
   - anchor `Section 4.1, Figure 8 discussion` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "indicating that LAPS can fully exploit the throughput benefits of CUDA Graphs for short prefill requests only within the PD-disaggregated architecture."
   - anchor `Section 4.1, ablation remark` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "enabling CUDA Graphs alone yields limited improvements and can even degrade throughput, as the overhead of graph eligibility checking and graph launching becomes non-negligible."
6. Evaluation: SGLang + ~2K lines, one H200 (temporal, 1 prefill instance) and 8xH200 (spatial, 8 prefill instances), Qwen2.5-7B/14B/32B, ShareGPT(-4) and LMSYS-Chat-1M multi-turn traces, client concurrency 1-64, Poisson arrivals, TTFT SLO 0.4 s. Baselines: vanilla SGLang PD disaggregation, SGLang PD + DP router (round-robin / load-balancing), vLLM PD disaggregation, plus two LAPS ablations (graphs only, disaggregation only). No static-pool (fixed n_s/n_l) baseline and no explicitly named chunked-prefill baseline (NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE).
   - *narrowed*: No static-pool baseline and no named chunked-prefill baseline; whether SGLang default chunked prefill was active in the baseline is not stated.
   - anchor `Section 4, setup` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "We implement and deploy LAPS on an NVIDIA H200 GPU as well as on an 8 \times H200 multi-GPU cluster. We build it upon SGLang by extending \sim 2K lines of code."
   - anchor `Section 4.1, Figure 7 discussion` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "We set the TTFT SLO to 0.4s and vary the client-side concurrency levels to observe the actual SLO violation rate."
7. Headline numbers in the body: up to 20% (single instance) and 33% (8 instances) higher RPS and about 20% lower average latency vs SGLang PD; SLO-violation rate about 10% lower than SGLang PD + router and about 30% lower than vanilla DP on one instance; zero violations vs 4.7% for the router on 8 instances; offline distillation (4P+4D, 1K decode) 7.3% (LMSYS) / 8.3% (ShareGPT) less wall time. Per-class short/long TTFT numbers are only in figures (not extractable from the HTML). The ABSTRACT states "over 30%" prefill latency, "28%" SLO, "12%" vs router and "35%" throughput on Qwen2.5-32B, which do not match the body text. Costs: 228/240/277 MB per captured graph (7B/14B/32B) and 8-12 s capture per graph.
   - *narrowed*: Abstract headlines (>30%, 28%, 12%, 35%) are not reproduced anywhere in the body text; the body figures (20%/33% RPS, 20% latency, ~10%/~30% SLO) are the citable ones.
   - anchor `Section 4.1, RPS/latency headline` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "Specifically, LAPS achieves up to 20% and 33% higher RPS than the baseline in the single-prefill instance and 8-prefill instance settings, while reducing average latency by 20% respectively."
   - anchor `Section 4.1, SLO headline` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "within a single instance, LAPS reduces the SLO violation rate by approximately 10% compared with SGLang (PD disaggregation) with router, and by around 30% compared with Vanilla DP."
   - anchor `Section 4.2, Cost analysis` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "We measure single-graph sizes of 228 MB, 240 MB, and 277 MB for the 7B, 14B, and 32B models,"
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2601.11589v2 HTML: "LAPS reduces prefill latency by over 30% compared to vanilla SGLang under prefill–decode disaggregation, and further decreases SLO violations by 28% in multi-instance deployments with vanilla data-parallel configuration."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b wave 3: dived v2 (27 Jan 2026) in full from the arXiv HTML; v1 (4 Jan 2026) carried the title PLA-Serve, under which the paper is an MLSys 2026 oral (19 May 2026), so citations under either title refer to this work. The abstract headline numbers (>30% latency, 28%/12% SLO, 35% throughput) do not appear in the body, whose figures are 20%/33% RPS, 20% latency, ~10%/~30% SLO; cite the body. The single-instance temporal split is underspecified (no alternation or priority rule) and the paper reports its gain vanishes when prefill and decode share an instance. No intake id assigned; entry not written to research/intake_index.yaml. Second reader (independent agent, 2026-10-21): re-find 18/18 in paper.txt, hash 18/18; support yes 6 / partial 2 / no 0 (the partials are negative sub-statements wider than their anchors, content verified against the baseline list and Table 2); MLSys oral page confirmed under the v1 title; no factual mismatch.

### Verdict

LAPS (MLSys 2026 oral) is the peer-reviewed statement of the intra-prefill long/short interference problem and of one remedy: never put a short prefill and a long-prefill chunk in the same forward pass, and batch short prefills by length into graph-shaped buckets. Its headline gains are measured on a DEDICATED prefill instance of a PD-disaggregated SGLang with up to 64-way short-prefill concurrency and CUDA Graphs; the paper itself reports the gain disappears when prefill and decode share an instance, and the single-instance temporal mode is never specified beyond batch homogeneity. On our stack the orchestrator lease (KVU-15a) already serialises long prefills and passes shorts, and llama-server has the decode-aware budget (KVU-16g) plus the PB-QCOST-1 iteration fit; the one thing LAPS adds as a distinct third lever is batch homogeneity for a short prompt that arrives mid-long-prefill, worth at most one budget iteration minus one solo short iteration per collision. Measure that gap on :8083 (LP-A2) before building anything; decline the graph bucketing (LP-A4); use the spatial pool idea only as a WP-15 replay arm scored on long-request throughput (LP-A3).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `gpu-serving-tie-in-program.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- updated `within-role-placement-state-machine.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): LP-A1 refine DI-126 (with MD-A3 and SRF-A1): decompose a colliding short request TTFT on :8083 into three measurable parts: (i) deque wait (all np slots busy), (ii) slot-order wait (iterations in which the in-flight long prefill filled the ubatch before the short slot got any tokens), (iii) co-batch inflation (iteration ms with the long chunk present minus the solo iteration from the PB-QCOST-1 fit, 324 + 0.987 q ms). LAPS batch homogeneity is lever (c) and can only remove part (iii); the budget bounds (ii)+(iii); SRF reordering removes part of (i).
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): LP-A2 refine KVU-16g-2 / MD-A1 gpu_slot.sh: add a solo-short probe to the collision arm (80k neighbour prefilling, budget on, 64- and 256-token request arriving 2 s in; record its TTFT, the iteration ms at collision, and solo-short TTFT on an idle server). Decision rule: if (collision TTFT - solo TTFT - deque wait) <= one solo short iteration, batch homogeneity adds nothing on :8083 and the lever is closed; if it is >= one budget iteration (target-ms), file an experimental-branch option that gives a newly arrived short prompt its own ubatch before the long slot resumes, priced at one long-prefill iteration per collision.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): LP-A3 refine WP-15 (two-tier arm): when the dispatch log has two windows, score a static length-pool rule (short -> the two half NUMA instances, long -> the full instance; threshold at the prompt-length valley per SRF-A3) beside the token-balanced rule, and report long-request throughput per MD-A2; LAPS spatial mode (pool migration with hysteresis) is knowledge-only because our three instances differ in shape and migrating one is a cpuset change, not a queue flag.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): LP-A4 decline the CUDA/HIP-Graph short-prefill bucketing: :8083 runs np=4 with agentic traffic, never 64-way short prefills, HIP graphs are used for decode only, and each captured graph costs 228-277 MB against the ~2.2 GiB headroom to the 62 GiB VRAM gate (KVU-16h); the paper itself reports graphs alone can degrade throughput and the gain vanishes when prefill and decode share an instance, which is our topology.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): LP-A5 knowledge-only: the orchestrator 16384-token long-prefill lease with bounded short-pass (KVU-15a) is already an orchestrator-level temporal disaggregation (at most one long prefill in flight, shorts pass); note this under KVU-15a so a future engine-side split is scoped as the residual after the lease, not as a new mechanism.

## intake-2223#record — Deadline-Aware Adaptive Prefill Chunking for Efficient Large Language Model Serving

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07883
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty low, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2219#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Deadline derivation: every active decode i gets a next-token deadline d_i = l_i + D, where l_i is the completion time of its most recent token (or its prefill completion) and D is the TPOT objective; the per-iteration budget is B_t = max(0, min_i d_i - s_t), i.e. the earliest remaining slack at scheduler time s_t. Conflicting deadlines are merged by taking the minimum; with an empty active set the scheduler uses an implementation-defined cap C_max (4096 in the simulator).
   - *narrowed*: On llama-server all generating slots decode in one batch per iteration, so l_i is identical across slots and the min collapses to D minus the host gap since the previous iteration; the per-slot form adds nothing over a global --prefill-budget-target-ms unless per-request D_i exist (they do not on our API).
   - anchor `Section 3.1 Deadline model, paper.txt lines 273-277` @ arXiv:2609.07883v1 HTML: "For active request i , let \ell_{i} be the completion time of its most recent token. If the request has just completed prefill, \ell_{i} is the prefill completion time. Given TPOT objective D , its next-token deadline is d_{i}=\ell_{i}+D."
1. Chunk solver: c*_t = max{c <= min(C_max, L_t) : T(|A_t|, c) <= B_t}, found by binary search over c (O(log C_max) cost-model queries) because the only requirement on the iteration-cost model T(n, c) (n = decode batch size, c = prefill tokens) is monotonicity; the model may be a profiled lookup table, a fitted regressor, or an analytical form. The simulator default is linear: T = 0.35 + [n>0](0.90 + 0.055 n) + [c>0](0.40 + 0.006 c) ms, with no term for the already-cached context; the GPU runtime instead uses a profiled P50/P99 table indexed by decode batch size, total cached context and candidate prefill tokens with a cumulative maximum along the chunk axis.
   - *unaffected*: Monotone-model requirement is satisfied by the PB-QCOST-1 quadratic (positive coefficients); binary search is unnecessary because the quadratic inverts in closed form at fixed c.
   - anchor `Section 2.1 Iteration-level LLM serving, cost-model paragraph, paper.txt lines 237-241` @ arXiv:2609.07883v1 HTML: "where T is nondecreasing in both arguments. The function can be a lookup table profiled by the runtime, a fitted regressor, or a conservative analytical model. SLOWeave requires only monotonicity in c , not a particular functional form."
2. When no positive chunk fits the scheduler runs a decode-only iteration; the policy is not starvation-free under overload, so the authors point to admission control (reject when predicted decode-only cost approaches D) and an aging threshold that permits a bounded, explicitly logged deadline violation; one pending prefill is admitted per iteration, oldest first, and multi-prefill admission is sketched as a small knapsack or first-fit.
   - *narrowed*: Our shipped budget has a 64-token floor instead of a zero-chunk decode-only fallback, which is the bounded-violation aging policy the paper describes rather than the strict form it proves.
   - anchor `Section 3.2 Adaptive chunk selection, paragraph after Algorithm 1, paper.txt lines 386-390` @ arXiv:2609.07883v1 HTML: "If no positive chunk fits, the scheduler performs a decode-only iteration. This is not starvation-free under unlimited overload: no work-conserving policy can satisfy arbitrary arrival rates and deadlines. Existing admission control or replica autoscaling can bound overload. In our implementation..."
3. Prediction error is handled by a one-sided margin: test T_hat(n, c) + delta(n, c) <= B_t, with delta from quantile regression or an empirical high-percentile residual table; Proposition 1 (safety + maximality) is stated under an exact monotone predictor and a feasible decode-only iteration, and is claimed to survive a bounded one-sided error.
   - *unaffected*: Directly applicable: PB-QCOST-1 reports rmse 40-65 ms and MAPE but no P99 residual, which is the number the margin needs (SW-A2).
   - anchor `Section 3.3 Safety and maximality, Prediction error paragraph, paper.txt lines 420-424` @ arXiv:2609.07883v1 HTML: "With a learned cost model, the scheduler can reserve a margin \delta(n,c) and test \widehat{T}(n,c)+\delta(n,c)\leq B_{t} . If the one-sided prediction error is bounded by \delta , the proposition continues to hold. Quantile regression or an empirical high-percentile residual table provides a pra..."
4. Heterogeneous SLOs are supported by substituting per-request D_i (and per-class margins delta_i) into d_i = l_i + D_i while keeping the min-over-slots budget; the paper claims this protects a 10 ms interactive stream while a 50 ms batch request absorbs the residual slack, but the Limitations section says the evaluated controller assumes a shared TPOT objective.
   - *narrowed*: Sketched, not evaluated; the Limitations section states the evaluated controller assumes a shared TPOT objective, so the 10 ms / 50 ms protection claim is a design argument without a measurement.
   - anchor `Section 3.5 Heterogeneous SLOs, paper.txt lines 450-455` @ arXiv:2609.07883v1 HTML: "For request-specific objectives D_{i} , the deadline becomes d_{i}=\ell_{i}+D_{i} , and the budget remains \min_{i}(d_{i}-s_{t}) . Priority classes can additionally reserve different prediction margins \delta_{i} . Thus the controller naturally protects a 10 ms interactive stream while using resi..."
5. Simulator headline (dependency-free Python, 1,000 requests per run, five seeds, oldest-first queueing, baselines = full prefill and fixed chunks of 64/256/1,024 tokens, SLOWeave C_max = 4096): at a 25 ms TPOT SLO goodput is 59.0 vs 42.4 req/s on mixed traffic (+39% over Fixed-1024) and 25.4 vs 18.4 req/s on long traffic (+38%); at 10 ms the ratios are 3.35x and 2.45x; at 50 ms on mixed traffic SLOWeave ties full prefill (60.4 vs 60.5). Goodput counts a request only if both its TTFT SLO and its within-request P99 TPOT SLO are met.
   - *narrowed*: Simulator result under a cost model with no cached-context term (T linear in c); our MI210 fit shows per-token cost tripling at an 80k prefix, so the magnitude does not transfer. The direction (adaptive chunk recovers stranded slack) is consistent with the PB-QCOST-1 flat-tail replay.
   - anchor `Section 6.1 Main comparison, second prose paragraph, paper.txt lines 815-820` @ arXiv:2609.07883v1 HTML: "The difference appears under heterogeneous and long prompts. On mixed traffic, SLOWeave reaches 59.0 requests/s of goodput, compared with 42.4 for the strongest fixed baseline, a 39% improvement. Full prefill has lower P99 TTFT than Fixed-1024 but frequently exceeds the token deadline, so only 6...."
6. GPU runtime results (Table 2; 8xA100-80GB and 8xH100-80GB nodes, unnamed 8B dense and 70B TP8 models, five-minute warm-up plus ten-minute window, five repeats): A100/8B mixed goodput 38.4 (runtime default) -> 52.7 (Fixed-1024) -> 67.1 req/s (SLOWeave) with P99 TPOT 24.8 ms; H100/70B-TP8 long 22.1 -> 31.4 -> 40.8 req/s; A100/8B bursty 34.7 -> 44.2 (Fixed-512) -> 50.6 req/s; energy per valid request falls 15.1 -> 12.4 J (A100 mixed). The Sarathi-style tuned-chunking baseline and the Fixed-256/512/2048 arms announced in Section 5.2 do not appear in Table 2 (NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE), and no scheduler-overhead number is reported beyond the O(|A| + log C_max) complexity statement.
   - *narrowed*: Authors' own numbers, unnamed models, vLLM-style runtime unspecified, Sarathi-tuned and the other fixed arms announced in Section 5.2 are absent from Table 2, no artifact URL; no replication found.
   - anchor `Section 5.3 GPU results, Table 2 row A100 / 8B mixed / SLOWeave (P99 TTFT ms, P99 TPOT ms, throughput req/s, SLO percent, goodput req/s, J per valid request), paper.txt lines 630-636` @ arXiv:2609.07883v1 HTML: "SLOWeave 1,190 24.8 73.4 91.4 67.1 12.4"
7. Stated limitations: one shared TPOT objective and one prefill chunk per iteration in the evaluated controller; no preemption cost, no KV transfer, no multi-node; real iteration cost is admitted to be non-smooth (kernel boundaries, TP communication, memory pressure, prefix-cache hits) and calibration drift across model/hardware updates is left open.
   - *unaffected*: Limitations as stated; they are the reasons SW-A4 is a monitor and SW-A6 keeps MD-A1 as the deciding arm.
   - anchor `Limitations, second paragraph, paper.txt lines 983-988` @ arXiv:2609.07883v1 HTML: "The current controller assumes a shared TPOT objective and one prefill chunk per iteration. Multi-tenant priorities require per-request deadlines, which the earliest-deadline formulation supports, but starvation and admission control deserve separate study. We also model no preemption cost and no..."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b wave 3: dived v1 (the only version, 2026-09-07) in full from the arXiv HTML; the 2606.09061 dive had seen it as a snippet only. Three WebSearches found no venue, no citation, no replication, and surfaced Niyama (2503.22562) as uncited prior art for deadline-slack dynamic chunking plus TokenScale (2512.03416), PolyServe (2507.17769) and P-PAS (2608.15171) as adjacent chunk-budget work. Announced GPU baselines missing from Table 2 and the context-free simulator cost model are recorded as contradicting evidence. No intake id assigned; entry not written to research/intake_index.yaml; no handoff edited. Second reader (independent agent, 2026-10-21): re-find 8/8 (block scalars and U+2009 kept), hash 8/8; support yes 7 / partial 1 / no 0 (claim 6 anchors one Table 2 row; the rest verified by the reader); simulator constants and Tables 2-4 numbers exact; no factual mismatch.

### Verdict

SLOWeave is the same fixed-time-budget-with-cost-model mechanism that KVU-16g already ships (--prefill-budget-target-ms) and that PB-QCOST-1 already calibrates with a quadratic, dressed as per-slot deadlines merged by min. In llama-server the min collapses to one global target minus the host gap, so the deadline derivation does NOT differ materially from a fixed target-ms; the two things it adds that we lack are (1) explicit dependence of the cost model on the decode batch size n, which PB-QCOST-1 fitted at a single n (SW-A1), and (2) a one-sided P99 margin instead of a mean fit when choosing the target (SW-A2). Both are text refinements to existing tasks, not new work. Per-request D_i is a monitor (no per-request TPOT on our API), the binary search and profiled grid are unnecessary given the closed-form quadratic, and the paper does nothing for the short-under-long prefill collision (no-decoder case is unbounded, one prefill per iteration), so MD-A1 stays the deciding measurement. Do not transfer the 39 percent / 3.3x headlines: they come from a simulator whose chunk cost ignores prefix length.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `gpu-serving-tie-in-program.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SW-A1 refine PB-QCOST-1 text (gpu-serving-tie-in-program.md:335): the fit t = 324 + 0.987 q + 2.41e-5 q c ms was taken at one decode-batch point (gen=3 slots, decode_rows=24 with DFlash2 draft rows), so the 324 ms intercept and the q coefficient carry no n dependence; SLOWeave's cost model is T(n, c) nondecreasing in n. Add: refit or at least re-validate at n = 1 and n = 4 generating slots (decode_rows 8 and 32) before the budget is enabled on the np4 :8083 shape, and report the per-n intercept with the noise floor and its unit.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SW-A2 refine KVU-16g-2 text (kv-unified-stack-rollout.md:1114): choose target-ms = (decode TPOT target D) minus a ONE-SIDED margin equal to the held-out P99 absolute residual of the PB-QCOST-1 fit, not from the mean fit; PB-QCOST-1's REPORT.md currently gives rmse 40-65 ms and MAPE only, so the P99 residual must be added to its offline output (a number, not a task).
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SW-A3 knowledge-only: in llama-server every generating slot advances in the same batch per iteration, so all l_i are equal and SLOWeave's min_i(l_i + D - s_t) collapses to D minus the host gap since the last iteration ended; the per-slot form only differs from one global target-ms by that host gap (GPU-ZOS-1 measures it) and by per-request D_i (SW-A4). No new task.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SW-A4 monitor: the per-request D_i form (Section 3.5) is the design reference if a shared server ever hosts two roles with different TPOT classes (interactive vs batch); trigger = a role->TPOT-class mapping becomes a request parameter on :8083 or any shared_with server. Until then decline: our API carries no per-request TPOT and the paper did not evaluate the heterogeneous case.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SW-A5 decline: the binary search over a profiled cumulative-max P50/P99 grid; the PB-QCOST-1 quadratic inverts in closed form (q = (target - 324) / (0.987 + 2.41e-5 c)) at MAPE 4.0-7.8 percent, and a start-up profiling grid costs GPU time with no shown accuracy gain.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SW-A6 knowledge-only, corroborates MD-A1 (Medha dive): SLOWeave also leaves the no-decoder case unbounded (C_max = 4096, full chunk when A is empty) and admits one prefill per iteration oldest-first, so it offers nothing for the short-prefill-under-long-prefill collision; MD-A1's arm remains the deciding measurement and the DI-126 trigger text stands as refined by MD-A3.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): SW-A7 decline: admission controller and aging threshold (Section 3.4); the existing floor of 64 prompt tokens per iteration in --prefill-budget-target-ms already bounds prefill starvation as a bounded-violation policy, and the API has no reject/redirect path to drive.

These traces had already been filtered, but the filtering was reportedly simple: putting the whole trace into a prompt and asking the model."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 STAGE-2b (DP-F wave 1, group g1): source read FULL from disk (post_en.md, sha256 e4261f823da4c930ef42b9c1197dcb6daff900bd55572366bb727a29518755e2). Claim verdicts: claim 0 unaffected; claim 1 unaffected; claim 2 unaffected; claim 3 narrowed; claim 4 narrowed.

### Verdict

Cite the file-system-environment and proposer/filter decomposition as design precedents only. Never cite the QC F1 numbers as evidence the debugger works: the QC rows are self-reported, merged-label, tuned on the evaluated model, and lose to the baseline on SWE-Bench with gemini-3-pro. Never cite 68/500 as a miss rate.

### Claims (as verified)

0. Spec frontmatter allows exactly name (<=64, lowercase/digits/hyphens, matches parent directory), description (<=1024), and optional license, compatibility (<=500), metadata (string-to-string map) and experimental allowed-tools; the body has no format restriction.
   - anchor `specification.md frontmatter table` @ agentskills.io specification.md sha256 9a67de89 fetched 2026-10-21: "| `metadata` | No | Arbitrary key-value mapping for additional metadata (a map from string keys to string values). |"
   - anchor `specification.md frontmatter table` @ agentskills.io specification.md sha256 9a67de89 fetched 2026-10-21: "| `name` | Yes | Max 64 characters. Lowercase letters, numbers, and hyphens only. Must not start or end with a hyphen. |"
   - anchor `specification.md name field` @ agentskills.io specification.md sha256 9a67de89 fetched 2026-10-21: "Must match the parent directory name"
1. EPYC B3 (src/skill_hub_interop.py @5dde3006) is not spec-conformant: export emits top-level version, author, tags and trust_level, its docstring uses name "My Skill", and its single-line regex parser cannot read a nested metadata map. B3 has no callers.
   - anchor `src/skill_hub_interop.py:L150-L151 @5dde3006` @ epyc-orchestrator origin/main 5dde3006: "lines.append(f"tags: {', '.join(meta.tags)}") lines.append(f"trust_level: {meta.trust_level}")"
   - anchor `src/skill_hub_interop.py:L25 @5dde3006` @ epyc-orchestrator origin/main 5dde3006: "_YAML_KV_RE = re.compile(r"^(\w[\w_-]*)\s*:\s*(.+)$", re.M)"
   - anchor `src/skill_hub_interop.py:L85 @5dde3006` @ epyc-orchestrator origin/main 5dde3006: "name: My Skill"
2. Progressive disclosure: ~100-token catalog at start, <5000-token instructions on activation, resources on demand; the guide recommends model-judged activation and protecting skill content from context compaction.
   - anchor `specification.md Progressive disclosure` @ agentskills.io specification.md sha256 9a67de89 fetched 2026-10-21: "1. **Metadata** (\~100 tokens): The `name` and `description` fields are loaded at startup for all skills"
3. Evaluation guidance: run each case with and without the skill, aggregate pass-rate deltas, drop assertions that pass in both arms; stddev is only meaningful with multiple runs. No paired test, no replicate floor.
   - anchor `evaluating-skills.md Analyzing patterns` @ evaluating-skills.md sha256 5bec4f1b: "**Remove or replace assertions that always pass in both configurations.**"
   - anchor `evaluating-skills.md Aggregating results note` @ evaluating-skills.md sha256 5bec4f1b: "Standard deviation (`stddev`) is only meaningful with multiple runs per eval."
4. Description optimization: run each query 3 times, trigger rate threshold 0.5, 60/40 train/validation split, pick the iteration by validation pass rate.
   - anchor `optimizing-descriptions.md Running multiple times` @ optimizing-descriptions.md sha256 49c74ac1: "Run each query multiple times (3 is a reasonable starting point) and compute a **trigger rate**"
   - anchor `optimizing-descriptions.md train/validation` @ optimizing-descriptions.md sha256 49c74ac1: "**Validation set (\~40%)**"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 (dpfw1-g4): intake-117#record surfaced item "confirm frontmatter fields once" DONE; result: B3 deviates (see claim 1).

### Verdict

The spec constrains frontmatter to six fields; anything else (version, author, tags, trust_level) belongs in the metadata string map. Treat B3 export output as non-conformant until fixed. The evaluation guide is a minimum practice, not a statistical protocol.

## intake-2227#record — Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows

- **URL**: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/workflows
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: agent_architecture, context_management, cost_aware_routing)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. A workflow moves the plan into code: with subagents, skills and agent teams Claude orchestrates turn by turn and every result goes into a context window, whereas a workflow script holds the loop, branching and intermediate results, so Claude's context holds only the final answer.
   - *unaffected*: Documented design; the comparison table in the page matches. Documentation of a product, not an empirical claim.
   - anchor `§When to use a workflow` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "A workflow script holds the loop, the branching, and the intermediate results itself, so Claude’s context holds only the final answer."
1. Resume is replay by agent start order: Date.now(), Math.random() and no-argument new Date() throw inside the script so a relaunch repeats the same agent() calls; the first agent whose prompt differs, and every agent after it, runs again; a failed agent also re-runs every later agent.
   - *unaffected*: Read in full; the replay rule is documented behavior. 'Even ones that completed' rerun is the cost of order-based replay and the page says so.
   - anchor `§Edit a saved script` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "Claude Code makes Date.now(), Math.random(), and a no-argument new Date() throw inside the script, so that a relaunched run repeats the same agent() calls."
   - anchor `§Resume after a pause` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "The first agent whose prompt differs from the previous run, because you edited the script or an earlier agent returned something different, runs again, and so does every agent after it, even ones that completed."
2. Quality patterns are first-class: independent agents adversarially review each other's findings, claims are voted on, and claims the verifier could not check (rate limit, API error) are listed as unverified instead of counted as refuted.
   - *unaffected*: Documented behavior of the bundled /deep-research workflow; no precision or accuracy of the voting is reported, so the benefit of adversarial review is asserted, not measured.
   - anchor `§Run a bundled workflow` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "When the verifier agents can’t check a claim, such as after a rate limit or API error, the report lists that claim as unverified instead of counting it as refuted."
   - anchor `§Bundled workflows` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "votes on each claim, and returns a cited report with claims that didn’t survive cross-checking filtered out"
   - anchor `§When to use a workflow` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "it can have independent agents adversarially review each other’s findings before they’re reported"
3. Prompt-cache-aware fan-out: agents with the same model, effort, agent type, tools, schema and working directory share a prefix, so the runtime holds all but the first matching agent until the first's response begins (cap CLAUDE_CODE_WORKFLOW_PREFIX_STAGGER_MS, default 5000), so siblings read the cached prefix instead of processing it uncached.
   - *narrowed*: Documented mechanism for Anthropic API prompt caching (5 min / 1 h TTL). Transfer to llama-server slots is a hypothesis: whether same-prefix concurrent requests on distinct slots each prefill uncached, and whether a hold-first stagger recovers prefill, has NOT been measured here.
   - anchor `§Prompt caching in a fan-out` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "Claude Code holds all but the first until the first agent’s response begins, then releases the held agents together so their first requests read the shared prefix instead of each processing it uncached."
4. Structured subagent output: agent() with a schema returns JSON validated before and after the run; a self-contradictory schema fails before any subagent starts; output failing validation after five attempts fails the call with the last validation error (MAX_STRUCTURED_OUTPUT_RETRIES).
   - *unaffected*: Documented behavior; analogous in shape to the schema-validated retries already in the orchestrator parallel_dispatch contract (K4 in the plan).
   - anchor `§What the saved script looks like` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "If the subagent’s output still fails validation after five attempts, the call fails with an error that includes the last validation failure."
   - anchor `§What the saved script looks like` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "when it can prove the schema contradicts itself, the call fails with an error naming the contradiction, and the subagent never starts."
5. Runtime bounds: 16 concurrent agents by default (fewer when CPUs are fewer), up to 4,096 items per parallel()/pipeline(), 1,000 agents per run; a Large-workflow warning fires above 25 agents or 1.5M projected tokens; stalled agents auto-restart up to five times; size guidelines small <5, medium <10, large <50 agents.
   - *unaffected*: Limits are version-dependent defaults (several flagged 'requires v2.1.2xx'); do not treat as constants.
   - anchor `§Behavior and limits` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "Up to 4,096 items in a single parallel() or pipeline() call"
   - anchor `§Behavior and limits` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "1,000 agents total per runPrevents runaway loops"
   - anchor `§Cost` @ Claude Code Docs page fetched 2026-10-21 (version-bearing text mentions Claude Code v2.1.219-v2.1.305), p.html sha256 5a71f2a8c4f8ead9f3fe8f4caec94de3f11af674df7315179fb30822c76f2117, text p.txt extracted from it; HTML <title> 'Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows - Claude Code Docs': "When a workflow schedules more than 25 agents, or its projected token total passes 1.5 million, its progress line in the task panel below the input box shows a Large workflow warning."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 STAGE-2b (DP-F wave 1, group g1): source read FULL from disk (p.txt, sha256 3860b0907a7c0e1ecd6cea9466b0ad133c638ca6390d4ff129b4cd360f318f29). Claim verdicts: claim 0 unaffected; claim 1 unaffected; claim 2 unaffected; claim 3 narrowed; claim 4 unaffected; claim 5 unaffected.

### Verdict

Cite for mechanisms (plan-in-code, order-based replay with nondeterminism banned, unverified-not-refuted, held-first prefix stagger, schema-validated subagent output). Never cite any default or limit as a constant: they are version-dependent. Never claim the stagger helps llama.cpp slots: untested.
