Premise to verify at apply: retrieval_count is populated in the backup.

## intake-2252#record — AgenTracer: Who Is Inducing Failure in the LLM Agentic Systems?

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03312
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance low; categories: agent_architecture, benchmark_methodology, autonomous_research)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1132#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Failure attribution is defined as finding the decisive error: the earliest (agent, step) whose correction (by an oracle) turns a failed trajectory into a success.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "Pinpointing the specific agent or step responsible for an error within long execution traces defines the task of agentic system failure attribution."
   - anchor `§3 Objective Formulation` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "we formalize this pivotal error as the decisive error, i.e., the earliest action in the trajectory whose correction is sufficient to steer the system from failure to success."
1. AgenTracer labels trajectories two ways: counterfactual replay for failed runs (a DeepSeek-R1 analyzer, given the trajectory, environment feedback and the ground-truth solution, proposes a minimal correction at each step and the run is replayed) and programmatic fault injection into successful runs (the decisive step is known by construction). Together: TracerTraj, over 2,000 annotated trajectories from six multi-agent systems and six benchmarks.
   - anchor `§4.1 Locating Decisive Errors` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "the analyzer proposes a minimally invasive, corrected action"
   - anchor `§5.1 Model & Parameter Configuration` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "The analyzer agent πanalyzer in Equation 7 and perturbation operator Π in Equation 9 are both based on DeepSeek-R1"
   - anchor `§4.1 Utilizing Successful Trajectories` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "Its core principle is to take a known-good trajectory and programmatically introduce a fault, thereby creating a synthetic failure instance where the decisive error is known by construction."
2. AgenTracer-8B (Qwen3-8B, GRPO with a gated step/agent reward) is reported to beat larger models on Who&When: up to +12.21% over DeepSeek-R1 and +18.18% over Gemini-2.5-Pro (abstract/introduction).
   - anchor `Table 1 (columns: handcraft agent / step, automated agent / step; each cell w/ G / w/o G), row AgenTracer` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "AgenTracer | 69.10/63.82 | 20.68/20.68 | 69.62/63.73 | 42.86/37.30"
   - anchor `Table 1, row Claude-Sonnet-4` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "Claude-Sonnet-4 | 56.90/50.00 | 17.24/18.97 | 57.93/51.11 | 40.65/38.83"
   - anchor `Table 1, row Gemini-2.5-pro` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "Gemini-2.5-pro | 51.72/51.72 | 9.72/6.90 | 61.11/57.14 | 29.52/25.86"
   - anchor `§1 Introduction, contribution 3` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "outperforming giant LLMs like DeepSeek-R1 by ∼12.21% and Gemini-2.5-Pro by ∼18.18% on Who&When benchmark"
3. Who&When (127 trajectories in total across its handcrafted and automated subsets) and MAST (200) are the only prior annotated sets, so Who&When cells rest on small denominators.
   - anchor `§1 Introduction` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "MAST (Cemri et al., 2025) and Who&When (Zhang et al., 2025c) contain only 200 and 127 manually annotated multi-agent failure trajectories, respectively"
4. Giving the attributor the ground-truth solution does not reliably help (Claude-Sonnet-4 on TracerTraj-math 46.03 with vs 50.79 without).
   - anchor `Table 2 (TracerTraj; columns: Code agent / step, MATH agent / step, Agentic agent / step), row Claude-Sonnet-4 (MATH agent-level 46.03 w/ G vs 50.79 w/o G)` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "Claude-Sonnet-4 | 65.98/63.78 | 15.51/11.02 | 46.03/50.79 | 38.10/46.03 | 55.20/49.13 | 30.33/29.80"
   - anchor `§5.2 Observation 1` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "Notably, providing 𝒢 does not consistently improve attribution accuracy"
5. Feeding AgenTracer-8B feedback to MetaGPT, OWL and MaAS raises task accuracy by 4.8 to 14.2 points over three rounds while CRITIC (GPT-4.1) degrades it (single-run figures, N not stated).
   - anchor `§5.3 Observation 3` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "Even when powered by GPT-4.1, CRITIC consistently degrades performance"
   - anchor `§5.3 Observation 3` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "On MaAS+MATH-500, the gains are even more striking, reaching +14.21%"
6. TracerTraj-2.5K statistics: 1288 coding, 630 math, 558 agentic annotated trajectories.
   - anchor `Appendix A Table 3` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "Curated Trajectories | 2,170 | 1,185 | 1,300 | TracerTraj-2.5K | 1288 | 630 | 558"
7. Release status: the repo ships the data pipeline and a released data version that differs from the paper; AgenTracer-8B weights are withheld.
   - anchor `README.md, header note` @ github.com/bingreeky/AgenTracer @ 256b19e53e8c7341822e5925c481fb942f606ed8 (commit 2026-08-27), README.md sha256 ce57b13fb422e090ec0e3a646ab93cb47d5d0e837d5f643d36c609d368254c13: "Due to internal considerations, we currently have no plans to release the AgenTracer-8B model weights."
   - anchor `README.md, header note` @ github.com/bingreeky/AgenTracer @ 256b19e53e8c7341822e5925c481fb942f606ed8 (commit 2026-08-27), README.md sha256 ce57b13fb422e090ec0e3a646ab93cb47d5d0e837d5f643d36c609d368254c13: "the released data is not exactly identical to the version reported in the paper and contains additional samples."
8. On TracerTraj-agentic (Table 2, a different benchmark from Who&When) Claude-Sonnet-4 beats AgenTracer-8B at agent level with ground truth (55.20 vs 53.28), so the claim of consistent superiority over Claude does not hold on every cell.
   - anchor `Table 2 (TracerTraj), row Claude-Sonnet-4 (Agentic agent-level 55.20 w/ G)` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "Claude-Sonnet-4 | 65.98/63.78 | 15.51/11.02 | 46.03/50.79 | 38.10/46.03 | 55.20/49.13 | 30.33/29.80"
   - anchor `Table 2 (TracerTraj), row AgenTracer (Agentic agent-level 53.28 w/ G)` @ arXiv:2509.03312v2 (04 Sep 2025); agentracer_v2.html sha256 cb1c1a1d1b210960b7f8de044e013dc2a4e2f9bebee6aa0290aae46270367a12, text agentracer_v2.txt extracted from it: "AgenTracer | 72.95/72.21 | 18.85/18.85 | 59.32/66.10 | 57.63/57.63 | 53.28/50.61 | 36.17/35.55"

### Verdict

Take the decisive-error definition and the fault-injection idea; do not cite the +18.18% or "outperforms Claude" as stated, and do not plan on AgenTracer-8B or TracerTraj as a gold set (weights withheld, labels LLM-generated and unaudited, released data differs from the paper).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `harness-improvement-loop.md`

## intake-2253#record — agentskills/agentskills: Agent Skills specification and skills-ref reference validator

- **URL**: https://github.com/agentskills/agentskills
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty low, relevance medium; categories: agent_architecture, tool_implementation)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-117#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. SETTLED: the skills-ref validator REJECTS unknown top-level frontmatter keys (ALLOWED_FIELDS = name, description, license, allowed-tools, metadata, compatibility) and a test asserts it. EPYC export_skill_md emits version, author, tags and trust_level as top-level keys on every export, so every exported file fails skills-ref validate; AG-01 is confirmed as a hard (not advisory) deviation.
   - anchor `dive-dpfw2-agentskills/repo/skills-ref/src/skills_ref/validator.py:L108` @ agentskills @69ef37e9424c0a7ea9dd2293b559e43ec8176379 / epyc-orchestrator @5dde30061fe461d96760888197a207a02086754a: "extra_fields = set(metadata.keys()) - ALLOWED_FIELDS"

1. Name rules: lowercase letters/digits/hyphens, <=64, no leading/trailing/consecutive hyphens, and the name must equal the parent directory. The EPYC fixtures and docstring use name "My Skill" / "Code Reviewer" (uppercase, space), which fail on three counts; EPYC never checks directory name equality.
   - anchor `dive-dpfw2-agentskills/repo/skills-ref/src/skills_ref/validator.py:L62` @ agentskills @69ef37e9424c0a7ea9dd2293b559e43ec8176379 / epyc-orchestrator @5dde30061fe461d96760888197a207a02086754a: "if dir_name != name:"
   - anchor `dive-dpfw2-agentskills/repo/skills-ref/src/skills_ref/validator.py:L51` @ agentskills @69ef37e9424c0a7ea9dd2293b559e43ec8176379 / epyc-orchestrator @5dde30061fe461d96760888197a207a02086754a: "if "--" in name:"
   - anchor `dive-dpfw2-agentskills/repo/docs/specification.mdx:L64` @ agentskills @69ef37e9424c0a7ea9dd2293b559e43ec8176379 / epyc-orchestrator @5dde30061fe461d96760888197a207a02086754a: "Must not contain consecutive hyphens (`--`)"
2. EPYC parser is a line regex, not YAML: by inspection the `\s*` after the colon can span a newline, so a spec-valid nested `metadata:` block parses as key metadata with value of its first child line, later child lines are dropped (they start with whitespace), and re-export writes `metadata: key: value` which is not valid YAML. Block scalars (`description: >`) likewise lose their content. Inferred from the regex, not executed.
   - anchor `dive-dpfw2-agentskills/repo/skills-ref/src/skills_ref/parser.py:L61` @ agentskills @69ef37e9424c0a7ea9dd2293b559e43ec8176379 / epyc-orchestrator @5dde30061fe461d96760888197a207a02086754a: "if "metadata" in metadata and isinstance(metadata["metadata"], dict):"
   - anchor `/workspace/repos/epyc-orchestrator/src/skill_hub_interop.py:L25` @ agentskills @69ef37e9424c0a7ea9dd2293b559e43ec8176379 / epyc-orchestrator @5dde30061fe461d96760888197a207a02086754a: "_YAML_KV_RE = re.compile(r"^(\w[\w_-]*)\s*:\s*(.+)$", re.M)"
   - anchor `/workspace/repos/epyc-orchestrator/src/skill_hub_interop.py:L110` @ agentskills @69ef37e9424c0a7ea9dd2293b559e43ec8176379 / epyc-orchestrator @5dde30061fe461d96760888197a207a02086754a: "kv_pairs = dict(_YAML_KV_RE.findall(frontmatter))"
3. [Withdrawn at Stage 4 2026-10-21: no supporting quote is anchored for this claim, so it is not citable; original text kept in notes.]
   - *narrowed*: Withdrawn at Stage 4: unanchored in the second-reader pass; original text in notes, not citable.
4. skills-ref README states it is for demonstration only and not for production; the spec itself does not say unknown keys are an error, so the allow-list is the reference implementation reading, not a normative MUST in the spec text read.
   - anchor `dive-dpfw2-agentskills/repo/skills-ref/README.md:L6` @ agentskills @69ef37e9424c0a7ea9dd2293b559e43ec8176379 / epyc-orchestrator @5dde30061fe461d96760888197a207a02086754a: "This library is intended for demonstration purposes only. It is not meant to be used in production."

### Verdict

AG-01 stands and is stronger than wave-1 framed it: exports fail the reference validator on four counts (unknown keys, non-slug name, directory mismatch, no nested metadata). It is latent because nothing in src calls the module. Fix is the existing AG-01 task; no caller changes needed.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `harness-selection-and-integration.md`
- updated `episodic-memory-integrity.md`

### Claims (as verified)

0. Oh-My-Pi 'works' but did not impress; the author found it too verbose out of the box and more of a power-user lab than a cohesive product; OpenCode and Oh-My-Pi performed about the same with the same model on one PR review, and neither Claude Code nor the others found everything.

URL located from the author's own tag listing https://akitaonrails.com/en/tags/reviews/ (captured search result then curl).: "My first impression of Oh-My-Pi is that it works. It is not bad. It also did not make me say, “how did I live without this?”"
   - anchor `So where does Pi fit?` @ https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/05/25/first-impressions-using-oh-my-pi-and-opencode/ curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, page.html sha256 b21a7a909d42100b5a2226266ab324a788ed878d8b5b02a6dd0f866125d30dcf; p2.txt (tag-stripped non-blank lines) sha256 0ddff48be27d1ad2c38fb0ba5edecff39d543192d77ddab83be24fda680be928; post dated May 25, 2026. URL located from the author's own tag listing https://akitaonrails.com/en/tags/reviews/ (captured search result then curl).: "Out of the box I found it too verbose."
1. From reading the source: Oh-My-Pi ships many more tools than upstream Pi or OpenCode (structural-summary reads, hash-anchored edits, AST search/edit, a universal read tool that understands SQLite, archives, notebooks and documents), with a system prompt that insists on the specialized tool; the advantage over OpenCode is large for non-source artifacts and small for ordinary source code.
   - anchor `What I checked in the code` @ https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/05/25/first-impressions-using-oh-my-pi-and-opencode/ curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, page.html sha256 b21a7a909d42100b5a2226266ab324a788ed878d8b5b02a6dd0f866125d30dcf; p2.txt (tag-stripped non-blank lines) sha256 0ddff48be27d1ad2c38fb0ba5edecff39d543192d77ddab83be24fda680be928; post dated May 25, 2026. 2. The same model (GPT 5.5) in two harnesses found different PR defects, so a single reviewer pass is not an audit.
   - anchor `Is Pi better than OpenCode?` @ https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/05/25/first-impressions-using-oh-my-pi-and-opencode/ curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, page.html sha256 b21a7a909d42100b5a2226266ab324a788ed878d8b5b02a6dd0f866125d30dcf; p2.txt (tag-stripped non-blank lines) sha256 0ddff48be27d1ad2c38fb0ba5edecff39d543192d77ddab83be24fda680be928; post dated May 25, 2026.    - anchor `Is Pi better than OpenCode?` @ https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/05/25/first-impressions-using-oh-my-pi-and-opencode/ curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, page.html sha256 b21a7a909d42100b5a2226266ab324a788ed878d8b5b02a6dd0f866125d30dcf; p2.txt (tag-stripped non-blank lines) sha256 0ddff48be27d1ad2c38fb0ba5edecff39d543192d77ddab83be24fda680be928; post dated May 25, 2026. 3. More subagents does not mean more parallelizable work: in the author's separate planner-plus-cheap-executor benchmark, coordination overhead consumed the gain and Tier A models did not delegate unless forced.

   - anchor `Is Pi better than OpenCode?` @ https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/05/25/first-impressions-using-oh-my-pi-and-opencode/ curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, page.html sha256 b21a7a909d42100b5a2226266ab324a788ed878d8b5b02a6dd0f866125d30dcf; p2.txt (tag-stripped non-blank lines) sha256 0ddff48be27d1ad2c38fb0ba5edecff39d543192d77ddab83be24fda680be928; post dated May 25, 2026. URL located from the author's own tag listing https://akitaonrails.com/en/tags/reviews/ (captured search result then curl).: "Local open source models came in Tier C or worse."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 (dpfw2-w2h): narrows the wave-1 characterization (dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp DIVE.md, surfaced-source bullet) that this review reaches 'the same OMP does more work than necessary conclusion' and corroborates OMP token appetite. The post makes a subjective 'too verbose / tries too hard' remark and one self-declared non-proof anecdote; it has no token measurements.

### Verdict

dive-verified, read FULL from disk. Cite for the qualitative harness contrast and the multi-harness-reviewer-union observation. Do NOT cite it as evidence that Oh-My-Pi uses more tokens (unmeasured; one anecdote) or as evidence about local-model coding quality (author's tier benchmark, consumer GPUs, no EPYC-class hardware).
worth_investigating only to dive the surfaced mixing-2-models benchmark; knowledge_only here.

## intake-2255#record — Archon: An Architecture Search Framework for Inference-Time Techniques

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.15254
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: agent_architecture, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Search space is six discrete axes (generators, samples, fusion layers, fusers, critic+ranker, verifier/unit-test) giving 9,576 valid configs; objective is dev-accuracy minus lambda*cost on a 20% dev split.
   - anchor `§3.3` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "In total, our search space contains 9,576 configurations"
   - anchor `§3.3` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "We use 20% of each target dataset as a development set for guiding architecture search."
1. Bayesian optimization (GP, EI, 230-240 random initial configs) finds the best architecture in 96.0% of searches with 88.5% fewer evaluations than greedy and 90.4% fewer than random; it is not better than greedy below 20 calls.
   - anchor `§3.3` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "Bayesian optimization found the best architectures in 96.0% of searches and required 88.5% fewer architecture evaluations than greedy search and 90.4% fewer than random search"
   - anchor `§3.3` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "For limited inference call budgets (<20 calls), Bayesian optimization is less effective, and traditional methods like greedy search may perform comparably"
   - anchor `A.4.2` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "For Archon’s implementation, we initialize with 230-240 random configurations"
2. Hard compute restrictions (inference calls, input tokens, output tokens) prune the space before search, enabling compute-matched comparison with MoA/ADAS/AFlow.
   - anchor `§3.3` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "we exclude any Archon architecture that would exceed the inference call, input token, or output token budgets from the search space."
3. Reported 15.1% average gain over frontier single models is on the held-out 80% split; the full-benchmark delta is 0.44% on average, so the dev/held-out leak is small on the same benchmarks the search targeted.
   - anchor `§4.1` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "Since we perform automatic architecture search on a randomly sampled 20% subset of each benchmark, we evaluate on the remaining held-out 80% subset of the benchmark"
4. Techniques are not equally useful: 7B components rank adequately but critique/fuse poorly; coding gains need unit tests and many samples; the framework costs up to 5x time and money of one call.
   - anchor `§4.4` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "The Archon framework is most effective when utilizing LLMs with 70B+ parameters."
   - anchor `§4.4` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "7B models work well for ranking but are less effective for critique and fusion."
   - anchor `§4.4` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "it can take 5x more time and money than a single LLM API call"
5. Fixed layer grammar (generators first, one component type per layer, critic before ranker/fuser) bounds the space to feed-forward ensembles of text-to-text modules.
   - anchor `§3.2` @ arXiv:2409.15254v6: "Only one type of component is allowed in any given layer."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 dive: see DIVE.md; notes record narrowed headline claims.

### Verdict

Archon is a budget-constrained discrete architecture search over an ensemble/fusion grammar. For us it is a comparison point for AutoPilot/PromptForge and HIL (what a search over inference-time modules looks like, and its eval-count cost), not evidence that ensembles pay off on CPU-decode-bound local roles.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `repl-turn-efficiency.md`
- updated `delegation-context-preassembly.md`

## intake-2256#record — AutoLab: Can Frontier Models Solve Long-Horizon Auto Research and Engineering Tasks?

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05080
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: autonomous_research, benchmark_methodology, harness_optimization)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-1457#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. AutoLab is 36 expert-curated long-horizon optimisation tasks in four domains (15 system optimisation, 10 puzzle and challenge, 7 model development, 4 CUDA), each starting from a correct but suboptimal baseline under a 2-12 hour wall-clock budget.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2606.05080v1: "AutoLab consists of 36 realistic, expert-curated tasks spanning four diverse domains: system optimization, puzzle & challenge, model development, and CUDA kernel optimization."
   - anchor `§2.1 Wall-clock budget` @ arXiv:2606.05080v1: "Wall-clock budgets range from 2 hours for the smallest puzzle tasks to 12 hours for end-to-end LLM development tasks."
1. Scoring is anchored: log-stretch for performance metrics (0 at baseline, 0.5 at the human reference, ->1 beyond) and linear for bounded quality metrics, with a minimum-improvement gate; three rollouts per (model, task), reported as Avg@3, Best@3 and head-to-head dominance.
   - anchor `§2.1 Scoring` @ arXiv:2606.05080v1: "A minimum-improvement gate ensures that"
2. [Withdrawn at Stage 4 2026-10-21: no supporting quote is anchored for this claim, so it is not citable; original text kept in notes.]
   - *narrowed*: Withdrawn at Stage 4: unanchored in the second-reader pass; original text in notes, not citable.
3. Models fail by mis-calibrated time use: some stop after minimal exploration (gpt-5.4, grok-4-20) while others exhaust the budget without submitting (deepseek-v4-pro, hunyuan-3-preview, qwen-3.6-plus); 302 zero-score rollouts were hand-classified into four failure modes.
   - anchor `§4.2` @ arXiv:2606.05080v1: "Models struggle to calibrate exploration with the remaining time budget."
4. Harness choice moves scores as much as model choice: the same model shifts by up to 0.43 across harnesses (kimi-k2.6 0.21 to 0.64), rankings are non-transitive, and per-trial inference cost varies more than 5x across harnesses.
   - anchor `§4.3` @ arXiv:2606.05080v1: "Figure 7 reveals that harness-induced variance can be comparable to model-induced variance."
   - anchor `§4.3` @ arXiv:2606.05080v1: "Harness choice is also a spending choice."
5. Anti-hacking design: sealed held-out verifier, correctness gate on disjoint inputs, adversarial agent at construction, SHA-pinned immutable files (unauthorised change scores zero), trajectory-driven verifier patches.
   - anchor `§2.2 Anti-Reward Hacking` @ arXiv:2606.05080v1: "First, the verifier is sealed: the agent is given access to a local evaluation script, but not to the held-out test inputs or reference outputs used for final scoring."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b (dpfw2-w2d): id resolved via arXiv abs page (title matches, submitted 3 Jun 2026, v1). Dedup against research/intake_index.yaml on origin/main (id, URL, title; unbounded): no own row; the ONLY mention is inside intake-1457#record ("Efficiency Matters in Autonomous Research"), dive_corrections line 170042 of the index: "Third-round surfaces (recommend decline, operator-pending): AutoLab 2606.05080 + repo, Toledo 2507.02554, AREK repo itself". Intake-1457 uses 12 of AutoLab's 15 system-optimisation tasks as its testbed. Also named by the EdgeBench dive (wave-1 g6c) as closest prior work.

- updated `harness-improvement-loop.md`

## intake-2257#record — Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes

- **URL**: https://www.anthropic.com/research/building-c-compiler
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty low, relevance low; categories: agent_architecture, autonomous_research, swarm_techniques)
- **Disposition**: knowledge_only
- **Expanded from**: intake-1132#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. VENDOR CLAIM: 16 parallel Claude agents, nearly 2,000 Claude Code sessions over about two weeks and just under $20,000, produced a 100,000-line Rust C compiler that builds Linux 6.9 on x86, ARM and RISC-V (clean-room, no internet).
   - anchor `Intro` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "Over nearly 2,000 Claude Code sessions and $20,000 in API costs, the agent team produced a 100,000-line compiler that can build Linux 6.9 on x86, ARM, and RISC-V."
1. Harness: an endless shell loop restarts a fresh Claude Code session per task; each agent runs in its own Docker container against a shared bare git repo; agents claim work by committing a lock file under current_tasks/; no orchestration agent and no inter-agent channel beyond git.
   - anchor `Running Claude in parallel` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "A new bare git repo is created, and for each agent, a Docker container is spun up with the repo mounted to /upstream."
   - anchor `Running Claude in parallel` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "Claude takes a "lock" on a task by writing a text file to current_tasks/"
   - anchor `Running Claude in parallel` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "I don’t use an orchestration agent."
2. Most author effort went into the task verifier (tests and CI), because the agent will solve whatever the verifier measures.
   - anchor `Lessons > Write extremely high-quality tests` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "the task verifier is nearly perfect, otherwise Claude will solve the wrong problem"
3. Test output is designed for a model reader: few lines, errors as single ERROR lines for grep, precomputed aggregates, and a deterministic-per-agent random subsample (--fast).
   - anchor `Lessons > Put yourself in Claude’s shoes (context window pollution)` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "At most, it should print a few lines of output and log all important information to a file so Claude can find it when needed."
   - anchor `Lessons > Put yourself in Claude’s shoes` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "Claude should write ERROR and put the reason on the same line so grep will find it."
   - anchor `Lessons > Put yourself in Claude’s shoes (time blindness)` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "This subsample is deterministic per-agent but random across VMs, so Claude still covers all files but each agent can perfectly identify regressions."
4. A known-good reference (GCC) used as an online oracle, compiling a random subset of kernel files with the candidate and the rest with GCC, turned one monolithic task into parallel per-file bug hunts.
   - anchor `Lessons > Make parallelism easy` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "The fix was to use GCC as an online known-good compiler oracle to compare against."
5. Cost data: 2 billion input and 140 million output tokens (about 14:1).
   - anchor `Evaluation` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "Opus 4.6 consumed 2 billion input tokens and generated 140 million output tokens, a total cost just under $20,000."
6. Disclosed failure modes: new features frequently broke existing functionality (a CI gate was added), the agent called GCC to cover a phase it could not implement, and tests passing is not completion.
   - anchor `Evaluation, limitations` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "New features and bugfixes frequently broke existing functionality."
   - anchor `Evaluation, limitations` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "Instead, Claude simply cheats here and calls out to GCC for this phase"
   - anchor `Looking forward` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "it is easy to see tests pass and assume the job is done, when this is rarely the case."
7. The post compares model generations (Opus 4 barely functional, 4.5 passes suites, 4.6 builds Linux) with the project held as the benchmark; it does not compare harness variants.
   - anchor `Stress testing the limits of agent teams` @ Anthropic Engineering post "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes", published 2026-02-05 (Nicholas Carlini); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (redirects to /research/building-c-compiler), post.html sha256 e86c9897981b9b0cfe95bb644be7bf61f3fbc5d8c5a3218632f2ccdb2bea23de, text post.txt extracted from it: "Opus 4.5 was the first to cross a threshold that allowed it to produce a functional compiler which could pass large test suites, but it was still incapable of compiling any real large projects."

### Verdict

Treat as a vendor-reported capability demo with useful harness-engineering anecdotes; it is not evidence about which harness structure works, and it publishes no failure data.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `harness-improvement-loop.md`

## intake-2258#record — Chain of Agents: Large Language Models Collaborating on Long-Context Tasks

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.02818
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: agent_architecture, context_management, document_processing, context_extension)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Mechanism: the long input is split into chunks that fit an agent window k; worker W_i receives chunk c_i, the query, a task instruction and the previous worker's 'communication unit' CU_{i-1}, and emits CU_i (Eq. 1; QA: evidence, summarization: running summary, code: code summary). A separate manager reads only the last CU and answers. Letting the last worker answer directly lowers performance (footnote 1). [CONFIRMED mechanism]
   - *unaffected*: Mechanism and footnote design choice confirmed in the text.
   - anchor `§3.1 Eq.1` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "Each worker Wi inputs the concatenation of a chunk ci from source text x, a query q, instruction for a specific task for worker agent IW, and the message passed from the previous agent, denoted as “communication unit” C​Ui−1."
   - anchor `§3.2 footnote 1` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "Our experiments show that using the last worker Wl to directly generate the final result leads to a performance drop."
1. Cost claim: encode cost O(nk) versus O(n^2) for full context and 'the same decoding time' (Table 2, App. A). NARROWED: the decode bound treats the per-worker response length r as constant, but each worker rewrites the whole CU (App. B prompt: 'generate a summary to include them both'), so decoded tokens scale with chunks x CU length; no wall-clock, token or dollar figure is measured anywhere in the paper. [NARROWED]
   - *narrowed*: Decode term treats r as constant though each worker rewrites the whole CU; no measured cost.
   - anchor `§3.3 Table 2` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "The time complexity is shown in Table 2 (Appendix A). As can be seen, the encoding time of CoA is less than Full-Context because k≪n in long context tasks, while they have the same decoding time."
2. Sequential beats parallel: CoA (8k) exceeds Merge (majority vote over per-chunk answers) and Hierarchical (judge-then-send-CU tree) on all 8 datasets (Table 6). NARROWED: both parallel comparators were built by the authors, have no re-ask round, no abstain-and-aggregate-with-confidence step and no cross-chunk protocol; the claim does not test a Minions- or LLMxMapReduce-style parallel design. In LLMxMapReduce's own InfiniteBench run a parallel LongAgent beats the CoA reimplementation (53.81 vs 15.97 avg), the opposite ordering at >100K tokens. [NARROWED]
   - *narrowed*: Authors-built parallel baselines without re-ask or confidence; third-party reverses at >100K.
   - anchor `§4.1 Baselines` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "In these approaches, worker agents are parallel and independent while CoA is sequential."
   - anchor `§4.2 Other Multi-Agent Frameworks` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "The reason behind the results is because Hierarchical and Merge do not allow workers to communicate with each other due to their parallel designs."
3. Setup scope: Google Vertex API models (PaLM 2 text-bison/unicorn 8k, Gemini Ultra 32k, Claude 3 haiku/sonnet/opus 200k), same model for workers and manager, temperature 0, generation capped 1024/2048 tokens, single runs, no intervals, nine datasets (Table 3-4, App. B). Headline 'up to 10%' (abstract) conflicts with text deltas of 13.30 and 22.00 points (section 4.2, absolute points not relative). [CONFIRMED as reported; NARROWED headline]
   - *narrowed*: Abstract's 10% vs text's 13-22 point deltas; single runs.
   - anchor `§4.1 LLMs` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "Although our framework is flexible to use diverse types of LLMs as workers and manager, we use the same model for each Wi and M if not specified."
   - anchor `App. B` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "Maximum generation token is set to 2048 for gemini-ultra and set to 1024 for the rest of the models. We set temperature to 0 for all experiments except for Self-consistency setting."
4. Ablations: removing the manager drops more than 10 points on MuSiQue; left-to-right order is best on most datasets but a random permutation beats it on HotpotQA (56.05 vs 53.62) and RepoBench-P (58.43 vs 58.25) (Table 7). [CONFIRMED; single runs]
   - *unaffected*: Table 7 numbers as reported; permutation wins on two datasets.
   - anchor `§5.5` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "As shown in Table 7, “w/o Manager” hurts the performance significantly, dropping more than 10% on MuSiQue."
   - anchor `§5.5` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "As shown in Table 7, on most of the datasets, left-to-right yields the highest score, demonstrating the advantages of natural reading order."
5. Cost and latency are not evaluated: the authors list cost and latency as a limitation and note more calls raise latency (Limitations, App. E). The 'cost effective' framing rests on the asymptotic bound only. [CONFIRMED; this governs EPYC transfer]
   - *unaffected*: Limitation stated by authors.
   - anchor `§6 Limitations` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "Third, the cost and latency of running CoA can be further reduced, such as replacing some LLMs with more effective models via model routing [56]."
   - anchor `App. E` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "Besides, it may increase the number of the calls for API, causing higher network traffic and higher latency for user pools."
6. Worker prompt: every worker is told to produce a summary that merges the previous summary with the current chunk ('Thus, your generated summary should be relatively long' for non-query tasks), so state is carried by rewriting, not appending. Example CUs (Tables 11-12) show near-verbatim repetition across workers. [CONFIRMED]
   - *unaffected*: Prompt text in App. B.
   - anchor `App. B Table 9 CoA worker prompt` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "You need to read current source text and summary of previous source text (if any) and generate a summary to include them both."
7. Multi-path: oracle selection over five permutation paths reaches 75.73 on HotpotQA versus 59.17 (judge) and 58.29 (vote), a large selector gap; the authors defer it to future work. [CONFIRMED as reported; oracle is not a method]
   - *unaffected*: Table 8 as reported.
   - anchor `§5.6` @ arXiv:2406.02818v1 HTML sha256 1d09d68b6c202dac979813c1eaf75b4babaea608376a7c28d443ac2f506d46d8: "Oracle picks the best path by evaluating score of each path, yielding the upper bound performance."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 STAGE-2b DIVE (arXiv:2406.02818v1, only version, full HTML text from disk, sha256 1d09d68b...). Mechanism CONFIRMED. NARROWED: decode-cost claim (r held constant), sequential-beats-parallel (authors-built baselines), headline 10% vs 13-22 point deltas. Added: duplicated NarrativeQA 25.26 cell across models; scorer-limited Claude Vanilla baseline possible; third-party reversal in LLMxMapReduce. Relevance medium; nothing overturned wholesale.

### Verdict

Cite for the sequential-chain mechanism and the authors' own ablations, each with scope (2024 API models, temperature 0, single runs). Do not cite the 'sequential beats parallel' result as general: the parallel baselines are authors-built and a third party reverses the order at >100K. Do not carry the O(nk) cost as an EPYC expectation: decode volume grows with chunk count and nothing is measured.
Mechanism confirmed and portable; headline gains are single-run API-model numbers against authors-built parallel baselines and the cost claim is asymptotic only. Disposition monitor; one comparator sentence routed to the M12 record.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `delegation-context-preassembly.md`
- updated `repl-turn-efficiency.md`
- updated `harness-improvement-loop.md`

## intake-2259#record — Coalition-Aware Skill Reliability for Self-Evolving Agents

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22610
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: memory_augmented, benchmark_methodology, agent_architecture, harness_optimization)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1321#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Auditing MemSkill skill banks by a knockout intervention paired on identical examples (Delta_s = V(B) - V(B minus s)) shows 'coalition pollution': the outcome gate admitted capture_activity_preferences on an online margin of +0.006 while its audited contribution is -0.005, and the skill that carries the bank (insert, +0.084, 95% CI excludes zero) loses most of its marginal (+0.084 to +0.018) when its partner capture_contextual_relationships is removed.
   - *unaffected*: Examined against the source text; see dive_corrections for any narrowing.
   - anchor `Sec 3.2; paper.txt line 123 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "finds the newly admitted capture_activity_preferences non-contributing (Δ=−0.005)"
   - anchor `Fig 3 caption; paper.txt line 98 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "Partner removed, marginal drops: insert falls from +0.084 to +0.018 once capture_contextual_relationships is removed."
1. The gate's accept/reject margins (0.006 to 0.02) are only one to a few standard errors of its own reward estimate (about 0.004), and auditing a single skill on 314 paired queries resolves its contribution only to about +-0.04, an order of magnitude coarser than the margins being acted on.
   - *unaffected*: Examined against the source text; see dive_corrections for any narrowing.
   - anchor `Sec 3.2; paper.txt line 161 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "auditing a single skill on 314 paired queries resolves its contribution only to ≈±0.04 (shaded band), an order of magnitude coarser than the margins being acted on."
   - anchor `Sec 3.2; paper.txt line 161 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "the accept/reject margins it acts on (0.006 to 0.02) are only one to a few times the per-stage standard error of its own reward estimate"
2. Theorem 1: a gate observing only the two endpoint values cannot identify whether the accepted bank contains a negatively contributing member (Appendix A.1 builds two banks with identical endpoints and opposite member signs). The appendix is not in the arXiv v1 PDF (9 pages), so the proof was not read.
   - *unaffected*: Examined against the source text; see dive_corrections for any narrowing.
   - anchor `Sec 3.2; paper.txt line 164 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "Appendix A.1 constructs two banks with identical endpoints yet opposite signs for one member’s contribution."
3. Cross-domain utility reversal: insert is +0.084 on the source (LoCoMo) and -0.035 on HotpotQA, but the target 95% CI still includes zero, so the authors read it as a directional reversal, not significant harm.
   - *unaffected*: Examined against the source text; see dive_corrections for any narrowing.
   - anchor `Sec 3.3; paper.txt line 171 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "on HotpotQA its point estimate reverses to Δ=−0.035, though with a 95% CI that still includes zero"
4. CASS (sampled-coalition knockout margin averaged over N coalitions, combined with online reward at lambda=0.2) accepted 1 of 12 candidate skills against MemSkill's 6 of 12; the additions MemSkill admitted are collectively net-negative (-1.38 pp average over three benchmarks, with LongMemEval +2.48 and LoCoMo -3.50, HP-50 -3.13).
   - *unaffected*: Examined against the source text; see dive_corrections for any narrowing.
   - anchor `Sec 5.3; paper.txt line 381 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "Across 12 candidate-skill proposals (4 gate decisions per seed, 3 seeds), CASS accepts 1 while MemSkill accepts 6 (Figure 5)."
   - anchor `Sec 5.3; paper.txt line 381 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "they are collectively net-negative (−1.38 pp on average), although every one satisfied the outcome criterion when admitted"
5. u-SMCO masks skills greedily by a label-free retrieval-quality score (mean cosine between target queries and memories rebuilt using only the kept skills); it masked Skill_IN on 5 of 6 trained banks, raised HP-50 LLM-judge by +2.13 to +8.58 pp, and the selection-policy-entropy alternative anti-correlates with the label-based ranking (rho -0.18) while retrieval quality reaches rho +0.76 (the supporting Table 5 is in an appendix absent from v1).
   - *unaffected*: Examined against the source text; see dive_corrections for any narrowing.
   - anchor `Sec 5.4; paper.txt line 425 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "Skill_IN is masked on 5/6 of them with no target label"
   - anchor `Sec 5.4; paper.txt line 425 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "Masking improves every bank, but it improves the MemSkill banks twice as much as the CASS banks (7.17 vs. 3.59 pp on average)"
   - anchor `Sec 5.5; paper.txt line 430 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "anti-correlates with the label-based ranking (ρ=−0.18, Table 5)"
   - anchor `Sec 5.5; paper.txt line 430 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "Retrieval quality is measured on the target itself and is decoupled from the training reward, reaching ρ=+0.76."
6. Cost as reported: CASS adds eight coalition evaluations per outer epoch (about 5 percent of a 20-hour run); u-SMCO needs O(K^2) memory rebuilds at 6-10 minutes per mask step, once before deployment.
   - *unaffected*: Examined against the source text; see dive_corrections for any narrowing.
   - anchor `Sec 5.5; paper.txt line 432 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.22610v1: "CASS adds eight coalition evaluations per outer epoch, about 5% of a 20-hour run"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 DIVE (dpfw2-w2b, arXiv:2608.22610v1 HTML read from disk, Sections 1-6; the arXiv v1 PDF is 9 pages and carries NO appendix, so Appendix A.1 (Theorem 1 proof), A.3 (permutation counts), C.1 (entropy ablation) and Table 5 were not available and are not read). Entry newly created. The wave-1 ASSAY dive described this paper as 'a different group's masking method, found but not read'; NARROWED on reading: it is two different things. (1) CASS is an admission-time gate and (2) u-SMCO is a deployment mask, both for MemSkill memory-operation skills; it is not a label-free causal attribution for retrieved task-procedure skills, so it does not answer the ASSAY A1 question (which entries make an M-23 cell net-negative). What does transfer is the audit design (paired leave-one-out knockout on identical examples) and the measured resolution (about +-0.04 at 314 paired queries). OVERTURNED as a citable claim: 'u-SMCO improves every bank by 2-8 pp' is not evidence of label-free reliability, because the probe is 20 unlabeled queries, the evaluation is a single LLM-judge number per bank, and the paper's own audit resolution is about +-4 pp.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `episodic-memory-integrity.md`

## intake-2260#record — Finding the Best Harness for DeepSeek V4 Flash (Composio; Sunil Kumar Dash)

- **URL**: https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty low, relevance low; categories: harness_optimization, benchmark_methodology, tool_implementation)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1354#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Eight harnesses on DeepSeek V4 Flash over 30 hard multi-SaaS workflows (900 s limit) gave 129 of 240 successes (53.8%); Pi 20/30 (66.7%), OMP 17/30, Claude Code, Codex and DeepAgents 16/30, Hermes 15/30, OpenCode 14/30, Prime Agent 15/24 valid runs; only 6 of 30 workflows passed on every harness.
   - anchor `The Outcome` @ https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash (Sunil Kumar Dash, Aug 11, 2026) page.html sha256 f40521ff47e0863f4d46ded4991c5f3741e3420ae4c14808e0f97241846915de (matches wave-1 f40521ff...); primary.txt (tag-stripped, reused from dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp) sha256 84fcf433c2a84aba62e5d6ebe4acc057fef30aa68ef52e6c8093a7c6f78bb907 re-hashed identical to the wave-1 copy: "Across 240 runs, the harnesses completed 129 successfully—an overall pass rate of 53.8%. Only six of the 30 workflows were completed successfully by every harness."
1. Grading is a programmatic state-inspecting verifier with per-harness isolated fixtures containing decoys; a run passes only if every check passes (including untouched decoys and required tools); no LLM judge; one agent passed 11 of 12 checks yet failed.
   - anchor `How did we grade the success?` @ https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash (Sunil Kumar Dash, Aug 11, 2026) page.html sha256 f40521ff47e0863f4d46ded4991c5f3741e3420ae4c14808e0f97241846915de (matches wave-1 f40521ff...); primary.txt (tag-stripped, reused from dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp) sha256 84fcf433c2a84aba62e5d6ebe4acc057fef30aa68ef52e6c8093a7c6f78bb907 re-hashed identical to the wave-1 copy: "A run counted as successful only when every required check passed."
   - anchor `How did we grade the success?` @ https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash (Sunil Kumar Dash, Aug 11, 2026) page.html sha256 f40521ff47e0863f4d46ded4991c5f3741e3420ae4c14808e0f97241846915de (matches wave-1 f40521ff...); primary.txt (tag-stripped, reused from dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp) sha256 84fcf433c2a84aba62e5d6ebe4acc057fef30aa68ef52e6c8093a7c6f78bb907 re-hashed identical to the wave-1 copy: "We didn’t use another LLM to determine whether the answer looked right."
2. Cost per successful task ranged from $0.028 (Pi) to $0.195 (Claude Code), explained mainly by cache share (Claude Code 1.5% of tokens cached versus about 70% for Codex and 57% for OMP; fresh input priced 5x cached); the costs are estimates from token use and list prices, and Pi ran at a different reasoning setting on two providers.
   - anchor `Cost per successful task` @ https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash (Sunil Kumar Dash, Aug 11, 2026) page.html sha256 f40521ff47e0863f4d46ded4991c5f3741e3420ae4c14808e0f97241846915de (matches wave-1 f40521ff...); primary.txt (tag-stripped, reused from dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp) sha256 84fcf433c2a84aba62e5d6ebe4acc057fef30aa68ef52e6c8093a7c6f78bb907 re-hashed identical to the wave-1 copy: "Only 1.5% of its tokens came from the cache, compared with about 70% for Codex and 57% for OMP. Fresh input cost five times more than cached input, so this made a large difference."
   - anchor `Cost per successful task` @ https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash (Sunil Kumar Dash, Aug 11, 2026) page.html sha256 f40521ff47e0863f4d46ded4991c5f3741e3420ae4c14808e0f97241846915de (matches wave-1 f40521ff...); primary.txt (tag-stripped, reused from dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp) sha256 84fcf433c2a84aba62e5d6ebe4acc057fef30aa68ef52e6c8093a7c6f78bb907 re-hashed identical to the wave-1 copy: "Pi used a different reasoning setting, and it used two model providers. This limits a direct comparison."
   - anchor `Cost per successful task` @ https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash (Sunil Kumar Dash, Aug 11, 2026) page.html sha256 f40521ff47e0863f4d46ded4991c5f3741e3420ae4c14808e0f97241846915de (matches wave-1 f40521ff...); primary.txt (tag-stripped, reused from dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp) sha256 84fcf433c2a84aba62e5d6ebe4acc057fef30aa68ef52e6c8093a7c6f78bb907 re-hashed identical to the wave-1 copy: "We estimated these costs from recorded token use and provider prices. They are API cost estimates."
3. Six Prime Agent runs were excluded as ungradable (two because the verifier timed out on sessions of up to 3.5M tokens, four unrecorded); Prime Agent used the most runtime tokens (about 1.4M per task); Hermes' cost is a lower bound (usage not saved on two timeouts).
   - anchor `Pass rate` @ https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash (Sunil Kumar Dash, Aug 11, 2026) page.html sha256 f40521ff47e0863f4d46ded4991c5f3741e3420ae4c14808e0f97241846915de (matches wave-1 f40521ff...); primary.txt (tag-stripped, reused from dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp) sha256 84fcf433c2a84aba62e5d6ebe4acc057fef30aa68ef52e6c8093a7c6f78bb907 re-hashed identical to the wave-1 copy: "We excluded 6 Prime Agent runs that our grader could not score."
   - anchor `Average token consumption per task` @ https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash (Sunil Kumar Dash, Aug 11, 2026) page.html sha256 f40521ff47e0863f4d46ded4991c5f3741e3420ae4c14808e0f97241846915de (matches wave-1 f40521ff...); primary.txt (tag-stripped, reused from dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp) sha256 84fcf433c2a84aba62e5d6ebe4acc057fef30aa68ef52e6c8093a7c6f78bb907 re-hashed identical to the wave-1 copy: "Each harness can count runtime tokens differently. So, use these values as an approximate comparison."
   - anchor `Cost per successful task` @ https://composio.dev/content/best-agent-harness-deepseek-v4-flash (Sunil Kumar Dash, Aug 11, 2026) page.html sha256 f40521ff47e0863f4d46ded4991c5f3741e3420ae4c14808e0f97241846915de (matches wave-1 f40521ff...); primary.txt (tag-stripped, reused from dive-dpfw1-composio-pi-vs-omp) sha256 84fcf433c2a84aba62e5d6ebe4acc057fef30aa68ef52e6c8093a7c6f78bb907 re-hashed identical to the wave-1 copy: "Hermes timed out on two tasks and did not save the final usage data, so its actual cost was higher than the reported value."

### Verdict

dive-verified, read FULL from disk. Cite only for: (a) the exclusion-handling worked example (an ungradable-run exclusion that correlates with the arm's own behavior changes its rank), (b) cache share as the largest driver of cost per success. Do NOT cite the harness ranking or dollar figures (vendor, n=1 per task, estimated prices, Pi at a different setting).
worth_investigating as a source of worked examples; knowledge_only.

## intake-2261#record — Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study: Deterministic Aggregation vs. Recursive Language Models, an OOLONG replication

- **URL**: https://github.com/Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty low, relevance medium; categories: benchmark_methodology, agent_architecture, context_management)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. The author retracted an earlier "87.8% exact match, zero query-time LLM calls" claim after the RLM authors flagged that the run had fed ground-truth labels (context_window_text_with_labels) into the context; a second self-correction (2026-07-28) moved the evaluation from 250 short tasks to the paper setting. The history is on the README and in git.
   - anchor `README Correction` @ github Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study @ 1652c4b879696782c3819a0239a28c2445d1587c (2026-07-28); README.md sha256 56b7dbead000e47eb7c0c45a79f861c98b7eb13d8c9052de0f2175955aec32f5: "Those results were inflated by a methodology error"
   - anchor `README Correction` @ github Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study @ 1652c4b879696782c3819a0239a28c2445d1587c (2026-07-28); README.md sha256 56b7dbead000e47eb7c0c45a79f861c98b7eb13d8c9052de0f2175955aec32f5: "The earlier run fed `context_window_text_with_labels` into the context"
   - anchor `README Correction` @ github Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study @ 1652c4b879696782c3819a0239a28c2445d1587c (2026-07-28); README.md sha256 56b7dbead000e47eb7c0c45a79f861c98b7eb13d8c9052de0f2175955aec32f5: "**Second correction (Jul 28, 2026), caught in our own re-audit:**"
1. Corrected, on the RLM paper setting (OOLONG trec_coarse, 50 tasks at 131K, validation split, labels withheld): classify each record with GPT-5 (minimal reasoning), then aggregate with Python Counter(): 63.38 score / 62.00 exact match with 320 LLM calls, against GPT-5 base 44.00 and RLM depth-2/3 56.50/58.00 (paper figures as quoted by the README).
   - anchor `README Corrected results` @ github Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study @ 1652c4b879696782c3819a0239a28c2445d1587c (2026-07-28); README.md sha256 56b7dbead000e47eb7c0c45a79f861c98b7eb13d8c9052de0f2175955aec32f5: "| **Ours — classify + `Counter()` (gpt-5, minimal reasoning)** | **63.38%** | **62.00%** | **320 total** |"
   - anchor `README Corrected results` @ github Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study @ 1652c4b879696782c3819a0239a28c2445d1587c (2026-07-28); README.md sha256 56b7dbead000e47eb7c0c45a79f861c98b7eb13d8c9052de0f2175955aec32f5: "| GPT-5 base (paper) | 44.00% | — | — |"
   - anchor `README Limitations` @ github Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study @ 1652c4b879696782c3819a0239a28c2445d1587c (2026-07-28); README.md sha256 56b7dbead000e47eb7c0c45a79f861c98b7eb13d8c9052de0f2175955aec32f5: "One benchmark, one dataset (OOLONG `trec_coarse`): 50 tasks at 131K, 250 tasks at 1K–16K."
2. By task: relative/most/least frequency 75-83%, but NUMERIC_ONE_CLASS only 5.8% (N=12): exact counts under 0.75**|err| collapse when per-record classification noise accumulates over about 3,182 records.
   - anchor `README By task type` @ github Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study @ 1652c4b879696782c3819a0239a28c2445d1587c (2026-07-28); README.md sha256 56b7dbead000e47eb7c0c45a79f861c98b7eb13d8c9052de0f2175955aec32f5: "| NUMERIC_ONE_CLASS | 5.8% | 12 |"
3. The author says the advantage over RLM is in total calls and determinism, not in eliminating inference; the runner vendors the official scorer verbatim.
   - anchor `README What holds` @ github Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study @ 1652c4b879696782c3819a0239a28c2445d1587c (2026-07-28); README.md sha256 56b7dbead000e47eb7c0c45a79f861c98b7eb13d8c9052de0f2175955aec32f5: "the advantage over RLM is in *total* calls and determinism, **not** in eliminating inference."
   - anchor `README Reproducing` @ github Cornjebus/rlm-replication-study @ 1652c4b879696782c3819a0239a28c2445d1587c (2026-07-28); README.md sha256 56b7dbead000e47eb7c0c45a79f861c98b7eb13d8c9052de0f2175955aec32f5: "scores with the official OOLONG scorer (vendored verbatim from [abertsch72/oolong](https://github.com/abertsch72/oolong))"

### Verdict

Use only as a worked example that classify-then-Counter is competitive on counting-light questions and collapses on exact counts. Never cite its 87.8% figure; never quote its headline as an RLM comparison without re-reading the RLM paper on the same split.

## intake-2262#record — Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.13359
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: cost_aware_routing, benchmark_methodology, routing_intelligence)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1313#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Cost-of-pass is the expected monetary cost of obtaining one correct answer: cost of one attempt divided by the probability of success, under independent retries; infinite when the success probability is zero.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "We formalize cost-of-pass, the expected monetary cost of generating a correct solution."
   - anchor `§2.2` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "assuming independent trials"
1. The frontier cost-of-pass takes the minimum over available models and a human-expert baseline per problem; the gain from a new model is the reduction it causes in that frontier, and a counterfactual frontier (remove a model family) shows which family is essential for which task type.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "We then define the frontier cost-of-pass as the minimum cost-of-pass achievable across available models or the human-expert(s), using the approximate cost of hiring an expert."
2. Across May 2024 to Feb 2025 the frontier cost-of-pass fell roughly exponentially; for complex quantitative tasks it halved every 2.6 months (MATH-500) to 7.1 months (AIME-24); lightweight models are best on basic quantitative tasks, large models on knowledge tasks, reasoning models on complex quantitative tasks.
   - anchor `§3.3` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "halved approximately every 2.6 months"
3. Majority voting did not improve the frontier cost-of-pass on any tested dataset; self-refinement helped on knowledge tasks (up to 24.9% on GPQA-Diamond); the budget-aware TALE-EP gave visible reductions on a subset: recent frontier gains are mostly model-level, not inference-time technique.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "We find that performance-oriented methods with marginal performance gains rarely justify the costs, while TALE-EP shows some promise."
   - anchor `§3.5` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "majority voting (despite potentially enhancing accuracy) does not offer relative economic improvement across the tested models and datasets"
4. Structural weakness (App. C.8): on multiple-choice tasks an extremely cheap random guesser attains a lower cost-of-pass than any competent model (BBQ 7.9e-5 vs 6.2e-3 dollars; GPQA-Diamond 2.1e-4 vs 8.07), so unreliable strategies must be excluded from the frontier or a stricter pass criterion (pass^k) used.
   - anchor `App. C.8` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "an extremely cheap yet essentially random agent can appear economically optimal, despite being useless (or even malicious) from an application perspective."
   - anchor `App. C.8` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "using stricter success criteria for a pass such as pass^k, which exponentially penalize low-accuracy models"
5. Stated limitations: binary success only; per-attempt cost omits verification overheads, wait time, retries and tool charges; one fixed prompt and decoding setting; benchmark contamination possible; hybrid human-AI workflows not modeled.
   - anchor `App. D.3` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "it still omits indirect and context-specific terms (like evaluation/verification overheads, wait times, invocation retries, tool-call charges etc.)"
   - anchor `App. D.3` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "our framework assumes a binary success/failure criterion, thus continuous or composite notions of success are not modeled directly."
   - anchor `App. D.3` @ arXiv:2504.13359v2 [cs.AI] 26 Feb 2026, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.13359 curl 2026-10-21 HTTP 200, paper.html sha256 2ea189f9818d2e95731cd2b8d9089d7d8ab2f06302850ec761_359_706_876_184bb (abs page title confirmed: 'Cost-of-Pass: An Economic Framework for Evaluating Language Models'); p.txt = tag-stripped non-blank lines sha256 993019673d25b635578dfcbc77b2e05db88f47d6d673754313f19f430ce10930: "the evaluated models may have been partially exposed to these datasets"

### Verdict

dive-verified, read FULL from disk. Cite for the definition, the frontier form, and the C.8 random-guesser weakness with its mitigations. Do NOT import the paper's dollar figures or halving times as local expectations (hosted API prices, 2024 models; EPYC local roles have no per-token price, so a local denomination such as slot-occupancy seconds must be declared). Cost-of-pass assumes independent retries and binary success.
adopt_patterns: the definition and its guards are portable; no numbers are.

- updated `harness-improvement-loop.md`
- updated `routing-intelligence.md`

## intake-2263#record — When a Verified World Model Still Loses: Play-Adequacy vs Prediction-Accuracy in LLM-Synthesized Code World Models

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14169
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: agent_architecture, benchmark_methodology, formal_verification)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1133#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. A gate-passing (transition accuracy 1.0), >=98% state-accurate LLM-synthesized world model can still lose systematically at play, because the under-1% it gets wrong is the pivotal dynamics; state accuracy is diluted, play is not.
   - anchor `§1.4` @ arXiv:2607.14169v2: "State accuracy is blind to the omission (dilution: the handful of wrong states is averaged into a large pool of correct ones, so the aggregate barely moves); play is not."
1. Isolated play cost of the omitted rule (hand-instrumented, budget-matched, 600 simulations, 20 seeds x 120 games per arm): fair baseline 0.495 vs rule-blind 0.404, play cost 0.091, seed-clustered 95% CI [0.065, 0.117].
   - anchor `§3.3 Panel A` @ arXiv:2607.14169v2: "play_cost = 0.091 (the fair-baseline win rate minus the rule-blind win rate)."
2. Through the actual synthesis pipeline (GPT-5.4-mini, 20 seeds), the incomplete-rules CWM passes the gate in 9/20 seeds, only when no material-at-cap terminal is in its sample, and then loses with play cost 0.154; complete-rules control is near parity (0.024).
   - anchor `§3.3 Panel B` @ arXiv:2607.14169v2: "The complete-rules control is near parity (a small residual deficit,"
3. Gate-miss probability for a rule firing with random-play rarity r under N fresh i.i.d. random draws is exactly (1-r)^N (Proposition 1); only fresh draws count, re-checking the same trajectories adds nothing; danger = play_cost x (1-r)^N is a lower bound on harm for an idealised synthesizer.
   - anchor `§4.1 Proposition 1 proof` @ arXiv:2607.14169v2: "Each play-through is a Bernoulli(rr) event (rule fires / does not fire), and the NN plays are i.i.d."
   - anchor `§4.1 What N counts` @ arXiv:2607.14169v2: "only fresh draws count — re-using the same trajectories does not increase NN."
4. More example transitions did not recover the omitted rule (mini and large GPT-5.x; naive/proper DAgger, targeted on-manifold data): e.g. 54 real on-manifold discriminating transitions with 12 refinements leave the gate at 0.959 and the rule unlearned; off-manifold repair data corrupts synthesis.
   - anchor `§5.2 Findings` @ arXiv:2607.14169v2: "Even 54 real, on-manifold discriminating transitions with 12 refinement iterations leave the gate at 0.959 and the rule unlearned."
5. Verification on the play distribution detects the gap: replacing one of 2000 random gate play-throughs with one held-out reference-planner trajectory rejects the Beacon instrument (4/8190 mismatches), and a bounded policy-free falsifier rejects it after 17 expanded states.
   - anchor `§6.6` @ arXiv:2607.14169v2: "The candidate is never used to generate gate states, so it cannot steer the check away from its own error region."
   - anchor `§1.3` @ arXiv:2607.14169v2: "Whenever a CWM passes the random-trajectory gate, it is also correct on the MCTS-visited distribution; whenever it is wrong on the search distribution, it also fails the gate."
6. Scope limits stated by the author: the rare-rule instrument is engineered, Beacon is a minimal strategically trivial witness, and on small fully specified games the random gate IS identifying (honest null result).
   - anchor `§8 The rare-rule instrument is engineered` @ arXiv:2607.14169v2: "We do not claim that all or even most rules in arbitrary games will produce this kind of gap"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 Stage-2b (dpfw2-w2d): id resolved via arXiv abs page (title matches, submitted 15 Jul 2026, v2 19 Jul 2026). Dedup against research/intake_index.yaml on origin/main (id, URL, title; unbounded) and in-flight wave entries: no existing row. Surfaced by the wave-1 CWM dive as a listing-only pointer; this dive reads it.

- updated `autokernel-research-loop.md`

## intake-2264#record — When Does Divide and Conquer Work for Long Context LLM? A Noise Decomposition Framework

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.16411
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: context_extension, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Long-context failure = task noise (cross-chunk dependence) + model noise (length-induced) + aggregator noise; fidelity is a product, losses additive (Eq 1-2).
   - anchor `Sec 3.1` @ v2: "The multiplicative nature of fidelity implies additive losses"
1. Chunking wins when model noise dominates (super-linear loss in length) and task noise is modest; saturates below optimum when task noise dominates (Char task).
   - anchor `Sec 3.6 Regime 2` @ arXiv:2506.16411v2 html sha256 5124b251...: "D&C strategies saturate below the optimal performance regardless of model quality"
   - anchor `Sec 5.3` @ v2: "Apart from the high-synergy character-inference problem, divide-and-conquer methods achieve performance on par with or exceeding the strongest single-shot model"
2. Optimal chunk size is task- and model-dependent; 3-5 sample sparse search finds it (Table 1; optima 2K-64K at 128K input, usually 4K-32K).
   - anchor `Sec 5.5` @ v2: "even with three to five samples per configuration, the selected chunk sizes are near-optimal"

### Verdict

adopt_patterns (chunk-size sparse search; task-noise screen)

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `repl-turn-efficiency.md`
- updated `delegation-context-preassembly.md`

### Claims (as verified)

0. The speedrun task is fixed and verified by a frozen checker: a record claim is the mean val loss over eight fixed-seed runs the agent cannot touch, below 3.27859, a margin giving roughly one-in-a-thousand false pass; baseline 3,290 steps, human record 2,600 steps.
   - anchor `README.md > The task` @ github.com/PrimeIntellect-ai/frontier-automated-speedrun @ 38e258afefb1ce206dd7595aa71d7740da405742 (git clone --depth 1, commit dated 2026-08-15, 2026-10-21). README.md sha256 b17e578abea76bc5f2b3fe5b9e564369db48dd9f9a209582dc4e66734d2198b4; program.md sha256 8af050630fa1deeef63a61da8d7ae1ec36b3925603f333c16572f5e30b53af08; program-serial.md sha256 8a00288cc9d860faab96857d41f941723ba3e10346622c7151339935abd1d4b5; traces/manifest.json.gz decompressed to manifest.raw.json sha256 a3176a2e9ef8a4aa943cac112467129b37d13a567d4185fe70fc63cf4dc02e41: "recipe eight times on fixed seeds the agent cannot touch and beating a mean val loss of"
1. program.md (the rulebook every agent ran under) prescribes screen-cheap-then-widen: a one-trial screen is a noisy read (per-run sigma about 0.0013), an idea is not to be called worse on a sub-sigma gap or generalized from one run, trials are added progressively for ideas that hold up, and only the fixed eight-trial set confirms a record; partial mid-run loss is a biased proxy and runs are not stopped early on it.
   - anchor `program.md > Evaluating an idea (screen bullet)` @ github.com/PrimeIntellect-ai/frontier-automated-speedrun @ 38e258afefb1ce206dd7595aa71d7740da405742 (git clone --depth 1, commit dated 2026-08-15, 2026-10-21). Do NOT cite it as evidence of a sandbox or verifier design (not in the repo), and do NOT compare serial-era and standard-contract rows as a harness effect.
