## intake-2293#record — LLM Benchmarks: Is It Worth ($$) Mixing 2 Models? (Planner + Executor) (Fabio Akita, AkitaOnRails)

- **URL**: https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/04/25/llm-benchmarks-vale-a-pena-misturar-2-modelos/
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty low, relevance medium; categories: agent_architecture, cost_aware_routing, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1354#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Unforced, no model delegated in any of 7 variants (n=1 each) even with 'Use PROACTIVELY' / 'ALWAYS delegate' language and a registered subagent; the main model did 100% of the work.
   - anchor `Segment 1` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "Zero delegations in zero of the 7 variants."
   - anchor `Segment 1` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "“Use PROACTIVELY” is a weak word against the model’s internal “this task is cohesive, I’ll do it myself” instinct."
1. When delegation was forced (planner forbidden from Write/Edit/Bash), every variant scored Tier A (90-95) but none beat solo Opus 4.7 (97) and all cost extra wall time (+1 to +35 min); only GPT 5.4 xHigh + medium executor was a real cost saving ($1-3 vs ~$16 solo, -3 points).
   - anchor `Segment 2` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "none beats solo Opus 4.7 on quality. All cost extra time (minimum +7 minutes, up to +35 minutes for multi_faster)."
   - anchor `Final comparison` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "The real exception is Codex GPT 5.4 xHigh + medium executor: 94/100 vs 97/100 (loses 3 points), but costs $1-3 instead of $16 for GPT 5.4 solo (80-85% cheaper)."
   - anchor `Methodology in one line` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "Solo Opus 4.7 in opencode is the comparison baseline: 97/100 Tier A, 18 minutes, ~$4 pay-as-you-go."
2. Manual cross-process orchestration (Opus driving opencode per subtask) tied solo on quality at best (Kimi K2.6, 97) but the planner's turns (~3-5 per dispatch at $0.15-0.25) were ~$11 for ~14 dispatches, not logged in the executor JSON, so true cost was ~3x solo.
   - anchor `The hidden planner cost` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "Total: **$11 of hidden planner cost**, not logged in the executor JSON."
   - anchor `The hidden planner cost` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "Manual orchestration costs 3× more than solo."
3. Lift from orchestration scales inversely with the executor's solo capability (GLM 46 to 93, Qwen 71 to 94, Kimi 87 to 97), and the author attributes the lift to the planner's detailed, API-naming prompts rather than autonomous executor capability.
   - anchor `What this round actually proves` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "The lift scales inversely with the executor’s solo capability."
   - anchor `Opus + Qwen 3.6 Plus (Variant 1)` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "But the entire lift comes from Opus’s detailed plan, not from autonomous Qwen capability."
4. Delegability depends on dependency structure: independent batch work (translate 100 docs, apply one refactor to 50 files) parallelises; a dependency-laden greenfield app does not, and the author says conclusions may invert on batch work.
   - anchor `Limit 2` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "Building a Rails app is exactly that."
5. A harness-level silent failure (DeepSeek V4 Pro reasoning_content stripped by opencode's ai-sdk, 400 on turn 2) made the 'executor' contribute zero lines while runs were reported complete, because the general fallback (Opus) did the writing.
   - anchor `Segment 3` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "DeepSeek V4 Pro returns reasoning_content on every response, and opencode strips that field when constructing the next request."
   - anchor `Why DeepSeek was the hardest model to test` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "DeepSeek V4 Pro never contributed a single line of code in any of the three multi-agent rounds"
6. Same model, different harness: Opus 4.7 in Claude Code cost 4-7x more and hallucinated an API in 2 of 3 runs versus opencode (n=1 each).
   - anchor `Segment 1` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "the same Opus 4.7 wrote worse code in Claude Code than in opencode, and cost 4 to 7× more ($6.74 vs $1.10)"
   - anchor `Segment 1` @ page.html sha256 7b7385f9873e: "Two of three Claude Code variants hallucinated a RubyLLM method that doesn’t exist (Tier 3 hallucination)"

## intake-2295#record — Block-Attention for Efficient Prefilling

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.15355
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance low; categories: inference_serving)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1313#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Blocks (one retrieved passage each) are encoded independently with positions reset to zero and later RoPE-rotated to their real offset; only the final block (the query) attends all earlier blocks, so a cached passage KV is reusable at any position.
   - anchor `Sec 1, para 5` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "Each block independently calculates its KV states through full-attention, without any attention to other blocks. Only the final block is able to attend other blocks"
   - anchor `Sec 2.3` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "in the KV cache, the positional encoding of the initial token of each block is standardized to zero"
1. The mechanism is NOT training-free: without fine-tuning, average RAG accuracy falls from 66.1 to 49.9; a block fine-tune (standard SFT with a modified attention mask, 8 H20 GPUs, ~500-1000 steps) is required.
   - anchor `Sec 1, para 7` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "this approach resulted in a substantial decrease in performance, with the average accuracy of Llama-3.1-Tulu-3-8B-SFT on four RAG benchmarks falling from 66.1% to 49.9%"
   - anchor `Sec 2.4` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "The only difference from the standard SFT process is the modification of the traditional lower-triangular attention mask matrix"
   - anchor `Sec 3.4` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "All experiments are conducted using 8 NVIDIA H20 GPUs"
2. Block-ft reaches 70.0 average on four RAG sets vs 71.3 for the full-attention RAG-tuned model; the stated "gap <= 1%" is contradicted by the paper own Table 1 (HotpotQA 72.3 vs 74.8 = 2.5 points; NQ 1.1).
   - anchor `Sec 3.5 point 2` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "the performance gap between Tulu3-block-ft and Tulu3-RAG on four benchmarks is less than or equal to 1%"
   - anchor `Table 1 (rows Tulu3-RAG / Tulu3-block-ft HQA)` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "Tulu3-RAG 73.2 74.8 61.5 75.8"
3. Baselines PromptCache (32.4/31.6/44.4/61.8) and Superposition collapse versus block-ft, but CacheBlend is only discussed in Sec 4.3 and NOT benchmarked; the PromptCache baseline setup is unspecified and its collapse is attributed to positional handling that Prompt Cache does handle.
   - anchor `Sec 3.3 baselines` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "Tulu3-RAG-promptCache: A baseline model that applies the PromptCache (Gim et al., 2024) to the Tulu3-RAG model."
   - anchor `Sec 4.3` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "CacheBlend introduces a trade-off between generation quality and KV cache reuse efficiency."
4. Reported TTFT drops from 3638 ms to 45 ms at 32K (98.7%), explicitly excluding the cost of loading the cached KV (footnote 4); hardware for Table 3 not stated in the text read.
   - anchor `Sec 1 footnote 4` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "in this paper we do not take the cost of KV cache into account"
   - anchor `Sec 3.6 Table 3` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "TTFT-vanilla 26 50 87 167 330 691 1515 3638"
5. Training mixes block and full attention so one model switches modes; passages are concatenated in ascending retrieval-score order, so reuse presupposes a fixed passage library.
   - anchor `Sec 3.2` @ arXiv:2409.15355v5 (13 Apr 2025), HTML sha256 f1d854afa1776589e74bf431ff96b1d346d81e9de45e502c1012be71a6411914: "For retrieved passages, we concatenate them in ascending order of retrieval score."

### Verdict

Do not propose Block-Attention against live roles: it needs a block-fine-tuned model and a pure-attention shiftable KV (neither exists in the lineup). Do not cite "<=1% gap" or "beats CacheBlend": the first is contradicted by its own Table 1 and the second was never run. The only open question is the chunk-library audit in DIVE.md B7.

## intake-2296#record — The Larger the Better? Improved LLM Code-Generation via Budget Reallocation

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.00725
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: cost_aware_routing, benchmark_methodology, local_inference)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. With unit tests available as the selector, repeated sampling from a smaller Code Llama (7B/13B) matches or beats a single output from 34B/70B at the same FLOPs or wall-time on HumanEval, MBPP and APPS (gaps 5 to 15 points in the small-budget regime; 13B best on APPS, about 5 points over 70B on competition by wall-time).
   - *narrowed*: Gain is with unit-test selection on code tasks; the abstract's 'up to 15%' is a small-budget-regime gap.
   - anchor `section 8 Conclusion` @ arXiv:2404.00725v2 HTML sha256 c13df69c530edc0a82b474595b0c97a25a7f8e79e6b4545918ac045ffa084a44: "Our findings reveal that generating multiple outputs from a 13B model may lead to gains of up to 15% over a single generation from a 70B model across five tasks."
   - anchor `section 4` @ arXiv:2404.00725v2 HTML sha256 c13df69c530edc0a82b474595b0c97a25a7f8e79e6b4545918ac045ffa084a44: "Our results indicate that small models can match or even outperform large ones under a fixed compute budget, assuming the availability of unit-tests."
1. The result is task-size dependent: on APPS the 7B model falls behind at larger budgets, which the authors read as a minimum model size for a given difficulty.
   - *unaffected*: Authors' own reading.
   - anchor `section 4` @ arXiv:2404.00725v2 HTML sha256 c13df69c530edc0a82b474595b0c97a25a7f8e79e6b4545918ac045ffa084a44: "This can be attributed to the 7B model’s inability to generate a sufficient number of correct answers for the task, and may suggest that there is a minimum size requirement for a certain level of task difficulty."
2. Without unit tests the result flips: selecting among the 7B model's samples by (average) negative log-likelihood from Code Llama 7B/13B/34B/70B, even with larger rankers, falls short of a single greedy output from the larger model at equal wall-time; the LEVER verifier did not help in the test-less setting.
   - *unaffected*: Stated in conclusion and section 5.2; the larger-model comparator is greedy decoding.
   - anchor `section 8 Conclusion` @ arXiv:2404.00725v2 HTML sha256 c13df69c530edc0a82b474595b0c97a25a7f8e79e6b4545918ac045ffa084a44: "We found the proposed ranking approach falls short in performance compared to a single output from the larger model."
   - anchor `section 5.2` @ arXiv:2404.00725v2 HTML sha256 c13df69c530edc0a82b474595b0c97a25a7f8e79e6b4545918ac045ffa084a44: "As shown in Figure 9, the LEVER verifier does not improve the results in the test-less setting, which is expected"
3. Wall-time budget is measured on 8 A100 with batch size tuned for optimal throughput; a 70B sample costs 14.19 times a 7B sample in wall-time (10.41 times in FLOPs).
   - *narrowed*: GPU, batch-tuned wall-time; not transferable to a bandwidth-bound CPU.
   - anchor `section 3.2` @ arXiv:2404.00725v2 HTML sha256 c13df69c530edc0a82b474595b0c97a25a7f8e79e6b4545918ac045ffa084a44: "We measure wall-time while assuming optimal throughput utilization of the hardware. Specifically, we use a node of 8 A100 GPUs, optimize the batch size per model"
4. Open question the authors state: whether larger models overtake at larger sample counts; only up to 2,000 (7B) to 200 (70B) samples were generated.
   - *unaffected*: Authors' own limitation.
   - anchor `section 7` @ arXiv:2404.00725v2 HTML sha256 c13df69c530edc0a82b474595b0c97a25a7f8e79e6b4545918ac045ffa084a44: "An interesting question we do not fully address is whether, given enough compute, the larger models will overtake the smaller ones"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 dpfw3-w3d: Stage-2b combined pass. Read arXiv 2404.00725v2 from disk (sha256 c13df69c530edc0a82b474595b0c97a25a7f8e79e6b4545918ac045ffa084a44). Per-claim corrections recorded in claim_corrections. Wave-2/wave-1 framings checked: see DIVE.md.

### Verdict

Cite as the budget-matched comparison method (equal FLOPs or equal wall-time, then sweep model size vs sample count) and as evidence that a deterministic checker is what makes small-model-many-samples win. Never cite the 5-15 point gain or the 14.19x wall-time ratio as EPYC numbers; never cite it for tasks without a checker, where the paper's own result is negative.

- updated `repl-turn-efficiency.md`

## intake-2297#record — CacheClip: Accelerating RAG with Effective KV Cache Reuse

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10129
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1313#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. At 20% recompute on Qwen2.5-14B CacheClip retains up to 85.2% (RULER/NIAH) and 91.1% (LongBench) of full-attention quality, beating CacheBlend by 16.1 and 4.5 points: reuse remains lossy even in the best-case paper.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "CacheClip retains up to 85.2% and 91.1% of full-attention performance on NIAH and LongBench, outperforming CacheBlend and APE by 16.1 and 12.8 points on NIAH, and by 4.5 and 4.2 points on LongBench (with recomp% = 20%)."
1. A 135M-0.5B auxiliary model last-layer attention overlaps the primary last-layer top-20% positions more than the primary first layer does (Jaccard, 4 pairs, 200 samples); this is the case against CacheBlend early-layer selection.
   - anchor `Sec 3.3 core result` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "in all 16 configurations, the auxiliary model’s last layer achieves higher top- position overlap with the primary model’s last layer than the primary model’s own first layer does"
   - anchor `Sec 4.4` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "the auxiliary LLM runs on the head node’s CPU"
2. Sliding-window grouping of recomputed tokens is the dominant fix on RULER (40.47 to 81.28 average), shared prefix on LongBench (+1.51); scattered token recomputation corrupts multi-token entities.
   - anchor `Sec 4.3` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "If the count meets or exceeds a predefined threshold (default ), we include all tokens in that window"
   - anchor `Sec 5.5` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "Grouping is the dominant factor on RULER, boosting the average from 40.47 to 81.28 (+40.81)."
3. CacheBlend (their reimplementation) degrades as recompute rises from 5% to 80% on single2/single3, a counterintuitive failure reported by an independent group.
   - anchor `Sec 5.2` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "its performance drops sharply as the recomputation ratio increases from 5% to 80%, a counterintuitive result where more recomputation leads to worse quality"
4. Quality gap grows with length: at 16K baselines converge to 58 while CacheClip holds 72.9/75.4, still well below full attention; APE drops 24.5 points 4K to 16K.
   - anchor `Sec 5.3` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "At 16K, the baselines converge to 58 while CacheClip maintains 72.9 and 75.4 with the two auxiliary models."
5. Speedup 3.33x at 16K (5.641 s to 1.695 s) on L20 GPU, 78.6% of the remainder is recompute using a custom Triton sparse-query FlashAttention kernel; the auxiliary model runs on idle Xeon CPU (0.238 s).
   - anchor `Sec 5.6` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "Recomputation (1.332s, 78.6% of total latency) uses a custom Triton [42] Flash Attention kernel supporting sparse query indices"
   - anchor `Sec 5.1` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "We evaluate CacheClip on Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct [37] as the primary model"
6. Ranks Block-Attention, TurboRAG and KVLink as the fine-tuning family, rejected for training cost and re-training per base model.
   - anchor `Sec 2.2` @ arXiv:2510.10129v2 (21 May 2026), HTML sha256 b695ea4d18b9db100e271545fd6c337d9ed4e199eaee0100fa031b4af656dd76: "Block Attention [17], TurboRAG [18], and KVLink [19] finetune LLMs to adapt to local attention mechanisms."

### Verdict

CacheClip confirms, with independent numbers, that selective-recompute reuse is lossy (15% NIAH, 9% LongBench at 20% recompute, widening with length). Do not port it to hybrid live roles; do not claim it beats Prompt Cache on our models (no EPYC measurement). Gate is the shared chunk-library audit (B7).

## intake-2298#record — CatchBench: When Can an Agent Failure Be Caught?

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22808
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty high, relevance medium; categories: benchmark_methodology, harness_optimization, agent_architecture)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1132#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. CatchBench scores auditors at three information states (PRE declared harness, LIVE growing prefix, POST finished trace) with 72 entrants over 1,187 declared configurations and 1,162 recorded runs; its Who&When board uses only the 126 Algorithm-Generated logs and excludes the 58 Hand-Crafted ones (independent confirmation that Who&When is 126 + 58 = 184).
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "CatchBench therefore puts one auditor’s question to three information states: the declared configuration before a run (PRE), a growing prefix of its trace (LIVE), and the finished trace (POST)."
   - anchor `Section 4.1 Source Corpora and Populations` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "Who&When localization uses 126 naturally occurring failures from its Algorithm-Generated split."
   - anchor `Appendix F, Table 24 note` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "The loader requires is_correct=false, which selects all 126 Algorithm-Generated files and excludes all 58 Hand-Crafted files, whose key is is_corrected."
1. No failure class is shown to need no judge at localization: eleven LLM judges reach 0.127 to 0.452 Top-1 on the 126 Who&When runs while the best no-LLM structural entrant reaches 0.211 and position alone 0.159 (random 0.119); the eight best judges cannot be ordered at n=126.
   - *unaffected*: Judge versus structural Top-1 read in Section 5.3 and Appendix D Table 5.
   - anchor `Section 5.3 POST localization` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "GPT-5.5 scores 0.452 Top-1 against 0.211 for the highest structural point estimate, and eight judges fall in the band drawn in Figure 5."
   - anchor `Appendix D, Table 5 (Who&When, Top-1 / Top-3 / MRR)` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "position | 0.159 | 0.516 | 0.407 | random (empirical) | 0.119 | 0.346 | 0.324 |"
2. A no-LLM execution-feature ranker (GRADE's exec-rank) beats the position prior on Top-3 by +0.098 [0.035, 0.167] but not on Top-1 (+0.052 [-0.003, 0.110]): a three-step shortlist without a language model.
   - anchor `Section 5.3 POST localization` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "clears position on Top-3 by +0.098 [0.035,0.167], while its Top-1 difference, +0.052 [−0.003,0.110], contains zero (Table 4). Execution features therefore give a three-step shortlist without a language model."
3. Structural failure DETECTION is largely a run-size signal: on SWE-Gym, where failed runs are shorter, adding dependency features gives +0.147 ROC-AUC over a linear size control but -0.005 [-0.026, +0.015] over a matched spline; on tau-bench no entrant reaches the 0.70 early-warning bar at any prefix.
   - *unaffected*: Linear +0.147 versus spline -0.005 read in Section 5.4; tau-bench bar in 5.2.
   - anchor `Section 5.4 POST detection` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "On SWE-Gym the failed runs are the shorter ones (Appendix D), so a linear size term is a weaker reference than the data support."
   - anchor `Section 5.4 POST detection` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "the linear and matched increments read +0.147 and −0.005 [−0.026,+0.015]."
   - anchor `Section 5.2 LIVE` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "On tau-bench, no nonrandom entrant’s point estimate reaches the bar at any prefix, so no cell records a time to detection (Table 9)."
4. A static rule scanner barely beats flag-everything on over-privilege (F1 0.654 versus 0.601), and a rule reading only declaration order scores a perfect F1 on one of six sources because of how that corpus was built: a rule score can measure corpus construction.
   - anchor `Section 5.1 PRE` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "Its 0.654 F1 is a gain of 0.053 over flag_all at 0.601."
   - anchor `Section 5.1 PRE` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "A rule that reads nothing but position scores 1.000 F1 on injecagent and far lower on crewai and n8n. The leak sits in the corpus rather than the rule (Appendix E)."
5. Injected-fault ("Gold") labels failed the authors' own admissibility bar: a construction-only baseline detects the file-level injection perfectly, so the Gold boards carry no evidential weight.
   - anchor `Section 4.3 CatchBench-Gold` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "File-level Gold fails test 2 outright. Its edited edge breaks a clean-graph predecessor invariant, and a construction-only baseline detects that marker perfectly."
   - anchor `Section 4.3 CatchBench-Gold` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "Both substrates therefore remain mechanism diagnostics and carry no evidential weight anywhere in the results."
6. TRAIL is described as 148 human-annotated traces carrying 841 errors (independent restatement of the TRAIL figures).
   - *unaffected*: Restates TRAIL's 148/841, consistent with the wave-2 TRAIL dive (which also records the 149/835 Table 5 mismatch).
   - anchor `Appendix A.3 Agent Failure Attribution` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "TRAIL supplies 148 human-annotated agent traces carrying 841 errors across GAIA and SWE-Bench Lite"
7. Method point: a benchmark number is not interpretable until the label process is published and tested for the shortcut it may leave; every record carries a label-process tag.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2608.22808v4 (17 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 31223009e7f24bec7feec97187ec92f745f51f416a8806ee6b0ea12c82376c49, text paper.txt extracted from it: "A benchmark number is therefore not interpretable until the process behind its labels is published and tested for the shortcut it may leave."

### Verdict

Use CatchBench as a method warning, not a leaderboard: any mechanical or structural failure detector must be reported against a run-length baseline and checked for a construction-order shortcut, and injected-fault fixtures must pass a "no construction-only baseline detects it" test. Do not cite any entrant score as evidence that a failure class is judge-free; the paper shows none that is.

## intake-2300#record — Consensus is Not Verification: Why Crowd Wisdom Strategies Fail for LLM Truthfulness

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06612
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: benchmark_methodology, agent_architecture, routing_intelligence)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. On four binary, verifier-absent benchmarks (Com2Sense, HLE binary subset, BoolQ, Predict-the-Future), five open models (Gemma-3-4B, GPT-oss-20B and 120B, Qwen-32B, Qwen3-235B), 25 samples per model at T in {0.7, 1.0}: no aggregation rule consistently beats single-sample baselines at up to 25x sampling cost; accuracy is flat or degrades while consensus rises; forecasting is at chance.
   - *narrowed*: True for verifier-absent binary tasks; the abstract's 'no consistent gains' still shows gains on some benchmarks (Figure 1, unread).
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2603.06612v1 HTML sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1: "Even at 25× the inference cost of naive sampling, polling-style aggregation yields no consistent accuracy gains over single-sample baselines and often amplifies shared misconceptions."
   - anchor `section 4.1` @ arXiv:2603.06612v1 HTML sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1: "Across models, temperatures, and datasets, no method consistently outperforms single-sample baselines (Figure 1). Increasing the number of samples increases consensus, but not correctness."
   - anchor `section 3 Models and crowds` @ arXiv:2603.06612v1 HTML sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1: "We evaluate five instruction-tuned, open-source models spanning 4B to 235B parameters across three families"
1. Errors are correlated: on MATH, plurality-wrong rates are 3.9 to 25.8% and the most common wrong answer is 65 to 87% of wrong responses; on AIME plurality-wrong ranges 3.9 to 75.8%. On 53% of MATH questions where several models err, they converge on the same wrong answer. When the plurality is wrong, a checker has nothing correct to find.
   - *unaffected*: Numbers stated in text; MATH and AIME runs use 128 problems x 200 samples.
   - anchor `section 4.2` @ arXiv:2603.06612v1 HTML sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1: "On MATH, plurality-wrong rates are low (3.9–25.8%), but errors are highly concentrated: the most common incorrect answer accounts for 65–87% of wrong responses."
   - anchor `section 4.2` @ arXiv:2603.06612v1 HTML sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1: "Aggregation succeeds in math because verifiers can filter out wrong answers - not because agreement signals truth."
2. Sampling temperature does not buy independence: between T=0.7 and T=1.0 the plurality answer flips in 2.9% of (question, model) pairs.
   - *unaffected*: Stated in text with a table reference.
   - anchor `section 4.2` @ arXiv:2603.06612v1 HTML sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1: "between T=0.7 and T=1.0, the plurality answer flips in only 2.9% of (question, model) pairs (Table 4)"
3. Verbalized confidence tracks expected agreement nearly as strongly as accuracy; weighting by it amplifies the dominant answer. The Surprisingly Popular rule's sign flips across benchmarks (inverse-SP 80% on HLE, chance elsewhere).
   - *unaffected*: Stated in text; the 80% inverse-SP figure is HLE binary, small subset.
   - anchor `section 5.1` @ arXiv:2603.06612v1 HTML sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1: "Verbalized confidence tracks expected agreement nearly as strongly as it tracks accuracy."
4. Scope: binary answers only, five models, one forecasting set described as small; the authors say alternatives (grounding, genuine diversity, learned verifiers) are outside the claim, and propose using predicted consensus to flag high-risk questions for retrieval, tools or deferral.
   - *unaffected*: Authors' own scope statements.
   - anchor `section 5.3` @ arXiv:2603.06612v1 HTML sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1: "We restrict to binary questions to make vote shares and surprise-based aggregation well-defined."
   - anchor `section 5.2` @ arXiv:2603.06612v1 HTML sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1: "predicted consensus can be used to flag high-risk questions and trigger retrieval, tool use, or deferral."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 dpfw3-w3d: Stage-2b combined pass. Read arXiv 2603.06612v1 from disk (sha256 fecdc1fec3746caea9c8ad1b6c68ea149ec81e93af6758ea3f75699cce1ffeb1). Per-claim corrections recorded in claim_corrections. Wave-2/wave-1 framings checked: see DIVE.md.

### Verdict

Cite for three things: agreement is not correctness when errors are correlated; temperature sampling barely diversifies answers (2.9% plurality flips); verbalized confidence is a consensus proxy. Never cite it against checker-based best-of-K (it agrees that a verifier is what makes sampling pay), and never as evidence about open-ended or agentic tasks: it is binary-answer only.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `repl-turn-efficiency.md`
- updated `delegation-context-preassembly.md`

## intake-2301#record — Demystifying Agent Skills: Why They Work-Until They Don't

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14036
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: memory_augmented, benchmark_methodology, agent_architecture, harness_optimization)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1321#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Skills built from the same trajectories beat direct workflow memory by +6.06 pp (95 percent bootstrap CI [+0.76, +11.36], 528 paired triples), but skill vs raw is +2.84 pp with CI [-2.27, +7.95] and workflow memory vs raw is -3.22 pp [-8.14, +2.08]; oracle success is 61.9 / 59.1 / 55.9 percent for skill / raw / workflow memory.
   - anchor `Sec 5.1; paper.txt line 278 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "skill improves over workflow memory by +6.06 percentage points, with a 95% bootstrap confidence interval of [+0.76, +11.36]"
   - anchor `Table 10; paper.txt line 970 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "Skill vs Raw +0.0284 [−0.0227,+0.0795]"
   - anchor `Table 10; paper.txt line 967 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "WM vs Raw −0.0322 [−0.0814,+0.0208]"
1. Mechanism labels (LLM judge, one representative trial per arm): procedural anchoring accounts for 65.7 percent of skill cases and explicit knowledge injection for 4.5 percent; the authors validate taxonomy construction by a human (kappa 0.952) but state that the full paired-triple labelling stays LLM-assisted.
   - anchor `Sec 5.1; paper.txt line 279 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "procedural_anchor accounts for 65.7% of skill mechanisms, whereas explicit knowledge_injection accounts for only 4.5%"
   - anchor `App A.3 (reliability controls); paper.txt line 981 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "the full paired-triple dataset remains LLM-assisted rather than exhaustively human-coded"
2. Skills move failures rather than remove them: execution-layer failures fall (23.5 percent of skill-arm labels vs 37.3 raw) while skill_guidance_misapplied_or_ignored rises to 10.0 percent (0.8 raw); workflow memory instead shows timeout and budget exhaustion at 10.6 percent (1.7 raw, 4.4 skill).
   - anchor `Sec 5.3; paper.txt line 287 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "The mode skill_guidance_misapplied_or_ignored appears in 10.0% of skill-arm cases, compared with only 0.8% in raw execution and 0.4% in workflow memory."
   - anchor `Sec 5.3; paper.txt line 288 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "timeout_budget_exhaustion appears in 10.6% of workflow-memory cases, compared with 1.7% in raw execution and 4.4% with skills"
3. Composition matters: when the source pool is failure-only (0s5f) the skill arm is below the raw baseline in all six benchmark-by-agent cells of Table 1, and withholding success/failure labels costs a lot once failures are in the pool (Gemini Terminal-Bench-2 at 3s2f: 0.7462 with labels vs 0.4000 without).
   - anchor `Table 1, Codex rows; paper.txt line 115 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "Codex + GPT-5.3-Codex Raw 0.5935 0.5083 0.5394"
   - anchor `Table 1, Codex 0s5f row; paper.txt line 156 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "0s5f 0.2839 0.5161 0.5833 0.4500 0.4788 0.4303"
   - anchor `Table 1, Gemini rows; paper.txt line 163 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "Gemini CLI + Gemini-3.1-Pro-Preview Raw 0.5000 0.4762 0.5615"
   - anchor `Table 1, Gemini 0s5f row; paper.txt line 204 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "0s5f 0.5231 0.4769 0.4286 0.4095 0.4769 0.4615"
   - anchor `Sec 5.4; paper.txt line 297 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "for Gemini on Terminal-Bench-2 at 3s2f, it reaches 0.7462 versus 0.4000 without outcome hints"
4. Retrieval: as the candidate pool grows from 5 to 100, parsed actual-use precision falls from 29.6 to 3.3 percent while downstream success moves only from 36.4 to 39.3 percent, so exact ground-truth skill invocation is neither sufficient nor necessary; confusable distractors hurt offline identification more than pool size does.
   - anchor `Sec 5.5; paper.txt line 303 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "downstream success changes only from 36.4% to 39.3% while actual-use precision falls from 29.6% to 3.3%"
5. Whole-run token cost on a matched 83-task intersection: skill 521.5K total tokens vs raw 555.7K and workflow memory 426.2K, with success 69.6 / 64.1 / 64.8 percent; skill is cheaper than raw and costlier than workflow memory.
   - anchor `Table 13 (A.9), skill vs raw row; paper.txt line 1144 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "Skill vs Raw +5.5 pp -29.8K -4.4K -34.2K better and cheaper"
   - anchor `Table 13 (A.9), absolute rows; paper.txt line 1122 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "Skill 69.6% 511.7K 9.8K 521.5K"
6. On 26 Terminal-Bench-2 tasks (Gemini, 5 trials each) compact procedural controls do not reproduce the skill gain: instruction-derived short plan 47.7 percent, workflow test-first template 59.2, workflow memory 62.3, skill 79.2, raw 50.0.
   - anchor `Table 12 (A.8); paper.txt line 1095 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "Skill Workflow 103 / 130 79.2%"
   - anchor `Table 12 (A.8); paper.txt line 1083 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2608.14036v1: "Short plan Task instruction 62 / 130 47.7%"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 DIVE (dpfw3-w3c, arXiv:2608.14036v1 HTML read from disk). Entry newly created. The wave-2 skillsdontdelp dive said this paper "claims skills mainly stabilise execution rather than supply missing facts, a rival mechanism to feedback-bandwidth (H1)". CONFIRMED as to the claim (65.7 vs 4.5 percent) and NARROWED as to rivalry: the two are not mutually exclusive, because H1 is a moderator (where the benefit is large) and this is a label of what the benefit consists of (execution-layer fixes); the paper's own dense-feedback terminal setting shows only +2.8 pp over raw with an interval spanning zero, which fits H1 rather than refuting it. OVERTURNED as a citable claim: "skills outperform raw execution" (CI includes zero); and "skills are cheaper" cannot be quoted next to the 61x/4.5x multipliers without saying that this paper counts total run tokens, not injected context.

## intake-2302#record — Design Patterns for Securing LLM Agents against Prompt Injections

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08837
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance high; categories: safety, agent_architecture)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1313#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Six patterns (action-selector, plan-then-execute, LLM map-reduce, dual LLM, code-then-execute, context-minimisation) give structural isolation of untrusted data from control flow, independent of model compliance.
   - anchor `§3` @ arXiv:2506.08837v3: "once an LLM agent has ingested untrusted input, it must be constrained so that it is impossible for that input to trigger any consequential actions"
   - anchor `§3.1 pattern 4` @ arXiv:2506.08837v3: "a privileged LLM that receives instructions and plans actions, and can use tools"
1. Patterns trade utility for security; none is sufficient alone.
   - anchor `§3` @ arXiv:2506.08837v3: "we believe it is unlikely that general-purpose agents can provide meaningful and reliable safety guarantees"
   - anchor `§3` @ arXiv:2506.08837v3: "These patterns impose intentional constraints on agents, explicitly limiting their ability to perform arbitrary tasks."
   - anchor `§5` @ arXiv:2506.08837v3: "no single pattern is likely to suffice across all threat models or use cases"
2. For coding agents the safest design shows the privileged agent only a strictly formatted, quarantined-LLM-produced API description of untrusted docs/code.
   - anchor `§4.10.3` @ arXiv:2506.08837v3: "Prompt injections would have to survive being formatted into an API description"

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `qwen-chat-template-evaluation.md`

### Verdict

A public message-content agent-trace corpus exists and is usable as a fixture and replay source; it is an outcome-labelled corpus, not a failure-labelled one, and the blog and the v1 URL describe two different datasets.

### Claims (as verified)

0. A 47-task benchmark of iterative propose-execute-evaluate optimisation under a fixed interaction budget, with continuous scores and hard feasibility constraints (abstract says eight models; Table 2 compares nine, a self-inconsistency in the paper).
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "We evaluate eight frontier language models using representative search frameworks"
   - anchor `3.1.2 Results and Analysis` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "We compare nine foundation models under a fixed openevolve budget of 100 iterations"
1. Improvement events follow a dual power law: frequency decays as 1/iteration and magnitude as 1/improvement rank, R2 above 0.83 under a CONSTRAINED slope of -1, from one model (GPT-OSS-120B), one framework (OpenEvolve), 500 iterations, 47 tasks; median 7 improvements per task; marginal returns near zero after about 50 to 100 iterations.
   - anchor `3.3.1` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "Both fits hold with R2>0.83R^{2}>0.83 under a constrained slope of −1-1."
   - anchor `3.3.1` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "We run OpenEvolve with GPT-OSS-120B on all 47 tasks for 500"
2. Depth beats width at fixed budget: along n x d = 256, normalised best-of-n falls 1.00, 0.99, 0.99, 0.97, 0.91 for n = 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, on a 10-task subset with one model and one framework.
   - anchor `3.3.2 Results` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "confirming that depth matters more than width under a fixed budget."
   - anchor `3.3.2` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "on a 10-task subset."
3. Model ranking is unstable under a rerun: the gpt-5.4 retest moved its average rank from 5.68 to 3.54, from the lower tier to first (3.54 vs claude-opus-4.6 3.63), so a one-run ranking in this benchmark is not stable.
   - anchor `3.1.2` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "Relative to the original gpt-5.4 run (average rank 5.68), this rerun shifts gpt-5.4 from the lower tier of the table to the top position."
4. Evaluation integrity is by construction: evaluator code read-only, sandboxed candidates, scores parsed from the evaluator output rather than from any candidate-written file, multi-seed evaluators where applicable.
   - anchor `2.2 Evaluation integrity` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "Isolation. All evaluator code, reference data, and verification scripts are marked read-only and cannot be modified by the agent."
   - anchor `2.2 Evaluation integrity` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "The agent cannot self-report a high score; it must earn one from the frozen verifier."
5. The suite includes three GPU kernel tasks (FlashAttention, MLA, TriMul in CUDA or Triton) scored as 1e9 / geometric-mean latency gated by a correctness check, plus MallocLab and cryptographic C++ throughput tasks.
   - anchor `2.3 Benchmark composition` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "GPU kernel engineering tasks (FlashAttention, MLA, TriMul) ask the agent to write CUDA or Triton kernels whose correctness is verified against a reference implementation and whose performance is measured by wall-clock latency."
6. The first improvement in a trajectory is typically a large structural rewrite, later ones are incremental refinement (qualitative and the case studies).
   - anchor `3.3.1 Results` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "improvement is a large structural rewrite, while each subsequent one is"
7. Both feasible and infeasible candidates consume budget, and the starting point is guaranteed feasible, so the metric is best-feasible score and never below the initial solution.
   - anchor `2.1 formulation` @ arXiv:2604.12290v2 [cs.AI] 27 Apr 2026, "Frontier-Eng: Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on Real-World Engineering Tasks with Generative Optimization" (Chi et al.); https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12290 fetched 2026-10-21, p.html sha256 3961bae2a8a6171d42bda8a92041e99ab69c9073292a44e5c63968330e9f60ce, text p.txt extracted from it: "The evaluator returns (vt,st)=ℰ⁡(xt)(v_{t},s_{t})=\mathcal{E}(x_{t}). Both feasible and infeasible steps consume budget."

### Verdict

A 47-task generative-optimisation benchmark whose useful findings for us are the diminishing-returns shape and the depth-over-width result, both from a single model-framework pair; the GPU kernel tasks are CUDA/Triton and not directly runnable on the MI210.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `harness-improvement-loop.md`

### Verdict

Do not cite GraphTracer for any number or claim: it was withdrawn by its authors for a methodology error. The Information Dependency Graph idea is available only as an untested design sketch; if dependency-edge attribution matters, take it from a source with working evidence (see CatchBench exec-rank and the GRADE paper in DIVE.md). Do not reuse the 18.18% or 4.8-14.2% figures from either GraphTracer or AgenTracer without the AgenTracer entry's narrowing.

## intake-2306#record — Are Long-LLMs A Necessity For Long-Context Tasks?

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.15318
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: agent_architecture, context_management, context_extension)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Method: a model capped at 4K context chooses, per chunk, among Task Understanding, Retrieve, Move, Append, Merge, Answer and Aggregation; the next-action choice conditions on the query, the current chunk and previously extracted evidence (Algorithm 1).
   - *unaffected*: Algorithm 1 and the action list are in the text.
   - anchor `section 2.3` @ arXiv:2405.15318v1 HTML sha256 84826c17a852329cd3c5c171384e30b12a6396815aa345da4ef298a99fe43ce2: "we define a discrete action space"
1. Author-reported: with GPT-3.5-turbo-16K as the foundation model capped at 4K, LC-Boost beats every listed baseline on single-doc QA (47.8), multi-doc QA (56.4), summarization (31.8), few-shot (44.1) and synthetic (27.5) averages, and trails Yi-9B-200K on code completion (59.0 vs 72.1); Table 1 re-run baselines include anomalous values (Claude-3-Haiku few-shot 7.2, code 16.9).
   - *narrowed*: Headline comparison uses GPT-3.5 as the foundation model and re-run baselines with visible anomalies; single run.
   - anchor `section 3.2 Main Results` @ arXiv:2405.15318v1 HTML sha256 84826c17a852329cd3c5c171384e30b12a6396815aa345da4ef298a99fe43ce2: "First, LC-Boost, with a context length of 4K, outperforms all baseline models in all tasks except for the Code Completion task."
2. The foundation model is GPT-3.5, not an open model: the authors state most open-source LLMs lacked the needed instruction-following zero-shot, and that letting the model write its own prompts or code as actions (a continuous action space) was highly unstable even for GPT-3.5.
   - *unaffected*: Quoted from the authors' own limitations; this is the claim that bounds portability.
   - anchor `Appendix D Limitations (3)` @ arXiv:2405.15318v1 HTML sha256 84826c17a852329cd3c5c171384e30b12a6396815aa345da4ef298a99fe43ce2: "We choose GPT-3.5 as the foundation model for LC-Boost, instead of open-source LLMs. The reason is that GPT-3.5 is a strong, yet efficient model that can generally understand most instructions. However, we found that most open-source LLMs lack these properties in a zero-shot setting."
   - anchor `Appendix D Limitations (2)` @ arXiv:2405.15318v1 HTML sha256 84826c17a852329cd3c5c171384e30b12a6396815aa345da4ef298a99fe43ce2: "These experiments resulted in highly unstable performance, particularly for models like GPT-3.5, as such requirements are still challenging."
3. Token cost is task-dependent, not uniformly lower: 34.1% of brute-force tokens on NarrativeQA, 112% on GovReport (summarization), 29.5% on LCC; energy figures are estimated on A100 by FLOP formula and recorded GPU time.
   - *unaffected*: Three datasets only (NarrativeQA, GovReport, LCC).
   - anchor `Appendix C Token Consumption Analysis` @ arXiv:2405.15318v1 HTML sha256 84826c17a852329cd3c5c171384e30b12a6396815aa345da4ef298a99fe43ce2: "LC-Boost’s token consumption was 34.1% of the brute-force method’s consumption in NarrativeQA, 112% in GovReport, and 29.5% in LCC."
4. Ablation claim (figure-only, unread): per-query dynamic trajectories beat fixed strategies (Retrieve only, Merge only, Append only, Merge+Move, Append+Move, Retrieve+Move, brute force, random) on NarrativeQA, HotpotQA and SAMSum. The 'short-context solvable' argument rests on a data-processing-inequality sketch and a GPT-4 pilot whose bars are in an image (Figure 2).
   - *narrowed*: Rests on figures that were not read; the pilot's theory is a sketch.
   - anchor `section 3.3 Ablation Study` @ arXiv:2405.15318v1 HTML sha256 84826c17a852329cd3c5c171384e30b12a6396815aa345da4ef298a99fe43ce2: "Compared to fixed processing strategies, LC-Boost customizes the action trajectory for each query, resulting in notable performance improvements."
   - anchor `section 2.2 Empirical Analysis` @ arXiv:2405.15318v1 HTML sha256 84826c17a852329cd3c5c171384e30b12a6396815aa345da4ef298a99fe43ce2: "Figure 2 presents the experiment results, which generally indicate that LC-Boost consistently performs as well as or better than the brute-force setting."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 dpfw3-w3d: Stage-2b combined pass. Read arXiv 2405.15318v1 from disk (sha256 84826c17a852329cd3c5c171384e30b12a6396815aa345da4ef298a99fe43ce2). Per-claim corrections recorded in claim_corrections. Wave-2/wave-1 framings checked: see DIVE.md.

### Verdict

Cite the action-space idea (retrieve or scan, append or merge, null to skip) and the token-cost caveat (112% on summarization). Never cite 'long-LLMs are not necessary' as established for local models: the foundation model is GPT-3.5, the authors say open models failed zero-shot, and self-authored actions were unstable.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `delegation-context-preassembly.md`
- updated `repl-turn-efficiency.md`

## intake-2307#record — Long-running Claude for scientific computing

- **URL**: https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty low, relevance low; categories: agent_architecture, autonomous_research, context_management)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-1132#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. The harness pattern is stated as applicable to tasks that are "well-scoped, the success criteria are clear, and human oversight can be occasional"; every example given (solver reimplementation, Fortran conversion, debugging against a reference) is a code-writing task with a machine-checkable oracle.
   - anchor `The premise` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "These are tasks where the work is well-scoped, the success criteria are clear, and human oversight can be occasional rather than continuous."
1. Parallelism depends on task coupling: the C compiler farms out to many agents, whereas a deeply coupled numerical pipeline suits one sequential agent spawning subagents and bisecting against a reference.
   - anchor `The premise` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "This kind of task is structurally different from the C compiler project, which can be farmed out to a large number of parallel agents."
   - anchor `The premise` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "may be better suited to a single agent working sequentially, spawning subagents as needed, and using the reference implementation to bisect discrepancies."
2. A progress file (CHANGELOG.md) is the portable long-term memory and must record FAILED approaches with reasons, otherwise later sessions repeat dead ends.
   - anchor `Memory across sessions` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "The failed approaches are important—without them, successive sessions will re-attempt the same dead ends."
3. A test oracle (reference implementation, quantifiable objective or test suite) is the precondition for long-running autonomous work.
   - anchor `The test oracle` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "While more open-ended scientific discovery via agents is certainly on the horizon, long-running autonomous scientific work today crucially depends on the agent having a way to know whether it’s making progress."
4. The "Ralph loop" (a for loop re-prompting the agent when it claims completion) counters agentic laziness; the post calls it a capability-uplift scaffold for current models that may fade as models improve.
   - anchor `The execution loop` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "To circumvent this, a useful orchestration pattern is the Ralph loop, which is essentially a for loop which kicks the agent back into context when it claims completion, and asks if it’s really done."
5. A commit-and-push-after-every-unit rule plus "never commit code that breaks existing passing tests" is the regression guard and the recovery trail.
   - anchor `Git as coordination` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "Never commit code that breaks existing passing tests."
6. Single run, Claude Opus 4.6, a few days: sub-percent agreement with CLASS, but not production-grade, and the agent left a test-coverage gap (single fiducial parameter point) for a time.
   - anchor `The result` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "While the resulting solver is not production-grade (e.g., it doesn’t match the reference CLASS implementation to an acceptable accuracy in every regime)"
   - anchor `The result` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "for a while it was only testing the code at a single (fiducial) parameter point, drastically reducing its bug-catching surface area."
7. The post gives no counts, no token or cost figures, no failure rates and no ablation of any harness piece.
   - anchor `The result` @ Anthropic Research post "Long-running Claude for scientific computing", published 2026-03-23 (Siddharth Mishra-Sharma); fetched 2026-10-21 from https://www.anthropic.com/research/long-running-claude, p.html sha256 4068a1ea7ac48faa81f4b0992c321b94c53835d15d60ccddef01e058d402ba7b, text p.txt extracted from it: "Claude worked on the project from scratch over a few days, reaching sub-percent agreement with the reference CLASS implementation across its various outputs."

### Verdict

The long-running harness lessons recur across two oracle-bound coding tasks but are not shown to hold outside coding; the transferable parts are the progress-file-with-failures, the oracle precondition and the coupling-dependent parallelism choice.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `harness-improvement-loop.md`

## intake-2308#record — LongAgent: Scaling Language Models to 128k Context through Multi-Agent Collaboration

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.11550
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: agent_architecture, context_extension, context_management)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Author-reported: a fine-tuned LLaMA2-7B leader plus identically parameterised LLaMA2-7B members, each reading a 4K chunk, beats GPT-4 Turbo on their Needle-in-a-Haystack PLUS by an average 19.53 points (62.00 to 81.53) single-document and 4.96 points (50.37 to 55.33) multi-document across 1k to 128k; test sets are 100 entity-swapped SQuAD items and 60 HotpotQA items with 10 trials per length/depth cell, no intervals or repeats reported.
   - *narrowed*: Headline gains are over GPT-4 on the authors' constructed Needle PLUS (100 SQuAD and 60 HotpotQA items), with a fine-tuned leader; a measured-in-paper result, not a transferable one.
   - anchor `p.1 section 4.1 Overall Performance` @ arXiv:2402.11550v2 HTML sha256 57441e59bde7b3783ef867ce2d70fc6abb42559119f3037e23c749762f377aea: "LongAgent, constructed from fine-tuned LLaMA2-7B, significantly outperforms GPT-4 across document length ranging from 1k to 128k, with an average improvement of 19.53% (from 62.00% to 81.53%) under the single-document setting, and an average improvement of 4.96% (from 50.37% to 55.33%) under the ..."
   - anchor `Appendix A.1` @ arXiv:2402.11550v2 HTML sha256 57441e59bde7b3783ef867ce2d70fc6abb42559119f3037e23c749762f377aea: "We randomly select 100 samples from the training set of SQuAD and replace the key entities in them."
1. Members fabricate an answer when their chunk contains none, instead of saying 'no mention', even after supervised fine-tuning; this is the paper's stated main error source (section 2.4, 4.2).
   - *unaffected*: Stated twice (2.4 and 4.2) and consistent with the Reject-data ablation.
   - anchor `section 2.4 Resolving Conflicts` @ arXiv:2402.11550v2 HTML sha256 57441e59bde7b3783ef867ce2d70fc6abb42559119f3037e23c749762f377aea: "When there are no answers in the chunk, the model frequently fabricates an answer instead of responding with ‘no mention,’ even after supervised fine-tuning."
2. Conflict resolution: the leader groups identical member answers into clusters, then pairs members from different clusters and has them re-answer on the concatenation of their two chunks; the cluster whose answer survives is kept. Ablation: +18.9 points average accuracy (1k to 128k), figure-only per-length values unread.
   - *unaffected*: Ablation figure is image-only; the 18.9 average is stated in text.
   - anchor `section 4.3 Ablation Study` @ arXiv:2402.11550v2 HTML sha256 57441e59bde7b3783ef867ce2d70fc6abb42559119f3037e23c749762f377aea: "the inter-member communication mechanism leads to an average accuracy improvement of 18.9%"
3. Abstain-data trade: raising 'Reject' (no-answer) training data lifts correct refusals from 51.0% to 78.6% but lowers answer accuracy (83.3% to 78.3% at Answer:Reject 2:1 to 2:3); hallucination also worsens once chunk size exceeds 2,000 tokens.
   - *unaffected*: Numbers stated in text; the Answer:Reject accuracy pair (83.3 to 78.3) is in section 4.2 text as well.
   - anchor `section 4.2 Hallucination Analysis` @ arXiv:2402.11550v2 HTML sha256 57441e59bde7b3783ef867ce2d70fc6abb42559119f3037e23c749762f377aea: "with the increase of ’Reject’ type data in the training data, the proportion of the model correctly refusing to answer increased from 51.0% to 78.6%. However, the increase of ’Reject’ data also slightly impairs the model’s ability to answer questions."
4. Gain is not stable across task shape: the authors attribute the drop from +19.53 to +4.96 in the multi-document setting to the 7B leader's weak problem decomposition.
   - *unaffected*: Authors' own limitation statement.
   - anchor `Limitations` @ arXiv:2402.11550v2 HTML sha256 57441e59bde7b3783ef867ce2d70fc6abb42559119f3037e23c749762f377aea: "LongAgent improved by 19.53% in a single-document setting compared to GPT-4, but this number dropped to 4.96% when switching to a more complex multi-document setting."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 dpfw3-w3d: Stage-2b combined pass. Read arXiv 2402.11550v2 from disk (sha256 57441e59bde7b3783ef867ce2d70fc6abb42559119f3037e23c749762f377aea). Per-claim corrections recorded in claim_corrections. Wave-2/wave-1 framings checked: see DIVE.md.

### Verdict

Cite only the mechanism: answer clustering followed by pairwise chunk-sharing is a cheap, deterministic way to adjudicate conflicting chunk readers, and members hallucinate when a chunk holds no answer. Never cite the +19.53 or +4.96 as an expected gain for EPYC roles: fine-tuned 7B, 100-item entity-swapped SQuAD set, no intervals, GPU-latency claims on A100.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `delegation-context-preassembly.md`
- updated `repl-turn-efficiency.md`

## intake-2309#record — A Controllable Examination for Long-Context Language Models

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02921
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_component (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: context_extension, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1806#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Id resolved: arXiv 2506.02921 is "A Controllable Examination for Long-Context Language Models", whose abstract introduces LongBioBench (the earlier wave-2 HELMET dive recorded the id as unverified). It proposes three desiderata for a long-context benchmark: seamless (coherent) context, controllability, and sound evaluation by exact match without an LLM judge, with data generated on the fly so parametric knowledge cannot help.
   - anchor `Abstract / Sec 1; paper.txt line 101 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "avoiding LLM-as-Judge and conduct exact-match to ensure deterministic and reproducible evaluation results"
   - anchor `Sec 2; paper.txt line 262 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "all biographies are artificial and can be generated on-the-fly, thus avoiding reliance on any parametric knowledge memorized by the model"
1. Headline validity claim: on 12 models overlapping with HELMET at 128K, LongBioBench correlates 0.853 with HELMET and 0.559 with RULER (Figure 1); per-task Spearman against HELMET is 0.92 for standard retrieval but 0.27 (p=0.39) for rank and 0.21 (p=0.50) for two-diff. At n=12 the Fisher 95 percent interval of 0.853 is about [0.55, 0.96] and that of 0.559 about [-0.02, 0.86].
   - anchor `Sec 1; paper.txt line 232 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "exhibit a stronger correlation (0.853) with the scores of HELMET [4], which employs real-world tasks, than with other existing purely synthetic dataset RULER [6] (0.559)"
   - anchor `Fig 1 caption; paper.txt line 222 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "The performance is tested on 128K context length from 12 overlapped models."
   - anchor `Table 7; paper.txt line 987 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "rank 0.27 0.39"
   - anchor `Table 7; paper.txt line 993 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "twodiff 0.21 0.50"
2. Coherence matters: replacing the coherent biography haystack with a Paul Graham essay haystack (BiaH) lowers Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-1M accuracy by 7.9 percent on standard retrieval and by 28.3 and 88.9 percent on harder multi-retrieval settings, which the authors read as models exploiting incoherent cues as shortcuts.
   - anchor `Sec 4.2; paper.txt line 407 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "While the gap is modest in simpler settings such as standard retrieval (-7.9%), it widens substantially as task difficulty increases, reaching -28.3% and -88.9% in more complex scenarios with larger retrieval scopes."
   - anchor `Sec 4.2; paper.txt line 407 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "This trend indicates that LLMs exploit incoherent cues as shortcuts when faced with harder tasks."
3. Validity checks used: tasks are near-perfect at 2K for nearly all models and two models fail the standard task without any context, so scores reflect context use, not memorised knowledge; each test set has 800 items because accuracy fluctuates within 1 percent between 32 and 800 items; greedy decoding, lengths 2K to 128K.
   - anchor `Sec 3 setup; paper.txt line 365 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "The accuracy fluctuates within 1% for 32 data points at around 800 data points, so each test set contains 800 data points."
   - anchor `Sec 3.2; paper.txt line 374 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "almost all models achieve near-perfect performance at 2k-token context length on our proposed tasks"
   - anchor `Sec 3.2; paper.txt line 376 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "Both models fail the task, ensuring that LongBioBench is not contaminated by memorized knowledge."
4. During long-context continual pretraining of Qwen2.5-7B (4K to 32K, checkpoints 2K to 20K steps), retrieval peaks by about 4K steps then flattens or slightly declines, reasoning stays near 10 percent, and trustworthiness (citation, refusing when the answer is absent) declines as training proceeds; one model family, 32K only.
   - anchor `Sec 4.3; paper.txt line 414 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "This suggests that during the initial 4K training steps, the model rapidly adapts to the previously unseen RoPE embeddings."
   - anchor `Sec 4.3; paper.txt line 414 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "Notably, accuracy on the retrieval task peaks at the 4K step checkpoint"
   - anchor `Sec 4.3; paper.txt line 419 (text rendering of the arXiv HTML)` @ arXiv:2506.02921v2: "The right figure in Fig. 7 shows a consistent decline in performance as pretraining progresses."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-21 DIVE (dpfw3-w3c, arXiv:2506.02921v2 HTML read from disk). Entry newly created. The wave-2 HELMET dive recorded this source as "title/id unverified... do not persist the id until resolved". RESOLVED: the id and the LongBioBench name match (abs title above). The HELMET dive described it as arguing "exact match over LLM-judge for reproducibility and contamination": CONFIRMED and WIDENED, since the paper's stronger content is the coherence control and the generator. NARROWED: "0.853 correlation with real tasks" is a 12-model figure whose interval overlaps the RULER comparison and 0.8, and two of ten tasks (rank, two-diff) do not correlate with HELMET at all. OVERTURNED as a citable claim: "long-context continual pretraining only adjusts RoPE" (abstract wording) generalises from one model family at 32K and says nothing about inference-time YaRN scaling.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `yarn-context-extension-research.md`
- updated `repl-turn-efficiency.md`
- updated `harness-improvement-loop.md`

## intake-2310#record — LongRCA Bench: Root-Cause Localization in Long-Horizon Agent Trajectories

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15242
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance low; categories: benchmark_methodology, harness_optimization, agent_architecture)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1132#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. LongRCA Bench holds 1,140 naturally failed (not injected) trajectories from five sources (SWE-bench Pro 128, Terminal-Bench 2 42, TravelPlanner 685, VitaBench 108, WebArena Verified 177), mean length 156.3 and median 145 recorded steps, with human root-step and responsible-role labels.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "LongRCA Bench: 1,140 complete failed trajectories from five sources, all human-annotated for responsible roles and earliest decisive root-cause steps."
   - anchor `Section 3.2` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "We retain evaluator-confirmed failed task attempts from SWE-bench Pro (SWE, 128), Terminal Bench 2 (TB2, 42), TravelPlanner (TP, 685), VitaBench (Vita, 108), and WebArena Verified (WA, 177)"
   - anchor `Section 3.2` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "Failures arise from ordinary execution rather than injected errors."
1. Human labels are noisy: 22 student annotators; on 310 doubly-annotated pairs (294 trajectories) role agreement is 66.1%, exact root-step agreement 40.3%, within-5-steps agreement 50.3%, and 38.1% of pairs differ by more than 20 steps; consensus plus two-expert review reconciles the rest and 60 of 1,200 reviewed trajectories were dropped.
   - *unaffected*: Agreement figures read directly from Section 3.3.
   - anchor `Section 3.3` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "Twenty-two master’s and doctoral students in computer science annotated the trajectories."
   - anchor `Section 3.3` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "The 294 multiply annotated trajectories yield 310 pairs: role agreement is 66.1%, step exact agreement is 40.3%, and ±5 agreement is 50.3%; 38.1% of pairs differ by more than 20 steps."
   - anchor `Section 3.3` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "Of 1,200 reviewed trajectories, 60 were excluded, leaving 1,140 human-annotated failed executions."
2. The root sits a median 48 recorded steps before completion, and 28.4% of trajectories have more than 100 steps after the root.
   - anchor `Section 3.4` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "Trajectory length counts recorded steps in a completed execution (mean 156.3; median 145). Root-to-end distance, T−1−r∗, counts steps after the reference root (median 48); 28.4% of trajectories have more than 100 such steps"
3. Exact root-step accuracy with one backbone (DeepSeek-V4-Flash) is 30.4% to 36.4% for the five baselines on Who&When (all 184 logs) but 2.8% to 13.2% on LongRCA; the authors state the gap does not isolate a length effect because domains and workflows differ.
   - *narrowed*: Authors state the cross-dataset gap does not isolate a length effect; both quotes anchored.
   - anchor `Section 1 Introduction` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "the five evaluated baselines achieve 30.4–36.4% exact root-step accuracy on Who&When (Zhang et al., 2025) but only 2.8–13.2% on LongRCA using DeepSeek-V4-Flash (DS-V4-Flash)."
   - anchor `Section 1 Introduction` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "The datasets also differ in domains and workflows, so this gap does not isolate a trajectory-length effect."
   - anchor `Appendix C` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "The dataset contains 184 trajectories: 126 from algorithm-generated systems and 58 from hand-crafted systems."
4. All six methods score lower in every longer length group than in the shortest group on LongRCA, but the pattern is non-monotonic, is confounded with source mix, and root-to-end distance within a source shows no consistent decline.
   - *narrowed*: Length-group decline is non-monotonic and confounded with source mix.
   - anchor `Section 5.3` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "RCTA has the highest observed Step Acc in every group, yet all six methods score lower in each longer group than in the shortest group."
   - anchor `Section 5.3` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "Source and workflow composition also differ across groups, preventing interpretation as an independent causal effect of length."
   - anchor `Section 5.3` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "localization performance does not consistently decline with root-to-end distance (Appendix F.1)."
5. Role attribution needs a prior: always predicting each source's most frequent reference role gives 40.3% Agent Acc against RCTA's 51.1%.
   - anchor `Section 5.2` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "always predicting each source’s most frequent reference role yields 40.3% Agent Acc, 10.9 percentage points below RCTA (Table 24)."
6. The authors' method RCTA (candidate retrieval plus handoff-instruction comparison) reaches 24.1% exact root-step accuracy on the full set, but none of its four component ablations passes Holm correction on the 200-trajectory Mini.
   - anchor `Section 5.5` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "All four removals lower Step Acc point estimates, but none passes Holm correction across the four comparisons (pHolm≥.17; Table 4)."
7. No matched-cost comparison: the paper does not establish an advantage under matched token or cost budgets.
   - anchor `Section 6 Discussion and Limitations` @ arXiv:2608.15242v4 (21 Sep 2026); paper.html sha256 98b93ac7da4be95970c4ba33843a204027ecca7faabd875ea39790dae525f8ad, text paper.txt extracted from it: "The comparisons do not establish an advantage under matched token or cost budgets (Appendix H)."

### Verdict

Cite only: natural long failures are hard to localize (best baseline 13.2% exact step, own method 24.1%, one backbone) and human exact-step agreement is 40.3%, so first-event or decisive-step labels carry a noise ceiling. Do not cite the length effect as causal, and do not adopt RCTA numbers: no matched-cost comparison, ablations not significant.
